Remark 2.11.4 Let G = O(n, 1) or G = U(n, 1). The kernel Ψ = log cosh d
on Hn(K) is G-invariant.
It therefore defines a function conditionally of
negative type on G, denoted by ψ (see Remark C.4.18). Since ψ is proper,
it is unbounded on every closed and non-compact subgroup L of G. It fol-
lows from Theorem 2.10.4 that such a subgroup has the Haagerup Property
(see Definition 2.7.5), and in particular it does not have Property (FH); for
another proof of this fact, see Theorem 2.7.2.

2.12
The Delorme-Guichardet Theorem

1-cohomology and weak containment

Recall that we defined in Section 2.4 the notion of “almost having invariant
vectors” for orthogonal representations of a topological group.

2.12. DELORME-GUICHARDET THEOREM
137

Remark 2.12.1 (i) Let π be an orthogonal representation of the topological
group G.
Let πC be the complexification of π (see Remark A.7.2).
The
orthogonal representation π almost has invariant vectors if and only if the
unitary representation πC almost has invariant vectors.
Indeed, let ξ ∈H be a (Q, ε)-invariant unit vector for π. Then ξ ⊗1 ∈HC
is a (Q, ε)-invariant unit vector for πC. Conversely, let η ∈HC be a (Q, ε/2)-
invariant unit vector for πC. We have η = ξ1⊗1+ξ2⊗i for vectors ξ1, ξ2 ∈H,
where, say, ∥ξ1∥≥1/2. Then

∥π(g)(ξ1/∥ξ1∥) −(ξ1/∥ξ1∥)∥< ε/ (2∥ξ1∥) ≤ε,
for all
g ∈Q

that is, ξ1/∥ξ1∥be a (Q, ε)-invariant unit vector for π.
(ii) Let α be an affine isometric action of the topological group G on a
real Hilbert space. For t > 0, let (Φt, Ht, πt)t be the associated triple as in
Proposition 2.11.1. The unitary representation πt is the complexification of
an orthogonal representation. Indeed, πt is associated by GNS-construction
to the real-valued function of positive type g �→exp(−t∥α(g)(0)∥2).

Recall that, for an orthogonal representation of a topological group G,
the real vector space Z1(G, π) is endowed with the topology of uniform con-
vergence on compact subsets of G, that is, with the locally convex topology
given by the family of seminorms

pQ(b) = sup
g∈Q
∥b(g)∥,

where Q runs over all compact subsets of G. Assume that G is σ -compact,
that is, G is the union of an increasing sequence (Qn)n of compact subsets.
Then the topology of G is given by the separating sequence of seminorms
(pQn)n and is therefore metrizable. Clearly, Z1(G, π) is complete, so that
Z1(G, π) is a Fr´echet space.
The following result establishes a link between 1-cohomology and Fell’s
topology and is due to A. Guichardet (see [Guic–72a, Th´eor`eme 1])

Proposition 2.12.2 Let G be a locally compact group, and let π be an or-
thogonal representation of G on a real Hilbert space H, without non-zero
invariant vectors.

(i) If π does not almost have invariant vectors, then B1(G, π) is closed in
Z1(G, π).

138
CHAPTER 2. PROPERTY (FH)

(ii) If G is σ-compact, then the converse holds, namely: if π almost has
invariant vectors, then B1(G, π) is not closed in Z1(G, π). In particular,
H1(G, π) ̸= 0.

Proof
Consider the mapping

Φ : H →B1(G, π),
ξ �→π(·)ξ −ξ.

It is clear that Φ is linear, continuous and surjective. Since π has no non-zero
invariant vectors, Φ is also injective.
(i) Assume that π does not almost have invariant vectors. Then there
exist a compact subset Q of G and ε > 0 such that

(∗)
pQ(Φ(ξ)) = sup
g∈Q
∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥≥ε∥ξ∥

for all ξ ∈H. Let (ξi)i be a net of vectors in H such that (Φ(ξi))i converges
to some b ∈Z1(G, π). Then there exists a subsequence (ξn)n such that

lim
n pQ(Φ(ξn) −b) = 0.

By (∗) above, (ξn)n is a Cauchy sequence and, hence, converges to some
vector ξ ∈H. It is clear that Φ(ξ) = b, showing that B1(G, π) is closed.
(ii) Assume that B1(G, π) is closed in Z1(G, π). Since G is σ-compact,
Z1(G, π) and, hence, B1(G, π) is a Fr´echet space. The open mapping theorem
(see [Rudin–73, Corollaries 2.12]) shows that Φ is bicontinuous. Therefore,
there exists a compact subset Q of G and ε > 0 such that

pQ(Φ(ξ)) = sup
g∈Q
∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥≥ε∥ξ∥

for all ξ ∈H. This implies that π does not almost have invariant vectors. ■

Example 2.12.3 (i) Let G be an non-amenable locally compact group, and
let λG be the left regular representation of G on L2
R(G). Then λG does not al-
most have invariant vectors (Theorem G.3.2). Therefore, B1(G, λG) is closed
in Z1(G, λG).
(ii) Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group.
Assume that G is
amenable and non-compact. Then λG almost has invariant vectors, but no
non-zero invariant ones. Hence, B1(G, λG) is not closed in Z1(G, λG).

2.12. DELORME-GUICHARDET THEOREM
139

The Delorme-Guichardet Theorem

The following theorem shows that Property (T) and Property (FH) are
closely related.
Part (i) is due to P. Delorme [Delor–77, Th´eor`eme V.1];
see also [Wang–74, Theorem 1] for the finite dimensional case. Part (ii) was
already shown in Corollary 2.4.7. We give here a different proof based on
Proposition 2.12.2.

Theorem 2.12.4 (Delorme-Guichardet) Let G be a topological group.

(i) If G has Property (T), then G has Property (FH).

(ii) If G is a σ-compact locally compact group and if G has Property (FH),
then G has Property (T).

Proof
(i) Assume that G does not have Property (FH). Then there ex-
ists an affine isometric action α of G without fixed points. For t > 0, let
(Ht, Φt, πt) be the triple associated to α as in Proposition 2.11.1. Set

π =

∞
�

n=1
π1/n.

By (v) of Proposition 2.11.1, the representation πt has no non-zero invariant
vector, for every t > 0. Hence, π has no non-zero invariant vector. On the
other hand, π almost has invariant vectors, by Proposition 2.11.2. It follows
that G does not have Property (T).
(ii) Assume that G is a σ-compact locally compact group and that G
does not have Property (T). Then there exists a unitary representation π
of G an a Hilbert space H which almost has invariant vectors, but has no
non-zero invariant vectors. Let HR be the space H viewed as real Hilbert
space and πR the representation π considered as orthogonal representation
on HR. Then πR almost has invariant vectors (see Remark 2.4.6). Hence,
H1(G, π) ̸= 0, by Proposition 2.12.2.ii. ■

Remark 2.12.5 (i) The assumption that G is σ-compact in Part (ii) of the
Delorme-Guichardet theorem is necessary. Indeed, as mentioned in 2.4.3,
there are examples of uncountable discrete groups which have Property (FH).
Such groups do not have Property (T), since they are not finitely generated.
(ii) The Delorme-Guichardet theorem extends to pairs (G, H) consisting
of a group G and a subgroup H; see Exercise 2.14.9.

140
CHAPTER 2. PROPERTY (FH)

Combining the previous theorem with Theorems 2.3.6 and 2.7.2, we ob-
tain the following consequences.

Theorem 2.12.6 Let G be topological group with Property (T). Every action
of G on a tree has either a fixed vertex or a fixed geometric edge.

Theorem 2.12.7 Let G be a topological group with Property (T). Any con-
tinuous homorphism G →O(n, 1) or G →U(n, 1) has relatively compact
image. In particular, a closed non-compact subgroup of O(n, 1) or U(n, 1)
does not have Property (T).

Remark 2.12.8 As SL2(R) ∼= SU(1, 1), we obtain another proof that SL2(R)
does not have Property (T); compare with Example 1.3.7, Example 1.7.4, and
Proposition 2.6.5.

Another characterisation of Property (T)

The following characterisation of σ-compact locally compact groups with
Property (T) is Theorem 1 in [BekVa–93].
It is based on the Delorme-
Guichardet theorem and will be used in Chapter 6 (see Theorem 6.3.4).

Theorem 2.12.9 Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group. The follow-
ing statements are equivalent:

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) any unitary representation (π, H) of G which weakly contains 1G con-
tains a non-zero finite dimensional subrepresentation.

Given an affine action α of G on a real Hilbert space H, we first identify
the family of unitary representations associated to the diagonal action α ⊕α
of G on H ⊕H.

Lemma 2.12.10 Let α be an affine isometric action of the topological group
G on the real Hilbert space H. Let A = α⊕α be the diagonal affine isometric
action of G on H ⊕H. Let (πt)t>0 and (Πt)t>0 be the families of unitary rep-
resentations of G associated respectively to α and A as in Proposition 2.11.1.
Then Πt is unitarily equivalent to πt ⊗πt for every t > 0.

2.12. DELORME-GUICHARDET THEOREM
141

Proof

mapping

⟨Ψt(ξ, ξ′), Ψt(η, η′)⟩
=
exp(−t∥ξ −η∥2) exp(−t∥ξ′ −η′∥2)
=
exp

and

ξ, ξ′ ∈H.

Moreover, the image of Ψt is total in Ht ⊗Ht. By the uniqueness statement
in Proposition 2.11.1, it follows that Πt and πt ⊗πt are unitarily equivalent.

Proof of Theorem 2.12.9 It is obvious that (i) implies (ii).
Assume that (ii) holds. By the Delorme-Guichardet theorem, it suffices
to show that G has Property (FH). Let α be an affine isometric action of G
on a real Hilbert space H. For each t > 0, let (Φt, Ht, πt)t be the associated
triple. Fix a sequence of positive real numbers with limn tn = 0, and write

π ⊗π =

a fixed point in H. ■

Remark 2.12.11 Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group without
Property (T). The proof above shows that there exists a unitary represen-
tation π of G with the following properties: π is the complexification of an
orthogonal representation, π contains weakly 1G, and π has no non-zero finite
dimensional subrepresentation.

2.14
Exercises

Exercise 2.14.1 Let π be an orthogonal (or unitary) representation of a
topological group G on a real (or complex) Hilbert space H. For b ∈Z1(G, π),
prove that

(i) b(e) = 0;

(ii) b(g−1) = −π(g−1)b(g), for all g ∈G;

(iii) b(hgh−1) = π(h)b(g) for all g ∈Ker π and h ∈G.

Exercise 2.14.2 Let π be an orthogonal (or unitary) representation π of a
topological group G on a real (or complex) Hilbert space H. Let b : G →H
be a 1-cocycle, that is, a mapping satisfying the cocycle relation b(gh) =
b(g) + π(g)b(h) for all g, h ∈G. Show that b is continuous on G if and only
if b is continuous at the unit element e of G.

Exercise 2.14.3 Let G be a locally compact group and let π be an orthogo-
nal (or unitary) representation of G on a separable real (or complex) Hilbert
space H. Let b : G →H be a 1-cocycle. Assume that b is weakly measurable,
that is, the function G →R, g �→⟨b(g), ξ⟩is measurable for every ξ ∈H.
We claim that b is continuous.
To show this, we imitate the proof of Lemma A.6.2. By Exercise 2.14.2,
it suffices to show that b is continuous at the unit element e. Fix ε > 0, and
let A = {g ∈G : ∥b(g)∥< ε/2.}.
(i) Show that A is measurable.
(ii) Verify that A = A−1 and that A2 ⊂{g ∈G : ∥b(g)∥< ε.}.
(iii) Since H is separable, there exists a sequence (gn)n in G such that
(b(gn))n is dense in b(G). Show that G = �

n gnA.
(iv) Show that b is continuous at e and is hence continuous on G.

Exercise 2.14.4 Let F2 be the free group on 2 generators x and y, and let
π be an orthogonal representation of F2 on a real Hilbert space H.
(i) Let f : Z1(F2, π) →H ⊕H be the mapping defined by f(b) =
(b(x), b(y)). Prove that f is a continuous linear bijection.
(ii) Deduce from (i) that H1(F2, π) ̸= 0. [This is a result from [Guic–72a].]

144
CHAPTER 2. PROPERTY (FH)

Exercise 2.14.5 Let G be a locally compact group acting continuously and
transitively on a locally compact space X. Assume that the stabilizer in G
of some (and, hence, of any) point in X is compact. Show that the action of
G (and, hence, of any closed subgroup of G) on X is proper, that is, for any
compact subsets Q and Q′ of X, the set

{g ∈G : gQ ∩Q′ ̸= ∅}

is compact. [This fact is used in the proof of Proposition 2.6.5.]

Exercise 2.14.6 Prove the uniqueness assertion of Proposition 2.10.2.

Exercise 2.14.7 Let G be a topological group and H a closed subgroup of
G. Prove that the following properties are equivalent:

(i) (G, H) has Property (FH);

(ii) for every orthogonal representation π of G, the restriction mapping
ResH
G : H1(G, π) →H1(H, π|H) is the zero mapping.

Exercise 2.14.8 Let G be a topological group and let N be a closed normal
subgroup of G. Assume that the quotient group G/N has Property (FH) and
that the pair (G, N) has Property (FH). Show that G has Property (FH).

Exercise 2.14.9 Let G be a topological group and let H be a closed sub-
group of G.
(i) Assume that the pair (G, H) has Property (T). Show that (G, H) has
Property (FH).
(ii) Assume that G is a σ-compact locally compact group and that (G, H)
has Property (FH). Show that (G, H) has Property (T).
[Hint: To show (i), use a suitable version of Proposition 2.11.1. To show (ii),
use the cocycle which appears in the proof of Proposition 2.4.5.]

Exercise 2.14.10 (This exercise was suggested to us by Y. Shalom.)
Let Γ denote the matrix group SU(2) with the discrete topology. Show that
Γ does not have Property (FH).
[Hint: Since the locally compact group SL2(C) does not have Property (FH),
there exists a continuous isometric action α of SL2(C) on a real Hilbert space
H without fixed point. Consider an action of Γ on H of the form α◦θ, where
θ is the automorphism of SL2(C) induced by a wild automorphism of C.]

2.14. EXERCISES
145

Exercise 2.14.11 Show that the stabilizer of the Lebesgue measure on the
circle S1 in Homeo+(S1) consists exactly of the rotations. [This fact is used
in the proof of Lemma 2.9.11.]

Exercise 2.14.12 (Affine isometric actions on Banach spaces) Let B
be a real Banach space. Let G be a topological group. We say that G has
Property (FB) if every continuous action of G on B by affine isometries has
a fixed point.
(i) Let G be a locally compact σ-compact group with Property (FB).
Show that G has Property (TB) from Exercise 1.8.20.
[Hint: Imitate the proof of 2.12.4.ii.]
(ii) Show that, in general, Property (TB) does not imply Property (FB)
[Hint: Let B = R. Show that every topological group has Property (TB).
Show that Z does not have Property (FB).]
Property (TB) does imply Property (FB) for some classes of Banach spaces.
This is the case, for instance, when B = Lp(X, µ) for a measure space (X, µ)
and 1 ≤p ≤2 (see [BaFGM]).

CHAPTER 2. PROPERTY (FH)

Chapter 3

Reduced Cohomology

Given an orthogonal representation π of a topological group G, the space
Z1(G, π) of 1-cocycles with coefficients in π has a natural topology (see Sec-
tion 2.12). In this chapter, we study the reduced cohomogy group

H1(G, π) = Z1(G, π)/B1(G, π),

where B1(G, π) is the closure of B1(G, π) in Z1(G, π). We first characterise
elements from B1(G, π) in terms of the associated affine isometric action
and in terms of the corresponding function conditionally of negative type.
This provides a new entry to the dictionary set up in the introduction to
Chapter 2:

Affine isometric actions
1-cocycles

negative type
almost having fixed points
cocycles in
Condition (ii) in
B1(G, π)
Proposition 3.1.5

The central result in this chapter is Shalom’s characterisation of Property
(T) for compactly generated, locally compact groups by the vanishing of the
reduced 1-cohomology of all their unitary representations.
As a first application of Shalom’s theorem, we give a new and short proof
of Property (T) for Sp(n, 1), n ≥2, and F4(−20), based on ideas due to
Gromov. Combined with Chapter 1, this completes the classification of the
semisimple real Lie groups with Property (T). As another application, we
show that every group with Property (T) is a quotient of a finitely presented
group with Property (T). The last section is devoted to the proof that Prop-
erty (T) is not invariant under quasi-isometries.

148
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

3.1
Affine isometric actions almost having fixed
points

Let G be a topological group, and let π be an orthogonal representation of G
on a real Hilbert space H. The space Z1(G, π), endowed with the topology of
uniform convergence on compact subsets of G, is a Hausdorff topological real
vector space (see Section 2.12). The closure B1(G, π) of B1(G, π) in Z1(G, π)
is a closed linear subspace of Z1(G, π).

Definition 3.1.1 The reduced 1-cohomology of G with coefficients in π is
the quotient vector space

H1(G, π) = Z1(G, π)/B1(G, π).

We are going to characterise the elements in B1(G, π) by their associated
affine actions, as we did for the elements in B1(G, π) in Section 2.2.

Definition 3.1.2 Let α be an affine isometric action of G on H. We say
that α almost has fixed points if, for every compact subset Q of G and every
ε > 0, there exists x ∈H such that

sup
g∈Q
∥α(g)x −x∥< ε.

Proposition 3.1.3 Let π be an orthogonal representation of the topological
group G on a real Hilbert space H. Let b ∈Z1(G, π), with associated affine
isometric action α. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) b belongs to B1(G, π);

(ii) α almost has fixed points.

Proof
Let Q be a compact subset of G and ε > 0. Observe that, for g ∈G
and ξ ∈H, we have

(∗)
∥b(g) −(π(g)ξ −ξ)∥= ∥α(g)(−ξ) −(−ξ)∥.

Assume that b ∈B1(G, π). Then there exists ξ ∈H such that

∥b(g) −(π(g)ξ −ξ)∥< ε,
for all
g ∈Q,

3.1. ALMOST FIXED POINTS
149

and, hence, α almost has fixed points.
Conversely, if α almost has fixed
points, then there exists ξ ∈H such that

∥α(g)(−ξ) −(−ξ)∥< ε,
for all
g ∈Q,

and (∗) shows that b ∈B1(G, π). ■

of G on the real Hilbert space Hψ such that ψ(g) = ∥αψ(g)(0)∥2 for every

Hψ. We denote by πψ the linear part of αψ and by bψ the 1-cocycle with
coefficients in πψ associated to αψ, that is,

bψ(g) = αψ(g)(0),
g ∈G.

We will call (Hψ, πψ, bψ) the triple associated to ψ.
We need a formula for the inner product on Hψ.

3.2
A theorem by Y. Shalom

This section is devoted to a characterisation, due to Y. Shalom, of Property
(T) for compactly generated locally compact groups in terms of their reduced
1-cohomology [Shal–00a, Theorem 0.2 and Theorem 6.1]; in the particular
case of finitely presented groups, the result has been shown earlier by N. Mok,
with a proof of another nature [Mok–95]. It is appropriate to formulate the
theorem in terms of unitary representations; to apply the results of Chapter
2, we view any such representation as an orthogonal one on the underlying
real Hilbert spaces (Remark 2.4.6).
Note that a compactly generated locally compact group is σ-compact, and
so Property (T) and Property (FH) are equivalent for this class of groups,
by the Delorme-Guichardet Theorem 2.12.4.

Theorem 3.2.1 Let G be a locally compact group which is second countable
and compactly generated. The following conditions are equivalent:

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) H1(G, π) = 0 for every irreducible unitary representation π of G;

(iii) H1(G, π) = 0 for every irreducible unitary representation π of G;

(iv) H1(G, π) = 0 for every unitary representation π of G.

Remark 3.2.2 (i) The fact that (ii) implies (i) answers positively a conjec-
ture of Vershik and Karpushev [VerKa–82, page 514].
(ii) The equivalence of (iii) and (iv) in Theorem 3.2.1 follows from stan-
dard properties of reduced cohomology.
See Lemma 3.2.4, which can be
found on Page 315 of [Guic–72a] and as Theorem 7.2 in [Blanc–79].
(iii) The hypothesis that G is compactly generated in the theorem is ne-
cessary, as the following example shows. Let G = �

n∈N Z/2Z be the direct
sum of countably many copies of Z/2Z. Then G is a countable group. As G
is not finitely generated, G does not have Property (T), by Corollary 2.4.2
or Theorem 1.3.1.
On the other hand, since G is abelian, every unitary
irreducible representation of G is given by a unitary character of G (Corol-
lary A.2.3). We claim that H1(G, χ) = 0 for every unitary character χ of G.
If χ = 1G, then
H1(G, χ) = Hom(G, C) = 0,

3.2. SHALOM’S THEOREM
153

since G is a torsion group (that is, every element of G has finite order).
Assume that χ ̸= 1G, and choose g0 ∈G with χ(g0) ̸= 1. For b ∈Z1(G, χ),
we have, for every g ∈G,

χ(g)b(g0) + b(g) = b(gg0) = b(g0g) = χ(g0)b(g) + b(g0),

and hence

(iv) The hypothesis of second countability of G in Theorem 3.2.1 has been
removed in [LoStV–04].

The following lemma is the crucial tool in the proof of Shalom’s theorem.

Lemma 3.2.5 Let G be a compactly generated locally compact group and let
Q be a compact generating subset of G, with non-empty interior. Let π be an

properties:

(i) δ(ξM) = 1;

(ii) δ(η) > 1/6 for every η ∈H such that ∥η −ξM∥< M.

Proof of Theorem 3.2.1
That (i) implies (ii) follows from Delorme-
Guichardet Theorem 2.12.4. It is obvious that (ii) implies (iii).
Let us check that (iii) implies (iv). If the Hilbert space H of π were sep-
arable, then π would have a decomposition as a direct integral of irreducible
unitary representations (Theorem F.5.3) and the claim would follow directly
from Lemma 3.2.4. In the general case, consider b ∈Z1(G, π). The cocycle
identity shows that the closure K of the linear span of the set {b(g) : g ∈G}
is π(G)-invariant. If π′ denotes the restriction of π to K, it suffices to show

160
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

that b ∈B1(G, π′). Since G is second countable, the space K is separable, so
that Lemma 3.2.4 applies.
We proceed now to show that (iv) implies (i), which is the deep part of
the theorem. Assume that G does not have Property (T). We will show the
existence of a unitary representation σ of G with H1(G, σ) ̸= 0.
Fix a compact generating subset Q of G, with non-empty interior. We
can assume that Q is symmetric, that is, Q = Q−1. Since G does not have
Property (T), there exists a unitary representation (π, H) almost having in-
variant vectors, but without non-zero invariant vector. It follows from the
previous lemma that, for every integer n > 1, there exists a vector ξn ∈H
such that δ(ξn) = 1 and

Recall that, for a topological group G, the unitary dual �G of G is equipped
with Fell’s topology (see Section F.2). Modifying slightly the definition given
in [VerKa–82, Definition 2], we say that π ∈�G is infinitely small if π is not
Hausdorff separated from the unit representation 1G, that is, if U ∩V ̸= ∅,
for all neighbourhoods U of π and V of 1G in �G. The cortex of G, denoted by
Cor(G), is the subset of �G consisting of all infinitely small irreducible unitary
representations of G.
Let G be a locally compact, compactly generated group and let π ∈�G.
It has been shown in [VerKa–82, Theorem 2] that, if H1(G, π) ̸= 0, then
π ∈Cor(G); see also [Louve–01]. We obtain from the theorem above the
following characterisation of locally compact second-countable groups with
Property (T), given by Shalom [Shal–00a].

Corollary 3.2.6 Let G be a second-countable locally compact group. The
following statements are equivalent:

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) G satisfies the following three conditions:

(ii.1) G is compactly generated;

(ii.2) Hom(G, R) = 0;

(ii.3) Cor(G) = {1G}.

Proof
If G has Property (T), then G satisfies the conditions of (ii) by
Theorem 1.3.1, Corollary 1.3.5, and Theorem 1.2.3 (see also Corollaries 2.4.2
and 2.5.2).
Conversely, assume that G satisfies the conditions (ii.1), (ii.2), and (ii.3).
Since G is compactly generated, to establish Property (T), it is enough to
show that H1(G, π) = 0 for every π ∈�G, by Theorem 3.2.1. By the result
of Vershik and Karpushev mentioned above, it follows from condition (ii.3)
that H1(G, π) = 0 if π ̸= 1G. On the other hand,

H1(G, 1G) = Hom(G, C) = 0,

by condition (ii.2). This proves that (ii) implies (i). ■

3.3. PROPERTY (T) FOR Sp(n, 1)
163

3.3
Property (T) for Sp(n, 1)

In this section, we present a proof of Kostant’s result ([Kosta–69], [Kosta–75])
that the real rank 1 simple Lie groups Sp(n, 1), n ≥2, and F4(−20) have Prop-
erty (T). Our proof rests on ideas of M. Gromov [Gromo–03] and Y. Shalom.
It is based on the consideration of harmonic mappings and therefore has some
similarities with geometric super-rigidity (see [MoSiY–93]).
Our proof of Property (T) for Sp(n, 1) and F4(−20) follows by combining
two results:

Theorem A Let G be a connected semisimple Lie group with finite centre,
and let K be a maximal compact subgroup of G. If G does not have Property
(T), there exists an affine isometric action of G on some Hilbert space H
and a mapping F : G/K →H which is G-equivariant, harmonic, and non-
constant.

We will see that this result holds under the more general assumptions
that G is a connected Lie group, Hom(G, R) = 0, and that K is a compact
subgroup such that (G, K) is a Gelfand pair.

Theorem B For G = Sp(n, 1) and K = Sp(n)×Sp(1), n ≥2, or G = F4(−20)
and K = Spin(9), any harmonic mapping G/K →H which is G-equivariant
with respect to some affine isometric action of G on a Hilbert space H has
to be constant.

Gelfand pairs

Definition 3.3.1 Let G be a locally compact group, and let K be a compact
subgroup of G. The pair (G, K) is a Gelfand pair if the convolution algebra
Cc(G//K) of continuous K-bi-invariant functions on G with compact support
is commutative.

Example 3.3.2 Let G be a real semisimple Lie group with finite centre, and
let K be a maximal compact subgroup of G. By a classical result of Gelfand,
the pair (G, K) is a Gelfand pair (see, e.g., [Helga–84, Chapter IV, Theorem
3.1]).

Let (G, K) be a Gelfand pair, and let (π, H) be an irreducible unitary
representation of G. The subspace HK of π(K)-invariant vectors in H is

164
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

invariant under the operators π(f) for f ∈Cc(G//K). (For the definition of
π(f) ∈L(H), see Section F.4.) If HK ̸= 0, then

f �→π(f)|HK

is an irreducible ∗-representation of the commutative ∗-algebra Cc(G//K)
(see [Helga–84, Chapter IV, Lemma 3.6]). By Schur’s lemma (Theorem A.2.2),
it follows that dimC HK = 1.
Summarizing, we see that, for π ∈�G, we have either dimC HK = 0 or
dimC HK = 1.

Definition 3.3.3 Let (G, K) be a Gelfand pair. An irreducible unitary rep-
resentation (π, H) of G is called a spherical representation if dimC HK = 1.

Lemma 3.3.4 Let G be a topological group, and let K be a compact subgroup
of G. Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G which does not contain the
unit representation. Assume that HK is finite dimensional. Then π does not
almost have invariant vectors.
In particular, if (G, K) is a Gelfand pair and π is a spherical representa-
tion of G, then π does not almost have invariant vectors.

Proof
Assume, by contradiction, that π almost has invariant vectors, that
is, there exists a net (ξ)i of unit vectors such that

lim

uniformly on compact subsets of G. For i large enough,we have

uniformly on compact subsets of G. Since HK is finite dimensional, its unit
sphere is compact. Hence, a subnet of (ηi)i converges to some unit vector η ∈
HK. It follows from (∗) that η is invariant under π(G). This is a contradiction,
since π does not contain 1G. ■

The following result is due to P. Delorme [Delor–77, Proposition V.3].
The proof we give was found by Y. Shalom (unpublished).

3.3. PROPERTY (T) FOR Sp(n, 1)
165

Proposition 3.3.5 Let (G, K) be a Gelfand pair, and let (π, H) be a spheri-
cal representation of G distinct from the unit representation. Then H1(G, π) =
0.

Proof
Let α be an affine isometric action of G on H, with linear part π.
We must show that α has a globally fixed point.
Let b ∈Z1(G, π) be defined by

α(g)ξ = π(g)ξ + b(g),
ξ ∈H.

For every compactly supported regular Borel measure µ on G, we can define
an affine transformation α(µ) on H by the H-valued integral

α(µ)ξ =
�

G
α(g)ξdµ(g) = π(µ)ξ +
�

G
b(g)dµ(g),
ξ ∈H.

Let µ0 be a symmetric, K-bi-invariant probability measure on G which is
absolutely continuous with respect to a left Haar measure on G and has
compact support. Assume, in addition, that the subgroup generated by the
support of µ0 is dense in G.

• First claim: the operator α(µ0) has a unique fixed point ξ0 ∈H. Indeed,
by the previous lemma, π does not almost have invariant vectors. Hence,
∥π(µ0)∥< 1 by Proposition G.4.2. Now, for ξ, η ∈H

∥α(µ0)ξ −α(µ0)η∥= ∥π(µ0)ξ −π(µ0)η∥≤∥π(µ0)∥∥ξ −η∥.

It follows that α(µ0) is a strict contraction of the affine Hilbert space H.
Hence, α(µ0) has a unique fixed point ξ0.

• Second claim: the affine subspace of the α(K)-fixed points has positive
dimension. Indeed, observe first that ξ0 is fixed under α(K) since

α(k)ξ0 = α(k)α(µ0)ξ0 = α(µ0)ξ0 = ξ0,
k ∈K

by the left K-invariance of µ0. (Observe that we used the fact that α(k)
is an affine mapping.) Moreover, as π is spherical, there exists a non-zero
π(K)-fixed vector ξ1 in H. Then ξ2 = ξ0 + ξ1 is α(K)-fixed and distinct from
ξ0.

• Third claim: ξ0 is fixed under α(µ) for every µ in the space M(G//K)
of K-bi-invariant, compactly supported probability measures on G. Indeed,

166
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

since (G, K) is a Gelfand pair, Cc(G//K) is commutative. As every measure
in M(G//K) can be approximated in the weak topology by absolutely con-
tinuous measures with a density from Cc(G//K), it follows that M(G//K)
is commutative. Hence, for every µ ∈M(G//K),

α(µ)ξ0 = α(µ)α(µ0)ξ0 = α(µ0)α(µ)ξ0,

so that α(µ)ξ0 is fixed under α(µ0). By uniqueness of the fixed point of α(µ0),
it follows that α(µ)ξ0 = ξ0.

• Fourth claim: ξ0 is fixed under α(G). Indeed, let νK be the normalised
Haar measure dk on K viewed as a measure on G. By the third claim, ξ0 is
fixed by α(νK ∗δg ∗νK), that is,

α(k)α(g)ξ0dk = ξ0

for every g ∈G. Hence, the closed affine subset

Corollary 3.3.6 Let (G, K) be a Gelfand pair. Let (π, H) be an irreducible
unitary representation of G, distinct from the unit representation, such that
H1(G, π) ̸= 0. Let b ∈Z1(G, π) be a 1-cocycle which is not a coboundary. Let
α be the affine isometric action of G on H associated to (π, b). Then there
exists a unique fixed point under α(K) in H.

Proof
As K is compact, α(K) has a fixed point ξ0 in H. Assume that α(K)
has another fixed point ξ1 ̸= ξ0. Then ξ0 −ξ1 is a non-zero π(K)-fixed point.
This implies that π is spherical. Since H1(G, π) ̸= 0, this is a contradiction
to the previous proposition. ■

A mean value property

assumptions:

(i) (G, K) is a Gelfand pair.

(ii) Hom(G, R) = 0;

(iii) G does not have Property (T).

• for every Φ ∈F, the mapping G →F, g �→gΦ is continuous.

We apply the previous proposition in the following situation. Let G be a
connected real Lie group. Let F be the space of the mappings Φ : G/K →H
which are C∞. Equip F with the Fr´echet topology defined by the requirement:
Φn tends to 0 if Φn as well as its derivatives of all orders tend to 0 uniformly
on compact subsets of G/K.

on G. This implies that F is C∞on G/K. ■

The space F satisfies all the assumptions made before Proposition 3.3.8.
Let D be a differential operator on G/K. Then D acts as a continuous linear
operator on F. We obtain therefore the following corollary.

Corollary 3.3.10 Let D be a differential operator on G/K commuting with
left translations by elements of G and annihilating the constants. Then DF =
0.

We will specialize to the case where D is the Laplacian on G/K and show
that F is harmonic in the usual sense.

x0 = K be the origin of G/K.
We recall the construction of a G-invariant Riemannian structure on
G/K. For g ∈G, let λ(g) denote the diffeomorphism of G/K given by left
translation by g :
λ(g)hK = ghK,
h ∈G.

As K is compact, there exists a K-invariant inner product ⟨·, ·⟩on the tangent
space Tx0(G/K) of G/K at x0. For every x = gx0, we define an inner product
⟨·, ·⟩x on Tx(G/K) by

⟨dλ(g)x0(X), dλ(g)x0(Y )⟩x = ⟨X, Y ⟩,
for all
X, Y ∈Tx0(G/K).

This is well-defined, since ⟨·, ·⟩is K-invariant. It is clear that x �→⟨·, ·⟩x is a
G-invariant Riemannian structure on G/K.
We can now consider the Laplacian on G/K with respect to this structure.

Proposition 3.3.15 The mapping F : G/K →H defined above is har-
monic.

Proof
See Corollary 3.3.10. ■

To summarize, in this subsection we have proved the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3.16 (Shalom, unpublished) Let G be a connected Lie group,
with Hom(G, R) = 0. Let K be a compact subgroup of G such that (G, K) is
a Gelfand pair. If G does not have Property (T), there exists an affine iso-
metric action of G on some Hilbert space H, and a G-equivariant mapping
F : G/K →H which is harmonic and non constant.

Next, we specialize further to semisimple Lie groups.

3.3. PROPERTY (T) FOR Sp(n, 1)
173

The case of a non-compact semisimple Lie group

Theorem 3.3.16 applies if G is a semisimple Lie group with finite centre, and
K is a maximal compact subgroup of G. In this way, we obtain Theorem A
stated at the beginning of this section. Actually, in this case, a stronger
result is true, namely: the G-equivariant harmonic mapping F : G/K �→H
can be taken to be locally isometric, that is, for every x ∈G/K,

∥dFx(Y )∥= ∥Y ∥,
for all
Y ∈Tx(G/K).

To see this, we first recall some classical facts on semisimple Lie groups.
Let G be a semisimple Lie group with finite centre, and let K be a max-
imal compact subgroup of G. There exists a Cartan involution θ, that is, an
involutive automorphism of G such that K is the set of θ-fixed elements in
G. Let g be the Lie algebra of G, and let k be the subalgebra corresponding
to K. Let
p = {X ∈g : dθe(X) = −X}.

The decomposition g = k⊕p is the Cartan decomposition of g. Fix a maximal
abelian subspace a of p, and let A = exp a. We have the Cartan decomposi-
tions

and

of G (see [Helga–62, Chapter V, §6, Chapter III, §7 ]). Every element X ∈p
defines a tangent vector DX ∈Tx0(G/K) for x0 = K, given by

f ∈C∞(G/K).

The mapping X →DX is a linear bijection between p and Tx0(G/K). This
allows us to identify Tx0(G/K) with p.
Observe that, for k ∈K, the linear automorphism dλ(k)x0 of Tx0(G/K)

Growth of harmonic mappings on rank 1 spaces

The presentation here follows ideas due to M. Gromov (see [Gromo–03, Sec-

Let X be an irreducible Riemannian symmetric space of the non-compact
type and of rank one. As discussed in Section 2.6, X is isometric to Hn(K)
for a real division algebra K and for an integer n. When K is one of R, C, H,
we assume that n ≥1; the Euclidean line H1(R) is of course not a Rie-
mannian symmetric space of the non-compact type, but we include it in the
table below because we want to consider the standard inclusion mapping
H1(R) →H2(R). When K = Cay, which is non-associative, the integer has
to satisfy n ≤2; here again, we do consider the space H1(Cay) and its stan-
dard inclusion in H2(Cay). There are isomorphisms for low values of n, since
H1(C), H1(H), H1(Cay) are isometric to H2(R), H4(R), H8(R) respectively.

Remark 3.3.19 Let X = G/K be an irreducible Riemannian symmetric
space of rank 1, and let F : X →H be a G-equivariant mapping, with respect
to an affine isometric action α of G on the Hilbert space H. If F(x0) = 0,
then ∥F∥2 is a radial function. Indeed, if d(x, x0) = d(y, x0), then there exists
k ∈K such that y = kx, since K is transitive on the spheres centered at x0.
As F(x0) = 0, we see that α(k) is a linear isometry and

∥F(y)∥2 = ∥α(k)F(x)∥2 = ∥F(x)∥2.

This table shows the main differences between the families Hn(R), Hn(C),
and Hn(K) with K = H or Cay. More precisely, on Hn(R) the growth rate
of harmonic G-equivariant mappings decreases with dimension, on Hn(C) it
is independent of dimension, while on Hn(K), for K = H or Cay, it increases
with dimension.
We are now in position to prove that Sp(n, 1) for n ≥2 and F4(−20) have
Property (T).

Proof of Theorem B Let X = G/K be either Hn(H) for n ≥2 or H2(Cay).
Assume, by contradiction, that there exists an affine isometric action α of G
on a Hilbert H and a non-constant G-equivariant harmonic mapping

F : X �→H

of class C2. Upon replacing F by λF for some λ > 0, we can assume by Propo-
sition 3.3.17 that F is a local isometry. (To preserve the G-equivariance, we

3.4. FINITE PRESENTABILITY
183

have to replace α by the affine action which has the same linear part and is
given by the 1-cocycle λb, where b is the 1-cocycle associated to α.)
Similarly, replacing F by F −F(x0), we can assume that F(x0) = 0. (Here
we have to replace α by the affine action which has the same linear part and
is given by the 1-cocycle g �→b(g) + π(g)F(x0) −F(x0).)
Suppose that X = Hn(H), n ≥2. Restrict F to a quaternionic hyperbolic
line Y = H1(H). Of course, F|Y has no reason to remain harmonic, but it
is still equivariant with respect to the isometry group Sp(1, 1) of Y. Since
we have normalised metrics in such a way that the inclusion Y →X is an
isometric totally geodesic embedding, F|Y must have the same growth rate
as F. Hence, by Proposition 3.3.21, we have for x ∈Y and r = d(x, x0),

∥F|Y ∥2(r) =
4n
2n + 1r + o(r),
as
r →+∞.

On the other hand, by the same proposition,

∥F|Y ∥2(r) ≤4

3r + o(r),
as
r →+∞.

Since 4/3 < 4n/2n + 1, this is the desired contradiction.
The proof for X = H2(Cay) and G = F4(−20) is completely similar. ■

Remark 3.3.22 (i) Theorem B is valid more generally for Γ-equivariant
mappings, where Γ is a lattice in Sp(n, 1) or F4(−20); see [JosLi–96].
(ii) It is proved in [Delor–77] that, for G = SU(n, 1), n ≥1, there are
exactly two inequivalent irreducible unitary representations π of G such that
H1(G, π) ̸= 0; these representations, called the cohomological representations
of G, are contragredient of each other. For G = SO0(n, 1), n ≥3, there
exists, up to unitary equivalence, a unique irreducible unitary representation
π of G such that H1(G, π) ̸= 0.

3.4
The question of finite presentability

Kazhdan asked in his original paper [Kazhd–67, Hypothesis 1] whether a
discrete group with Property (T) is necessarily finitely presented. The answer
is negative, as it follows from each of the following examples. The first one
appears in [Margu–91, Chapter III, (5.11)] and the second one has been
pointed out to us by Y. de Cornulier. We show then a result of Shalom,

184
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

according to which any discrete group with Property (T) is the quotient of
a finitely presented group with Property (T).

Example 3.4.1 Let p be a prime. Denote by Fp((X)) the field of Laurent
series over the field with p elements; recall from Chapter D.4 that it is a local
field. The subring Fp[X−1] of Fp((X)) of series of the form �n
k=0 akX−k is
naturally isomorphic to the ring of polynomials Fp[X]. Denote by G the
locally compact group SL3(Fp((X))) and by Γ the subgroup SL3(Fp[X−1]),
which is a lattice. Then G has Property (T) by Theorem 1.4.15 and Γ has
Property (T) by Theorem 1.7.1. Therefore, the group SL3(Fp[X]), which
is isomorphic to Γ, has Property (T); on the other hand, it was shown in
[Behr–79] that the group SL3(Fp[X]) is not finitely presented.
(Observe
that, by [RehSo–76, Page 164], the group SLn(Fp[X]) is finitely presented
for n ≥4. On the other hand, SL2(Fp[X]) is not finitely generated; see
[Serr–70b, II, §1.6, Exercice 2 ].)

Other examples of non-finitely presented groups with Property (T) are
provided by infinite torsion quotients of uniform lattices in Sp(n, 1), n ≥2;
see Corollary 5.5.E in [Gromo–87].
The following example is due to Yves de Cornulier.

Theorem 3.4.2 Let p be a prime. The countable group Γp = Sp4(Z[1/p]) ⋉
(Z[1/p])4 has Property (T) and is not finitely presented.

ring with unit. Consider a non-degenerate alternate bilinear form on R4, for

the semi-direct product corresponding to the standard action of Sp4(R) on
R4; in particular, we have Γp = G(Z[1/p]).
Let H5(R) denote the 5-dimensional Heisenberg group over R, which is
the set R4 × R equipped with the product

(x, λ)(y, µ) = (x + y, λ + µ + ω(x, y)),
x, y ∈R4, λ, µ ∈R.

Assume that the mapping R →R, x �→2x is injective. Then the centre Z
of H5(R) is {(0, λ) : λ ∈R} ∼= R and H5(R)/Z ∼= R4. We have therefore a
central extension
0 →R →H5(R) →R4 →1.

The symplectic group acts by automorphisms on H5(R) :

g(x, λ) = (gx, λ),
for all
g ∈Sp4(R), x ∈R4, λ ∈R.

We denote the corresponding semi-direct product by

�G(R) = Sp4(R) ⋉H5(R).

Since the action of Sp4(R) on the centre of H5(R) is trivial, we have another
central extension
0 →R →�G(R) →G(R) →1.

Set �Γp = �G(Z[1/p]).

Lemma 3.4.3 The group �Γp has Property (T); in particular, �Γp is finitely
generated.

Proof
The group �G(K) has Property (T), for any local field K (Exer-
cise 1.8.10). Hence, the claim will be proved if we show that �Γp is isomorphic
to a lattice in
G = �G(Qp) × �G(R).

The diagonal embedding Z[1/p] →Qp × R has a discrete and cocompact
image; in particular, it induces an embedding of �Γp into G with discrete
image. Observe that G is isomorphic to the semi-direct product

(Sp4(Qp) × Sp4(R)) ⋉(H5(Qp) × H5(R)) .

CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

Hence, it suffices to show that Sp4(Z[1/p]) and H5(Z[1/p]) are lattices in
Sp4(Qp)×Sp4(R) and in H5(Qp)×H5(R), respectively (see Exercise B.3.5).
The group Sp4, viewed as an algebraic group defined over Q, is perfect
and has therefore no non-trivial rational character. By the Borel-Harish-
Chandra Theorem (Theorem 12.3 in [BorHa–62], see also Theorem 3.2.7 in
[Margu–91]), it follows that Sp4(Z[1/p]) is a lattice in Sp4(Qp) × Sp4(R).
The proof that H5(Z[1/p]) is a lattice in H5(Qp) × H5(R) is more el-
ementary: if X ⊂Qp × R is a compact fondamental domain for Z[1/p],
then X4 × X ⊂H5(Qp) × H5(R) is a compact fundamental domain for
H5(Z[1/p]).■

We will need the following elementary fact, observed by P. Hall [Hall–54,
Page 421].

Lemma 3.4.4 Let G be a finitely generated group, and let N be a normal
subgroup of G. Assume that G/N is finitely presented. Then N is finitely
generated as a normal subgroup of G.

Proof
Since G is finitely generated, there exists a surjective homomor-
phism β : Fn →G, where Fn denotes the free group on a finite set {a1, . . . , an}.
Set R = β−1(N) and denote by p : G →G/N the canonical projection. The
kernel of the surjective homomorphism p ◦β : Fn →G/N is R; in other
words,
⟨a1, . . . , an | r ∈R⟩

is a presentation of G/N. Since G/N is finitely presented, R is generated as a
normal subgroup of Fn by finitely many elements r1, . . . , rm. (Recall that, if
a group has a finite presentation with respect to some finite set of generators,
it has a finite presentation with respect to any other finite set of generators.)
It follows that N is generated as a normal subgroup of G by the finite set
{β(r1), . . . , β(rm)}. ■

Proof of Theorem 3.4.2 By Lemma 3.4.3, the group �Γp is finitely gener-
ated. Consider the central extension

0 →Z[1/p] →�Γp →Γp →1.

The group Γp has Property (T), since it is a quotient of �Γp. Observe that the
kernel Z[1/p] is not finitely generated and, since it is central, it is not finitely
generated as a normal subgroup of �Γp. Hence, by the previous lemma, Γp is
not finitely presented. ■

3.4. FINITE PRESENTABILITY
187

We now turn to a consequence, due to Shalom [Shal–00a, Theorem 6.7],
of the method of proof of Theorem 3.2.1.

Theorem 3.4.5 Let Γ be a discrete group with Property (T). Then there
exists a finitely presented group �Γ with Property (T) and a normal subgroup
N of �Γ such that Γ is isomorphic to �Γ/N.

Proof
Since Γ has Property (T), it is finitely generated. Let

β : Fn →Γ,

be a surjective homomorphism, where Fn is the free group on n generators
a1, . . . , an. Set N = Kerβ, and let w1, w2, . . . be an enumeration of the ele-
ments of N. For each k ∈N, let Nk be the normal subgroup of Fn generated
by w1, . . . , wk, and set
Γk = Fn/Nk.

The group Γk is finitely presented, and β factorizes to a surjective homo-
morphism Γk →Γ. It is enough to show that Γk has Property (T) for some
k ∈N.
Assume, by contradiction, that, for every k ∈N, the group Γk does not
have Property (T). Then there exists an orthogonal representation (πk, Hk)
of Γk which almost has invariant vectors and no non-zero invariant vectors.
We view πk as representation of Fn. Define

δk : Hk →R+,
ξ �→max
1≤i≤n ∥πk(ai)ξ −ξ∥.

By Lemma 3.2.5, there exists ξk ∈Hk with δk(ξk) = 1 and such that δk(η) >
1/6 for every η ∈Hk with ∥η −ξk∥< k.
For every k ∈N, the function

ψk : g �→∥πk(g)ξk −ξk∥2

is conditionally of negative type on Fn. As in the proof of Theorem 3.2.1, the
sequence (ψk)k is uniformly bounded on finite subsets of Fn. Hence, upon
passing to a subsequence, we can assume that (ψk)k converges pointwise to
a function ψ conditionally of negative type on Fn.
Let (Hψ, πψ, bψ) be the triple associated to ψ. As in the proof of Theo-
rem 3.2.1, it can be checked that bψ does not belong to B1(Fn, πψ) and, in
particular, not to B1(Fn, πψ).

188
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

Since ψk = 0 on Nk, the function ψ vanishes on N. Hence, both the
representation πψ and the cocycle bψ factorize through Fn/N. Since Fn/N
is isomorphic to Γ, we obtain in this way an orthogonal representation of
Γ with non-zero first cohomology, contradicting our assumption that Γ has
Property (T). ■

3.5
Other consequences of Shalom’s Theorem

In this section, using Theorem 3.2.1, we give another treatment of the be-
haviour of Property (T) under covering of groups (compare Section 1.7). We
then give the classification of semisimple real Lie groups with Property (T).

Lemma 3.5.1 Let G be topological group, with centre Z(G). Let (π, H) be
an irreducible unitary representation of G. If π is non-trivial on Z(G), then
H1(G, π) = 0.

Proof
Let b ∈Z1(G, π). We have to show that b is a coboundary. By our
assumption on π, there exists z0 ∈Z(G) such that π(z0) ̸= IH. By Schur’s
lemma (Theorem A.2.2), π(z0) = λ0IH for some complex number λ0 ̸= 1. We
have

π(g)b(z0) + b(g) = b(gz0) = b(z0g) = π(z0)b(g) + b(z0) = λ0b(g) + b(z0),

and hence

b(g) = π(g)
�b(z0)

λ0 −1

�
−
�b(z0)

λ0 −1

�
,

for all g ∈G. ■

The proof of the following result was shown to us by Y. Shalom.

Theorem 3.5.2 Let G be a topological group. Assume that G/[G, G] is com-
pact, and let C be a closed subgroup contained in the centre of G.
If H1(G/C, σ) = 0 for every irreducible unitary representation σ of G/C,
then H1(G, π) = 0 for every irreducible unitary representation π of G.

Proof
Let (π, H) be an irreducible unitary representation of G. We con-
sider the restriction π|C of π to C. Two cases occur.
• π|C is non-trivial. Then H1(G, π) = 0 by the previous lemma.
• π|C is trivial, that is, π factorizes through G/C. Let b ∈Z1(G, π). We
claim that b|C ≡0. Indeed, assume, by contradiction that there exists z0 ∈C

3.5. OTHER CONSEQUENCES
189

such that b(z0) ̸= 0. Then, expanding b(gz0) = b(z0g) in two ways as in the
proof of the previous lemma, we obtain

π(g)b(z0) + b(g) = π(z0)b(g) + b(z0)

and, hence,
π(g)b(z0) = b(z0)

for all g ∈G, since π(z0) = IH. Thus, π has a non-zero invariant vector. Since
π is irreducible, it follows that π is the unit representation and b : G →C is
a continuous homomorphism. Hence, b factorizes through G/[G, G]. As the
latter group is compact, it follows that b ≡0. This contradicts the fact that
b(z0) ̸= 0.
Therefore, b|C ≡0. This means that, not only π, but also b factor-
izes through G/C. Hence, b ∈Z1(G/C, π) = B1(G/C, π) and therefore
b ∈B1(G, π). ■

From Theorem 3.2.1, we obtain immediately the following corollary, which
we proved earlier by another method (Theorem 1.7.11).

Corollary 3.5.3 Let G be a second countable locally compact group. Assume
that G is compactly generated, and that G/[G, G] is compact. Let C be a
closed subgroup contained in the centre of G. If G/C has Property (T), then
G has Property (T).

As a first consequence, we classify the semisimple real Lie groups with
Property (T).

Theorem 3.5.4 Let G be a connected semisimple real Lie group, with Lie
algebra g and universal covering group �G. The following conditions are equiv-
alent:

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) �G has Property (T);

(iii) No simple factor of g is isomorphic to so(n, 1) or su(n, 1).

Proof
To show that (i) and (ii) are equivalent, let Z = π1(G) be the
fundamental group of G. Then Z can be viewed as a discrete central subgroup
of �G, and �G/Z is isomorphic to G. Since �G is a connected locally compact

190
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

group, �G is compactly generated. Moreover, the Lie algebra of the analytic
subgroup [ �G, �G] of �G is [g, g]. As g is semisimple, we have [g, g] = g and,
hence,

[ �G, �G] = [ �G, �G] = �G.

Therefore, the previous corollary applies and shows the equivalence of (i) and
(ii).
To show that (ii) and (iii) are equivalent, let Ad G = G/Z(G) be the
adjoint group of G. The centre of Ad G is trivial and

Ad G = G1 × · · · × Gn

for simple Lie groups G1, . . . , Gn with trivial centres. By the previous corol-
lary, G has Property (T) if and only if Ad G has Property (T). On the other
hand, Ad G has Property (T) if and only if every Gi has Property (T) by
Corollary 1.7.8 or by Propositions 2.5.4 and 2.5.1. By Theorems 1.6.1, 2.12.7,
and by Section 3.3, the simple Lie group Gi has Property (T) if and only if
its Lie algebra is not isomorphic to so(n, 1) or su(n, 1). ■

Remark 3.5.5 (i) The previous theorem shows that, for connected semisim-
ple real Lie groups, Property (T) is invariant under local isomorphisms. This
is not true for more general Lie groups. Indeed, the circle group G = S1 has
Property (T) since it is compact, but its universal covering �G = R does not
have Property (T).
(ii) Let G be a connected Lie group with finite fundamental group. Then
G has Property (T) if and only if �G has Property (T).
(iii) Let G be a simple Lie group with trivial centre. It is known (see
[Helga–62, Chapter IX, Section 4]) that π1(G) is infinite cyclic if and only
if G is the connected component of the isometry group of some irreducible
non-compact, hermitian symmetric space, and this is the case if and and only
if the Lie algebra of G is one of the following list:

su(p, q)
(p ≥q ≥1)
so(2, r)
(r ≥3)
so∗(2s)
(s ≥4)
sp∗(2t, R)
(t ≥2)
e6(−14)
e7(−26).

3.5. OTHER CONSEQUENCES
191

With the exception of the adjoint group of su(p, 1), p ≥1, such a group G
has Property (T), as shown above. In this case, �G is a Kazhdan group with
infinite centre.

We give now a result about the non-vanishing of second cohomology.

Corollary 3.5.6 Let G be a simple Lie group with an infinite cyclic funda-
mental group, and with Property (T). If Γ is a lattice in G, then H2(Γ, Z) ̸= 0.

Proof
Let �G be the universal covering of G and p : �G →G the covering
mapping. Set �Γ = p−1(Γ). Then �Γ is a lattice in �G containing Z( �G) = π1(G).
Since π1(G) is infinite cyclic, we have a central extension

0 →Z →�Γ →Γ →1.

We claim that this central extension defines a non-zero element in H2(Γ, Z).
For this, it is enough to prove that the group �Γ is not isomorphic to the
direct product Γ × Z.
By the previous corollary, �G has Property (T). Hence, �Γ has Property (T),
since it is a lattice in �G (Theorem 1.7.1). On the other hand, Γ × Z does not
have Property (T) as it has Z as quotient (Corollary 1.3.5 or Corollary 2.5.2).
■

Remark 3.5.7 (i) The previous corollary is true and easy to prove when
G is the connected component of the isometry group of an irreducible non-
compact, Hermitian symmetric space X and Γ is a cocompact torsion-free
lattice in G. Indeed, the K¨ahler form on Γ\X defines a non-zero element in
H2(Γ, Z) = H2(Γ\X, Z).
(ii) Let G be the connected component of the isometry group of an irre-
ducible non-compact, Hermitian symmetric space and let Γ be a non-uniform
lattice. In view of the list of simple Lie groups given in Remark 3.5.5 (iii),
we have H2(Γ, Z) ̸= 0 if G ̸= PU(n, 1), the adjoint group of SU(n, 1).
Now SU(1, 1) contains the free group on two generators F2 as non-uniform
lattice and H2(F2, Z) = 0. Hence, the previous corollary fails for SU(1, 1). On
the other hand, B. Klingler and P. Pansu communicated to us a proof that the
previous corollary remains true for non-uniform lattices in SU(n, 1), n ≥2.

192
CHAPTER 3. REDUCED COHOMOLOGY

3.6
Property (T) is not geometric

A group-theoretic property (P) is said to be a geometric if, for a pair (Γ1, Γ2)
of finitely generated groups which are quasi-isometric, Γ1 has (P) if and only
if Γ2 has (P); for the notion of quasi-isometric groups, see the definition
below. Examples of such properties are: being virtually free, being virtually
infinite cyclic, being virtually nilpotent, being amenable (for this and other
examples, see [Harpe–00, IV.50]).
A question due to E. Ghys [HarVa–89, page 133] is whether Property (T)
is geometric. This remained open until early 2000, when suddenly news of
counterexamples percolated through the geometric group theory community,
without being attributed to a single person. However, the name of S. Gersten
is frequently associated with the idea. Our aim is now to present a class of
such counterexamples.

Definition 3.6.1 Let (X1, d1) and (X2, d2) be metric spaces. We say that
X1 and X2 are quasi-isometric if there exists mappings

f : X1 →X2
and
g : X1 →X2,

and a constant C > 0 such that, for all x1, x′
1 ∈X1 and x2, x′
2 ∈X2, we have

• 1

C d1(x1, x′
1) −C ≤d2(f(x1), f(x′
1)) ≤Cd1(x1, x′
1) + C

• 1

C d2(x2, x′
2) −C ≤d1(g(x2), g(x′
2)) ≤Cd2(x2, x′
2) + C

• d1(x1, g ◦f(x1)) ≤C

• d2(x2, f ◦g(x2)) ≤C.

Example 3.6.2 (i) Assume that (X1, d1) and (X2, d2) are bi-Lipschitz equiv-
alent metric spaces, that is, there exists a homeomorphism h : X1 →X2 and
a constant C > 0 such that

1
C d1(x1, x′
1) ≤d2(h(x1), h(x′
1)) ≤Cd1(x1, x′
1)

for all x1, x′
1 ∈X1. Then X1 and X2 are quasi-isometric.
(ii) Let Γ be a finitely generated group, and let S1 and S2 be two finite
generating subsets of Γ. Let d1 and d2 be the word metrics on Γ defined by

3.6. PROPERTY (T) IS NOT GEOMETRIC
193

S1 and S2 (see Section G.5). Then the metric spaces (Γ, d1) and (Γ, d2) are
bi-Lipschitz equivalent (Exercise 3.7.3) and hence quasi-isometric.
(iii) Two finitely generated groups Γ and Λ are said to be quasi-isometric
if the metric spaces (Γ, d) and (Λ, δ) are quasi-isometric, where d and δ are
the word metrics defined by finite generating sets S and T of Γ and Λ. By
(ii) above, this definition does not depend on the choices of S and T.
(iv) Let (X, d) be a metric space, and let Γ be a group acting properly
by isometries on X. If the quotient space Γ\X is compact, then Γ is finitely
generated and quasi-isometric to X (for the proof, see [Harpe–00, IV.23]).

Let G be a connected semisimple Lie group with finite centre, and let
K be a maximal compact subgroup of G. Recall that G has an Iwasawa
decomposition G = ANK, where S = AN is a solvable simply connected
closed subgroup of G (see, e.g., [Walla–73, Theorem 7.4.3]). Recall also that
there exists a G-invariant Riemannian metric on G/K, and that, for any such
metric, G/K is a Riemannian symmetric space (see [Helga–62, Chapter IV,
Proposition 3.4]). The proof of the following lemma was explained to us by
C. Pittet (see also Lemma 3.1 in [Pitte–02]).

Lemma 3.6.3 Let G be a connected semisimple Lie group with finite cen-
tre, and let K be a maximal compact subgroup of G. Let G and G/K be
endowed with G-invariant Riemannian metrics, and let K be endowed with
a Riemannian metric.
Then G and G/K × K are bi-Lipschitz equivalent, where G/K × K is
endowed with the product Riemannian metric.

Now, let G be a connected simple Lie group with infinite cyclic funda-
mental group (Remark 3.5.5), and let �G be the universal covering group of G.
Let Γ be a cocompact lattice in G (such lattices exist, see [Borel–63]). It is
easy to see that Γ is finitely generated (Exercise 3.7.4). Let �Γ be the inverse
image of Γ in �G, which is also a cocompact lattice in �G. Since �Γ contains
π1(G) ∼= Z, we have a central extension

0 →Z →�Γ →Γ →1.

Lemma 3.6.4 Let Γ and �Γ be the finitely generated groups defined above.
Then �Γ and Γ × Z are quasi-isometric.

Proof
Let K be a maximal compact subgroup of G. As in the previous
lemma, we endow G, K and G/K with appropriate Riemannian metrics. We
lift the metrics on G and K to left-invariant Riemannian metrics on �G and
on �K, the inverse image of K in �G.
On the one hand, �Γ acts freely by isometries (via left translations) on �G,
with compact quotient. Hence, �Γ is quasi-isometric to the metric space �G
(see Example 3.6.2.iv).

3.7. EXERCISES
195

On the other hand, Γ acts properly by isometries on G/K, with compact
quotient. Moreover, since π1(K) = π1(G) ∼= Z, the group Z acts freely by
isometries on �K, with compact quotient. Hence Γ × Z is quasi-isometric to
G/K × �K.
By the previous lemma, G and G/K × K are bi-Lipschitz equivalent.
Hence �G and G/K × �K are bi-Lipschitz equivalent and therefore quasi-
isometric. It follows that �Γ and Γ × Z are quasi-isometric. ■

Let G be a simple real Lie group with infinite cyclic fundamental group.
Assume that G has Property (T); for examples of such groups, see Re-
mark 3.5.5. Let Γ be a cocompact lattice in G. By Theorem 3.5.4, �G and �Γ
have Property (T). On the other hand, Γ × Z does not have Property (T).
By the previous lemma, �Γ and Γ × Z are quasi-isometric.
We summarize the discussion as follows:

Theorem 3.6.5 Let G be a connected simple real Lie group with infinite
cyclic fundamental group.
Assume that G has Property (T). Let Γ be a
cocompact lattice in G, and let �Γ be the inverse image of Γ in the universal
covering group �G. Then �Γ has Property (T) and is quasi-isometric to the
group Γ × Z which does not have Property (T).

Chapter 4

Bounded generation

In this chapter, we explain a result of Y. Shalom [Shal–99a] which provides
explicit Kazhdan constants for several groups, including SLn(Z), n ≥3. This
gives a direct proof of Property (T) for SLn(Z) which does not use the fact
that SLn(Z) is a lattice in SLn(R).
The main idea is to relate Property (T) to the following group-theoretic
property. A group G has bounded generation (or is boundedly generated) if
there exists a finite subset S of G and a positive integer ν such that every
element g in G can be written as a product

g = gk1
1 gk2
2 · · · gkν
ν ,

where the gi’s are in S and the ki’s are integers. Carter and Keller [CarKe–83]
established this property for the groups SLn(O) with respect to the set of
elementary matrices as generating set, where O is the ring of integers of a
number field and n ≥3. (See also [AdiMe–92] for the case O = Z.)
We discuss first SLn(Z) and then SLn(R) for a class of topological rings
R. When R is the ring of continuous functions on the circle, this shows that
the loop group of SLn(C) has Property (T) for n ≥3; this is our first example
of a non locally compact group with Property (T).
In the first section, we give a complete proof of the bounded generation
of SLn(Z) for n ≥3. In a first reading, this fact can be taken for granted
and one can directly proceed to the next section for the proof of Property
(T) of these groups.

197

198
CHAPTER 4. BOUNDED GENERATION

4.1
Bounded generation of SLn(Z) for n ≥3

Let R be a commutative ring with unit, and let SLn(R) be the group of
(n × n)–matrices with coefficients in R and with determinant 1. For integers
i, j with 1 ≤i, j ≤n, i ̸= j and t ∈R, recall from Section 1.4 that the
elementary matrix Eij(t) ∈SLn(R) is the matrix with 1 on the diagonal, t
at the entry (i, j), and 0 elsewhere.

Definition 4.1.1 The group SLn(R) has bounded elementary generation (or
is boundedly elementary generated) if there exists a positive integer ν such
that every matrix in SLn(R) can be written as a product of at most ν ele-
mentary matrices. The minimal integer with this property will be denoted
by νn(R).

Remark 4.1.2 (i) If K is a field, SLn(K) is boundedly elementary generated
for n ≥1. Indeed, Gauss’ elimination shows that νn(K) ≤n(n −1); see
Exercise 4.4.2.
(ii) Let H be a subgroup of finite index of the group G. Then G is bound-
edly generated if and only if H is boundedly generated (Exercise 4.4.3). It
is clear that a non-abelian free group cannot be boundedly generated. Since
SL2(Z) has a subgroup of finite index which is free on 2 generators (Exam-
ple B.2.5), it follows that SL2(Z) is not boundedly generated.

Theorem 4.1.3 For n ≥3, the group SLn(Z) is boundedly elementary gen-
erated with

νn(Z) ≤1

2(3n2 −n) + 36.

In particular, every matrix on SL3(Z) can be written as a product of at most
48 elementary matrices
The proof will be given in several steps. An elementary operation on a
matrix A is the mutiplication of A by an elementary matrix Eij(t), on the
left or on the right. Multiplying A by Eij(t) from the left amounts to adding
t times the j-th row to the i-th row of A, and multiplying A by Eij(t) from
the right amounts to adding t times the i-th column to the j-th column of
A.
Observe that, if k elementary operations transform A into a matrix B,
then k elementary operations transform At into Bt and A−1 into B−1.

4.2
A Kazhdan constant for SLn(Z)

In this section, we give a proof, due to Shalom, of Property (T) for SLn(Z),
n ≥3. This proof does not use the embedding of SLn(Z) as a lattice in
SLn(R) as in Example 1.7.4; it gives, moreover, an explicit Kazhdan con-
stant. We start by establishing a quantitative version of Property (T) for
the pair (SL2(Z) ⋉Z2, Z2), due to M. Burger [Burge–91, §1, Examples]. We
follow the simpler proof given in [Shal–99a, Theorem 2.1].

Lemma 4.2.1 Let ν be a mean on the Borel sets of R2 \ {0}. There exists a
Borel subset M of R2\{0} and an element γ ∈{U ±, L±} such that |ν(γM)−
ν(M)| ≥1/4 for the linear action of SL2(Z) on R2.

Proof
Consider the eight domains in the plane R2 limited by the four
lines of equations x = 0, y = 0, x = y and x = −y. Pairing these domains,
we define a partition of R2 \ {0} into four regions A, B, C, D, with

A =

ν(A) = ν(A ∪B) −ν(B) = ν(A ∪B) −ν(L+(A ∪B)) < 1/4

and similarly ν(B), ν(C), ν(D) < 1/4. This is impossible, since

1 = ν(R2 \ {0}) = ν(A) + ν(B) + ν(C) + ν(D).■

Remark 4.2.6 (i) The Kazhdan constant for SLn(Z) from the previous the-
orem has been improved by Kassabov [Kassa] to the value 1/(42√n + 860).
(ii) As was observed by A. Zuk, the optimal Kazhdan constant for SLn(Z),
with respect to the set Qn introduced above, is bounded from above by
�

2/n
(Exercise 4.4.4). This shows that the order 1/√n in Kassabov’s constant from
(i) is optimal.
(iii) Bounded generation of SL3(Z) has already been used by Colin de
Verdi`ere [ColVe–98, Th´eor`eme 3.9] to estimate a variant of Kazhdan con-
stants related to the family of all finite dimensional unitary representations
(instead of all unitary representations as in Theorem 4.2.5).
Previously,
Kazhdan constants for finite dimensional unitary representations have been
estimated by M. Burger [Burge–91, Proposition 3].

4.3
Property (T) for SLn(R)

In this section, we will show that SLn(R), n ≥3, has Property (T) for
suitable topological rings R. We first deal with finitely generated discrete
rings. The strategy of the proof is the same as in the case R = Z, only the
technical details are more involved.

Property (T) for (SL2(R[t]) ⋉R[t]2, R[t]2)

Let R be a commutative ring with unit. Let R[t] be the ring of polyno-
mials over R. We view R and R[t] as discrete topological rings. The nat-
ural embedding of R into R[t] induces an embedding of SL2(R) ⋉R2 into
SL2(R[t]) ⋉R[t]2.
The following result is Theorem 3.1 in [Shal–99a]. It will provide us with
an inductive procedure to treat finitely generated rings in the next section.
For a a subset Q of SL2(R) containing the four elementary matrices
E12(±1) and E21(±1), we define

Property (T) for SLn(R)

Let R be a topological commutative ring with unit. Then SLn(R) is a topo-
logical group for the topology induced from the product topology on Rn2.
The following result is [Shal–99a, Main Theorem].

Theorem 4.3.5 Fix an integer n ≥3, and let R be a topological commuta-
tive ring with unit. Assume that SLn(R) has bounded elementary generation.
Assume, moreover, that there exist finitely many elements in R which gen-
erate a dense subring.
Then SLn(R) has Property (T).

Remark 4.3.6 The proof of Theorem 4.3.5 will show that (Q, ε) is a Kazh-
dan pair for SLn(R), where Q and ε are defined as follows. Let r1, . . . , rm be
elements in R generating a dense subring. Let Q1 be the set of the elemen-
tary matrices Eij(1), 1 ≤i ̸= j ≤n, and let Q2 be the set of the elementary
matrices Eij(rk) where j = i + 1 or j = i −1 for 1 ≤k ≤m and 1 ≤i ≤n.
Set Q = Q1 ∪Q2 and ε = 1/νn(R)22m+1.

Before we give the proof, we establish some preliminary results.
For

indeterminates X1, . . . , Xm.

As-

Proof of Theorem 4.3.5 Let r1, . . . , rm be elements in R generating a dense
subring. Let Q1 be the set of the elementary matrices Eij(1), 1 ≤i ̸= j ≤n,
and let Q2 be the set of the elementary matrices Eij(rk) where j = i + 1
or j = i −1 for 1 ≤k ≤m and 1 ≤i ≤n. Set Q = Q1 ∪Q2 and
ε = 1/νn(R)22m+1.
We claim that (Q, ε) is a Kazhdan pair for SLn(R). The proof is similar
to the proof of Theorem 4.2.5. Indeed, let (π, H) be a unitary representation
of SLn(R) which has a (Q, ε)-invariant unit vector ξ. Let g be any elementary
matrix in SLn(R). By Lemma 4.2.4, there exists an embedding α of SL2(R)⋉
R2 into SLn(R) such that g ∈α(R2) and such that α(F) = (Q∪Q−1)∩Im(α),
where F is the subset of SL2(R)⋉R2 defined in the previous corollary. Hence,
by this corollary, ξ is (α(R2), 1/νn(R))-invariant. In particular,

∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥<
1
νn(R)

for any elementary matrix g in SLn(R). As in the proof of Theorem 4.2.5,
it follows from the bounded generation of SLn(R) that ∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥≤1 for
every g ∈SLn(R), and this implies that π has non-zero invariant vectors. ■

Remark 4.3.9 (i) The previous proof shows the following more general re-
sult. Let E(n, R) denote the subgroup of SLn(R) generated by all elementary
matrices. Assume that R contains a dense finitely generated subring and that
E(n, R) is boundedly generated by the elementary matrices. Then E(n, R)
has Property (T).
(ii) Let R be as in Theorem 4.3.5.
Assume that there exists a fixed
m ∈N such that, for every neighbourhood U of 0 in R, we can find m
elements r1, . . . , rm ∈U generating a dense subring. Then the set Q1 of the
n2 −n elementary matrices Eij(1), with 1 ≤i ̸= j ≤n, is already a Kazhdan
set, with the same Kazhdan constant (see Exercise 4.4.5).

4.3. PROPERTY (T) FOR SLn(R)
223

(iii) In a major breakthrough, Shalom has shown that SLn(Z[X1, . . . , Xm])
has Property (T) when n ≥m + 3 [Shal–ICM]. The question, due to W. van
der Kallen [Kalle–82], whether this group is boundedly generated is still open
even for the case m = 1.

Property (T) for the loop group of SLn(C)

We apply now Theorem 4.3.5 to the loop group of SLn(C).
Let X be a topological space and G a topological group. The set GX

of continuous mappings from X to G is a group for the pointwise product,
defined by (f1f2)(x) = f1(x)f2(x) for f1, f2 ∈GX and x ∈X. Endowed with
the topology of uniform convergence, GX is a topological group.
In case
X = S1 is the unit circle, it is called the loop group of G and denoted by
LG. The following simple proposition gives two necessary conditions for LG
to have Property (T).

Proposition 4.3.10 Let G be a topological group and assume that LG has
Property (T). Then

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) if G is a connected Lie group, the fundamental group π1(G) of G is
finite.

Proof
(i) The mapping

LG →G, f �→f(e)

is a continuous surjective group homomorphism, and the claim follows from
Theorem 1.3.4.
(ii) Let LeG be the normal subgroup of LG consisting of all f ∈LG
with f(1) = e. Then LG is naturally isomorphic to the semi-direct product
G⋉LeG. It is well-known that π1(G) = π1(G, e) is the set of path-connected
components of LeG. Hence, π1(G) is isomorphic to the quotient of LeG by its
(open) path-connected component (LeG)0 of the identity. Since G is path-
connected, LG/(LG)0 is isomorphic to LeG/(LeG)0 and hence to π1(G). It
follows that π1(G) has Property (T) as a discrete group. On the other hand,
π1(G) is abelian. Hence, π1(G) is finite (Theorem 1.1.6). ■

224
CHAPTER 4. BOUNDED GENERATION

Remark 4.3.11 (i) Examples of Kazhdan Lie groups with infinite funda-
mental group are SO(n, 2) for n ≥3 and Spn(R) for n ≥2 (see Exam-
ple 1.7.13). Thus, we see that the loop groups of such groups do not have
Property (T).
(ii) Let G be a compact connected simple Lie group. Then its loop group
LG does not have Property (T); see Exercises 4.4.6 and 4.4.7.

Let G = SLn(C). Then LG can be identified with SLn(R), where R =
C(S1) is the ring of all continuous complex-valued functions on S1.
We first observe that R = C(S1) has a finitely generated dense subring.
Indeed, fix an irrational real number θ and let S be the subring generated
by the functions

r1 = sin 2πx, r2 = cos 2πx, r3 = e2πiθ.

By the Stone-Weiestraß theorem, the subring generated by C ∪{r1, r2} is
dense in C(S1). Moreover, r3 generates a dense subring of C. Hence, S is
dense in C(S1).

Theorem 4.3.12 The loop group L(SLn(C)) has Property (T) for n ≥3.

This will be a consequence of Theorem 4.3.5, once we have shown that
SLn(R) is boundedly elementary generated for R = C(S1). The proof is
based on the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 4.3.13 Let f, h ∈C(S1) be two continuous functions on S1 with no
common zero. Then there exists a continuous fonction ϕ ∈C(S1) such that
h + ϕf has no zero.

Proof
The following proof is due to M. Neuhauser. Consider the closed
subset A = {z ∈S1 : |f(z)| = |h(z)|} of S1. Since f and h have no common
zero,

ϕ1 : A →S1,
z �→h(z)

we can take ϕ = 0.
Assume that A ̸= S1. Then there exists a continuous function ψ1 : A →R
such that ϕ1(z) = exp(iψ1(z)) for all z ∈A. By Urysohn’s lemma, ψ1 extends
to a continuous function ψ : S1 →R. Let ϕ : S1 →S1 be defined by

ϕ(z) = exp(iψ(z)). Then h(z) + ϕ(z)f(z) ̸= 0 for every z ∈S1. Indeed,
this is clear if z ∈A, since h(z) + ϕ(z)f(z) = 2h(z) ̸= 0. If z /∈A, then
|f(z)| ̸= |h(z)| and hence h(z) + ϕ(z)f(z) ̸= 0. ■

Remark 4.3.15 (i) Theorem 4.3.5 and the previous proposition show that
ε = 2/(3n2 · 222) > 1/(106n2) is a Kazhdan constant for the set Q defined
in Remark 4.3.6. Observe that every neighbourhood of 0 in C(S1) contains
m = 3 functions generating a dense subring: fix an irrational real number θ
and take

r1 = 1

N sin(2πx), r2 = 1

N cos(2πx), r3 = e2πiθ

N
for suitable N ∈N. It follows from Remark 4.3.9.ii that the set Q1 of the
n2 −n constant functions z �→Eij(1), 1 ≤i ̸= j ≤n, is a Kazhdan set, with
the same Kazhdan constant.
(ii) The loop group of SLn(R) is not elementary boundedly generated.
In fact, the elementary matrices do not even generate LSLn(R); with the
notation of Remark 4.3.9.i, this means that E(n, R) ̸= LSLn(R), where R
is the ring of all real-valued continuous functions on S1. Indeed, every map-
ping f ∈E(n, R) is homotopic to a constant path (Exercise 4.4.8). On the
other hand, π1(SLn(R)) is non-trivial. However, as observed in [Cornu–b],
LSLn(R) has Property (T) for n ≥3. In view of Remark 4.3.9.i, this fol-
lows from the fact that E(n, R) is boundedly generated by the elementary
matrices, a result due to Vaserstein [Vaser–88], and that π1(SLn(R)) is finite.
(iii) Using similar methods, Neuhauser [Neuh–03b] showed that the loop
group of Sp2n(C) has Property (T) for n ≥2.

Chapter 5

A spectral criterion for
Property (T)

The aim of this chapter is to give a spectral condition implying Property (T)
and allowing the computation of Kazhdan constants for groups acting on a
simplicial complex of dimension 2. This condition is due to A. Zuk [Zuk–96]
and W. Ballmann and J. Swiatkowski [BalSw–97] (see also [Pansu–98] and
[Wang–98]), after fundamental work by H. Garland [Garla–73] (see the Bour-
baki report [Borel–73]). In [Zuk–03], an abstract form of this criterion is
given involving only the spectrum of the Laplace operator of a finite graph
associated to a generating set of a group.
The proof we give for the spectral criterion is based on the article of
M. Gromov [Gromo–03] and on the exposition of this work given by E. Ghys
[Ghys–04]. The main result Theorem 5.5.4 holds for arbitrary locally compact
groups acting properly on a simplicial complex of dimension 2, while only
discrete groups are treated in the papers quoted above. This generalisation
was pointed out by G. Skandalis and allows us to give a new proof of Property
(T) for SL3(K) when K is a non-archimean local field.
Sections 5.1 and 5.2 describe our setting for a random walk µ on a set X,
including the corresponding Laplace operators ∆µ acting on spaces Ω0
H(X)
of Hilbert-space-valued functions on X. In Section 5.3, the set X is finite.
In Section 5.4, the set X can be infinite, and there is a group G acting on X
with finitely many orbits. The interaction finite/infinite, or local/global, is
analyzed in Section 5.5, and applications follow in the two last sections.
More precisely, Proposition 5.3.1 establishes in the case of a finite set

229

230
CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

X the basic properties of the smallest positive eigenvalue λ1 of ∆µ acting
on Ω0
C(X). Proposition 5.4.5 relates Property (T) for G to minorations of
the operators ∆µ, and this is reformulated in Proposition 5.4.8 as Poincar´e
type majorations of the operators ∆µ∗k. The situation for comparing “local”
random walks on finite sets Xx and a “global” random walk on X is described
in Section 5.5, together with the main example of a simplicial 2-complex
X and the links Xx of its vertices; the comparison itself is the object of
Theorems 5.5.2 (general situation) and 5.5.4 (a 2-complex and its links).
The first application is Zuk’s criterion (Theorem 5.6.1). In Section 5.7, there
are applications to Euclidean buildings of type �A2.

5.1
Stationary measures for random walks

A random walk or Markov kernel on a non-empty set X is a kernel with
non-negative values
µ : X × X −→R+

such that �

y∈X µ(x, y) = 1 for all x ∈X. Such a random walk is irreducible
if, given any pair (x, y) of distinct points in X, there exist an integer n ≥1
and a sequence x = x0, x1, . . . , xn = y of points in X such that µ(xj−1, xj) > 0
for any j ∈{1, . . . , n}.
A stationary measure for a random walk µ is a function

ν : X −→R∗
+

such that
ν(x)µ(x, y) = ν(y)µ(y, x)
for all
x, y ∈X.

A random walk is reversible if it has at least one stationary measure.
Let µ be a random walk on a set X. There are two obvious necessary
conditions for µ to have a stationary measure: the first is

(∗)
µ(y, x) ̸= 0
if and only if
µ(x, y) ̸= 0
(x, y ∈X)

and the second is

(∗∗)
µ(x1, x2) · · · µ(xn−1, xn)µ(xn, x1) = µ(xn, xn−1) · · · µ(x2, x1)µ(x1, xn)

for any integer n ≥3 and any sequence x1, . . . , xn of points in X. We leave
as Exercise 5.8.1 to check that Conditions (∗) and (∗∗) are also sufficient

5.2. LAPLACE AND MARKOV OPERATORS
231

for the existence of a stationary measure, and for its uniqueness in case µ is
irreducible.

Example 5.1.1 Let G = (X, E) be a locally finite graph. It is convenient to
adopt here a definition slightly different from that used in Section 2.3. Here,
the edge set E is a subset of X × X which contains e = (y, x) whenever it
contains e = (x, y); the source of e is x (also written e−) and the range of e
is y (also written e+). Thus G has no multiple edge (namely has at most one
edge with given source and range in X) and G can have loops, namely edges
of the form e = (x, x), for which e = e. The degree of a vertex x ∈X is the
integer
deg(x) = # {y ∈X : (x, y) ∈E} .

For x, y ∈X, set

µ(x, y) =

�
1/deg(x)
if (x, y) ∈E
0
otherwise.

Then µ is the so-called simple random walk on X and ν : x �−→deg(x)
is a stationary measure for µ. [It is important to allow loops in G, since
µ(x, x) ̸= 0 should not be excluded.]

Conversely, to any random walk µ on X for which Condition (∗) holds,
we can associate a graph Gµ = (X, Eµ) with edge-set

Eµ = {(x, y) ∈X × X :
µ(x, y) > 0} .

The graph Gµ is connected if and only if the random walk µ is irreducible;
this graph is locally finite if and only if the random walk µ has finite range,
namely if and only if the set {y ∈X : µ(x, y) ̸= 0} is finite for all x ∈X.
Here is a particular case of the criterion for the existence of a stationary
measure: if µ is a random walk for which Condition (∗) holds and for which
the graph Gµ is a tree, then µ has a stationary measure.

Let µ, µ′ be two random walks on the same set X. The convolution µ∗µ′

is defined by

(µ ∗µ′)(x, z) =
�

and is clearly again a random walk on X. For an integer k ≥1, we write
µ∗k for the k-th convolution power of µ; moreover µ∗0(x, y) = 1 if x = y and
µ∗0(x, y) = 0 if x ̸= y. The following proposition is straightforward.

Proposition 5.2.3 If ν is a stationary measure for two random walks µ, µ′

on the same set X, then ν is also a stationary measure for 1

2(µ ∗µ′ + µ′ ∗µ).

The Markov operator for the convolution µ ∗µ′ is the product of the Markov
operators for the factors:

Mµ∗µ′ = MµMµ′.

In particular, ν is a stationary measure for µ∗k and

Mµ∗k = (Mµ)k

for any k ≥0.

identity operator on H. A number λ ∈R which is an eigenvalue of some
multiplicity N in the scalar case is therefore an eigenvalue of multiplicity
dim(H)N in the H-valued case.

5.4
G-equivariant random walks on quasi-tran-
sitive free sets

Let X be a set and let G be a unimodular locally compact group acting on
X. We assume that

(Pi) the action is continuous, namely the subgroup Gx = {g ∈G : gx = x}
of G stabilizing x is open for all x ∈X;

(Pii) the action is proper, namely the subset {g ∈G : gF ∩F ′ ̸= ∅} of G
is compact for all pairs (F, F ′) of finite subsets of X; in particular, the
stabilizers Gx are compact subgroups of G;

(Piii) the action is cofinite, namely the set G \ X of orbits is finite.

238
CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

Let moreover µ be a reversible random walk on X and let ν be a stationary
measure for µ. We assume that µ and ν are G-invariant

(Piv) µ(gx, gy) = µ(x, y) and ν(gx) = ν(x) for all g ∈G and x, y ∈X.

Example 5.4.1 (i) Let Γ be a finitely generated group. Let S be a finite
generating set of Γ which is symmetric, namely such that s−1 ∈S if and only
if s ∈S. We define a random walk µ on Γ (or on the Cayley graph of Γ with
respect to S) by

µ(γ, γ′) =






1
#S
if γ−1γ′ ∈S

0
otherwise

(this is a particular case of Example 5.1.1). Observe that µ is irreducible,
since S generates Γ. As µ is symmetric and irreducible, the stationary mea-
sures for µ are the constant functions on Γ. Properties (Pi) to (Piv) are
straightforward to check.
(ii) Let A, B be two finite groups and let Γ be their free product. Let
G = (X, E) be the canonical tree on which Γ acts (see [Serre–77]). Recall
that X is the disjoint union of the coset spaces Γ/A and Γ/B, and that each
γ ∈Γ corresponds to a pair (e, e) of edges, e having source γA and range
γB. The simple random walk on G has properties (Pi) to (Piv).

We return to the general case. We choose a Haar measure dg on G; it is
both left and right-invariant. For x ∈X, we write |Gx| for the Haar measure
of the compact open subgroup Gx; observe that |Ggx| = |Gx| for each g ∈G
since Ggx = gGxg−1.
A fundamental domain for the action of G on X is a subset T of X such
that X is equal to the disjoint union of the orbits (Gx)x∈T; such a T is finite
by (Piii) and its cardinality is that of G \ X.
If ϕ : X →C is a G-invariant function, the value �

�

π(X) are the Laplace

Example 5.4.4 Let Γ be a group generated by a finite symmetric S and

We will now assume that µ has finite range, that is,

(Pv) #{y ∈X : µ(x, y) > 0} < ∞for all x ∈X.

(Recall that (Pi) to (Piv) have been defined at the beginning of the present
Section 5.4.) This has the the following consequence:

(Pvi) The group G is compactly generated. More precisely, if T is a funda-
mental domain and if x0 ∈T is some base point, then G is generated
by the union of the compact subgroup Gx0 and the compact subset

S = {s ∈G : µ(x, sy) > 0
for some
x, y ∈T} .

Indeed, consider g ∈G.
Since Gµ is connected, there exists a sequence
x1, . . . , xn = gx0 in X such that µ(xi−1, xi) > 0 for all i ∈{1, . . . , n}. Let
y1, . . . , yn be a sequence in T and g1, . . . , gn a sequence in G such that xi =
giyi (1 ≤i ≤n). If si = g−1
i−1gi (with g0 = 1), we have

and therefore si ∈S for all i ∈{1, . . . , n}. Thus

so that yn = x0 and g−1 �

Proposition 5.4.5 Let X be a set and G a unimodular locally compact group

(i) G has Property (T);

Remark 5.4.6 The only implication which is used below is (iii) =⇒(i).
Observe that the implication (iii) =⇒(ii) is obvious.

Proof

on X as well as, for each y ∈T, a set Sy of representatives of G/Gy. Recall
that S is the subset of G of those s ∈G for which µ(x, sy) > 0 for some
x, y ∈T.
Several of the equalities written below are of the following kind: given
x ∈X and a measurable function α : G −→R∗
+ such that α(gh) = α(g) for
