5.5
A local spectral criterion

Let X be a set and G a unimodular locally compact group acting on X. Let
µ be a reversible random walk on X and let ν be a stationary measure for
µ. Assume that Properties (Pi) to (Pvi) from the previous section hold, and
moreover that µ is irreducible.

250
CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

We will give a condition in terms of a family of locally defined random
walks on X implying the Poincar´e inequality (iii) from Proposition 5.4.8 and
hence Property (T) for G.
Assume that, for every x ∈X, there exists a finite subset Xx of X, a
reversible random walk µx on Xx, a probability measure νx on Xx which
is stationary for µx, and a G-invariant positive measure τ on X (a “weight
function”) with the following properties:

(i) µgx(gy, gz) = µx(y, z) and νgx(gy) = νx(y),

(ii) �

x∈X τ(x)νx(y)µx(y, z) = ν(y)µ(y, z),

(iii) �

x∈X τ(x)νx(y)νx(z) = ν(y)µ∗2(y, z),

for all x ∈X, y, z ∈Xx, and g ∈G, where we view all µx’s as measures on
X × X and all νx’s as measures on X.
Here is a first example; a second one appears below in connection with
Zuk’s criterion (Theorem 5.6.1).

Example 5.5.1 Let X be a simplicial complex such that any vertex belongs
to some edge and any edge belongs to some triangle. Let X denote the set of
vertices of X. For vertices y, z ∈X, denote by τ(y, z) the number of triangles
containing y and z when y ̸= z, and the number 0 when y = z; denote by τ(y)
the number of oriented triangles containing y, so that τ(y) = �

z∈X τ(y, z).
Define a random walk µ on X by

µ(y, z) = τ(y, z)

τ(y)
for all
y, z ∈X.

Then µ is reversible, with stationary measure ν given by

ν(y) = τ(y)
for all
y ∈X.

Moreover µ is irreducible if and only if X is connected.
Choose now a vertex x ∈X. Denote by Xx the set of vertices y of X
distinct from x such that x and y are vertices of a triangle in X. Let Ex be
the set of pairs (y, z) ∈Xx ×Xx such that x, y, z are the vertices of a triangle
in X. Thus Gx = (Xx, Ex) is a graph which is called the link of x in X. The
simple random walk µx on Gx is given by

µx(y, z) =





1

if (x, y, z) is a triangle in X

Remark 5.5.3 (i) Let T be a fundamental domain for the action of G on
X and let S be as in Property (Pvi) of the last section. The proof above and
Remark 5.4.9 show that
�

2(2 −λ−1) is a Kazhdan constant for S, where
λ = minx∈T λ1(x).
(ii) The condition that λ1(x) > 1/2 for all x in the fundamental domain
T is equivalent to the condition that λ1(x) > 1/2 for all x ∈X. Indeed,
every g ∈G induces an isomorphism between the graphs Gx and Ggx, for any
x ∈X.

We apply now the local criterion 5.5.2 to a group acting on a simplicial
complex as in Example 5.5.1.

Theorem 5.5.4 (Local criterion for groups acting on a simplicial
complex) Let X be a simplicial complex such that every vertex belongs to
some edge and every edge belongs to some triangle. Assume that, for each
x ∈X, the link Gx of x is connected and that the smallest non-zero eigenvalue
of the Laplacian corresponding to the simple random walk on Gx satisfies
λ1(x) > 1/2.
Let G be a unimodular locally compact group with a continuous, proper
and cofinite action on X. Then G has Property (T).

Remark 5.5.5 (i) The condition λ1(x) > 1/2 cannot be improved. Indeed,
let X be the simplicial complex induced by the tiling of the Euclidean plane
by equilateral triangles. The link Gx at each point x ∈X is a 6-cycle and has
λ1(x) = 1/2 (Exercise 5.8.3). On the other hand, the group Z2 acts freely
and transitively on X, and does not have Property (T).
(ii) The assumption that G is unimodular is necessary; see Remark 5.7.9
below.
(iii) Theorem 5.5.4 generalises [BalSw–97, Corollary 1], where G is as-
sumed to be discrete.

5.6. ZUK’S CRITERION
255

5.6
Zuk’s criterion

Let Γ be a group generated by a finite set S. We assume that 1 /∈S and that
S is symmetric, that is, S−1 = S. The graph G(S) associated to S has vertex
set S and edge set the pairs (s, t) ∈S ×S such that s−1t ∈S, as in [Zuk–03].
Observe that (s, t, s−1t) ∈S3 if and only if (s−1, s−1t, t) ∈S3, so that (s, t) is
an edge if and only if (s−1, s−1t) is an edge; in particular, deg(s) = deg(s−1).
Let µS be the simple random walk on G(S) as in Example 5.1.1, that is,
for s, t ∈S,

µS(s, t) =

�
1/deg(s)
if t−1s ∈S
0
otherwise.

Let
d =
�

s∈S
deg(s).

We assume from now on that d ≥1. The probability measure νS, defined by

νS(s) = deg(s)

d
,
s ∈S,

is stationary for µS.
Define now a left invariant random walk µ on X = Γ by

µ(γ, γ′) =

�
deg(γ−1γ′)/d
if γ−1γ′ ∈S
0
otherwise,

where deg(s) refers to the degree of s ∈S in the graph G(S). Observe that
µ is symmetric, so that ν = 1 is a stationary measure for µ. The graph
Gµ associated to µ is the Cayley graph G(Γ, S) of G with respect to S. In
particular, Gµ is connected.
For every γ ∈Γ, define a random walk µγ on Xγ = γS by

µγ(γs, γt) = µS(s, t)

and a stationary measure νγ by

νγ(γs) = νS(s),

for all s, t ∈S.

CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

We verify that Conditions (i), (ii) and (iii) from the beginning of Sec-

observe first that

The following result which is [Zuk–03, Theorem 1] is now a consequence

Theorem 5.6.1 (Zuk’s criterion) Let Γ be a group generated by a finite
symmetric set S with e /∈S. Let G(S) be the graph associated to S. As-

(ii) The condition λ1(G(S)) > 1/2 cannot be improved.
Indeed, let
Γ = Z with the set of generators S = {±1, ±2}. Then λ1(G(S)) = 1/2
(Exercise 5.8.4) and Z does not have Property (T).
(iii) Assuming that G(S) is connected is not a strong restriction. Indeed,
if S is a finite generating set of Γ, then the graph G(S′) associated to the
generating set S′ = S ∪(S2 \ {e}) is connected (Exercise 5.8.5).

CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

(iv) Theorem 5.6.1 can be deduced from Theorem 5.5.4 by considering
the Cayley complex associated to a presentation of Γ (Exercise 5.8.8).
(v) The spectral criterion implies much more than Property (T): it en-
sures that every isometric action of Γ on a complete CAT(0)–space, with the
property that every tangent cone is isometric to a closed convex cone in a
Hilbert space, has a globally fixed point; see Theorem 1.2 in [IzeNa–05].
Note that a Riemannian symmetric space of the non-compact type is
a CAT(0)–space with that property. If Γ is a lattice in a semisimple Lie
group G (e.g. Γ = SL3(Z)), then Γ acts on properly isometrically on the
Riemannian symmetric space associated to G. This means that one cannot
prove Property (T) for such a group Γ by means of the spectral criterion.

5.7
Groups acting on �A2-buildings

We will apply Theorem 5.5.4 from the previous section to groups acting on
�A2-buildings. In particular, we give a new proof of the fact that SL3(K)
has Property (T) for a non-archimedean local field K (compare with Theo-
rem 1.4.15). Concerning basic facts on buildings, we refer to [Brown–89] and
[Ronan–89].

Definition 5.7.1 A projective plane consists of a set P of “points”, a set
L of “lines”, and an incidence relation between points and lines, with the
following properties:

(i) given two distinct points, there is a unique line incident with them,

(ii) given two distinct lines, there is a unique point incident with them,

(iii) each point is incident with at least three lines, and each line is incident
with at least three points.

The incidence graph G(P,L) of a projective plane (P, L) is defined as fol-
lows:

• the set of vertices of G(P,L) is the disjoint union P �L,

• the set of edges of G(P,L) is the set of pairs (p, ℓ) ∈P × L and (ℓ, p) ∈
L × P such that p and ℓare incident.

5.7. GROUPS ACTING ON �A2-BUILDINGS
259

Observe that the graph G(P,L) is connected, by Properties (i),(ii), (iii) above,
and that it is bipartite.

Remark 5.7.2 Let (P, L) be a finite projective plane. It is known that there
exists an integer q ≥2, called the order of (P, L), such that each point is
incident with exactly q + 1 lines and each line is incident with exacly q + 1
points. Therefore, the degree of each vertex of G(P,L) is q + 1. Moreover,

#P = #L = q2 + q + 1.

For all this, see [HugPi–73].

Example 5.7.3 The most familiar example of a projective plane is the pro-
jective plane P2(F) over a field F. In this case, P the set of 1-dimensional
subspaces of the vector space F3 and L is the set of 2-dimensional subspaces
of F3. The incidence relation is defined by inclusion. In case F is a finite
field, the order of P2(F) is q = #F.
Here is for example the incidence graph of the projective plane P2(F2) :

Definition 5.7.4 An �A2-building is a 2-dimensional contractible connected
simplicial complex X such that the link of any vertex of X has the structure
of the incidence graph of a finite projective plane.

Example 5.7.5 Let K be a non-archimedean local field, with absolute value
x �→|x| (see Section D.4). We briefly review the construction of the �A2-
building XK associated to K. The closed unit ball

O = {x ∈K : |x| ≤1}

is an open and compact subring of K with unique maximal ideal

℘= {x ∈K : |x| < 1}.

The quotient ring F = O/℘is a field, called the residual field of K. Since O
is compact and ℘is open in O, the residual field F is finite. Let π ∈℘\ ℘2.
Then ℘= πO.
A lattice in V = K3 is an O-submodule L of V of rank three, that is,

L = Ov1 + Ov2 + Ov3,

260
CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

where {v1, v2, v3} is a basis of V. Two lattices L and L′ are equivalent if
L′ = xL for some x ∈K \ {0}.
The set of vertices of the building XK is the set of equivalence classes
[L] of lattices L in V. Three distinct vertices x1, x2, x3 in XK determine a
triangle if there exists lattices L1 ∈x1, L2 ∈x2, L3 ∈x3 such that

πL1 ⫋L3 ⫋L2 ⫋L1.

This implies that the link Xx of x ∈XK consists of the vertices y for which
there exist lattices L1 ∈x and L2 ∈y such that πL1 ⫋L2 ⫋L1. Since
L1/πL1 ∼= (O/πO)3, consideration of the quotient mapping L1 →L1/πL1
shows that the vertices in Xx are in bijection with the 1-dimensional sub-
spaces and the 2-dimensional subspaces of F3 and that the graph structure
of Xx correponds to the incidence graph of the projective plane associated to
the residual field F = O/πO of K. In particular, Xx has 2(q2 +q +1) vertices
and every vertex in Xx is adjacent to q + 1 vertices in Xx, where q = #F.

Next, we determine the spectrum of the Laplace operator of the incidence
graph of a finite projective plane. The result is due to Feit and Higman
[FeiHi–64].

5.8. EXERCISES

Remark 5.7.9 The subgroup B of all triangular matrices in SL3(K) has the
same orbits on XK as SL3(K) and the stabilizers in B of vertices are open
and compact. However, B does not have Property (T), since it is solvable
and non-compact (see Theorem 1.1.6). This shows that the unimodularity
assumption of G in Theorem 5.7.7 is necessary.

Example 5.7.10 A (discrete) group is called an �A2-group if it acts freely
and transitively on the vertices of an �A2-building, and if it induces a cyclic
permutation of the type of the vertices. These groups were introduced and
studied in [CaMSZ–93]. Some �A2-groups, but not all, can be embedded as
cocompact lattices in PGL3(K) for a non-archimedean local field K.
It was shown in [CaMlS–93] through a direct computation that an �A2-
group Γ has Property (T) in the case where the underlying projective plane
is associated to a finite field. Theorem 5.7.7 is a generalisation of this result.
Moreover, the Kazhdan constant from Theorem 5.7.7 for the given gener-
ating set S of Γ coincides with the one found in [CaMlS–93]; as shown in
[CaMlS–93], this is the optimal Kazhdan constant for S in this case.

Example 5.7.11 The following examples of groups described by their pre-
sentations and satisfying the spectral criterion in Theorem 5.5.4 are given in
[BalSw–97]. Let G be a finite group, and let S be a set of generators of G
with e /∈S. Assume that the Cayley graph G = G(G, S) of G has girth at
least 6. (Recall that the girth of a finite graph G is the length of a shortest
closed circuit in G.) Let ⟨S |R⟩be a presentation of G. Then the group Γ
given by the presentation

⟨S ∪{τ} | R ∪{τ 2} ∪{(sτ)3 : s ∈S}⟩

acts transitively on the vertices of a CAT(0) two-dimensional simplicial com-
plex, with finite stabilizers of the vertices, such that the link at every vertex
of X is isomorphic to G. Therefore, if λ1(G) > 1/2, then Γ has Property (T).
Examples of finite groups G satisfying the conditions above are the groups
PSL2(F) over a finite field F with a certain set of generators (see [Lubot–94]
or [Sarna–90]).

CHAPTER 5. A SPECTRAL CRITERION FOR PROPERTY (T)

Chapter 6

Some applications of Property
(T)

In this chapter, we present some applications of Property (T). The first
application is the by now classical contruction of expander graphs, due to
G. Margulis [Margu–73]. We then turn to the role of Property (T) in er-
godic theory.
It is first shown that Property (T) for a locally compact
group G implies the existence of a spectral gap and provides an estimate
for convolution operators associated to ergodic actions of G. We expose the
Schmidt-Connes-Weiss dynamical characterisation of Property (T) in terms
of ergodic measure-preserving actions. Next, we study Property (T) in the
context of orbit equivalence; in particular, we prove the Furman-Popa result
showing that Property (T) is invariant under measure equivalence. In the
last section, we show how Property (T) is used for a solution of the Banach-
Ruziewicz problem for n ≥5; this problem asks whether the Lebesgue mea-
sure is the unique rotation-invariant finitely additive probability measure on
the Lebesgue measurable subsets of the unit sphere in Rn.

6.1
Expander graphs

We introduce families of expander graphs defined by the existence of a uni-
form bound for their expanding constants. We describe two examples of such
families.

267

268
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

Expander graphs

Let G = (V, E) be a locally finite graph, where V is the set of vertices and E
the set of edges of G. As in Example 5.1.1, we assume that the edge set E is
a subset of X × X which contains e = (y, x) whenever it contains e = (x, y);
in particular, G has no multiple edge but is allowed to have loops.
For a subset A of V, the boundary ∂A of A is the set of vertices in V \ A
which are adjacent to a vertex in A, that is,

∂A = {y ∈V \ A : y ∼x for some x ∈A}.

The expanding constant or isoperimetric constant of G is the positive real
number

h(G) = min
�
#∂A
min{#A, #(V \ A)} : A ⫋V, 0 < #A < ∞
�
.

Remark 6.1.1 If we view the graph G as modelling a communication net-
work, then the expanding constant h(G) is a way to measure the spreading
of information through the network.

Example 6.1.2 The proof of the following claims is left as Exercise 6.5.1.
(i) Let G = Cn be the cycle with n vertices. Then

h(Tk) = k −2.

Definition 6.1.3 Let k ∈N be a fixed integer and let ε > 0 be a constant. A
family Gn = (Vn, En) of finite connected graphs is a family of (k, ε)-expanders
if

(i) limn→∞#Vn = ∞;

6.1. EXPANDER GRAPHS
269

(ii) deg(x) = #({y ∈Vn : y ∼x}) ≤k for all n ∈N and all x ∈Vn;

(iii) h(Gn) ≥ε for all n ∈N.

The constant ε is called an expanding constant for (Gn)n.

The existence of expander graphs is settled by the following proposi-
tion, which can be proved by elementary counting arguments (see [Lubot–94,
Proposition 1.2.1]).

Proposition 6.1.4 Let k be an integer with k ≥5 and ε = 1/2. There exists
a family of (k, ε)-expanders.

Remark 6.1.5 (i) Let G = (V, E) be a connected k-regular finite graph;
consider the simple random walk associated to G (see Example 5.1.1). Let ∆

We will give an explicit construction of a family of expander graphs using
Property (T). As a preparation, we review the construction of the Schreier
graph associated to a subgroup H of a group Γ.
Let Γ be finitely generated group, and let S be a finite generating set of
Γ with S−1 = S. Let Γ act on a set V. Define the Schreier graph G(V, S) as
follows:
• the set of vertices is V,

270
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

• (x, y) ∈V × V is an edge if and only if y = sx for some s ∈S.
Observe that, for every vertex x ∈V, we have deg(x) ≤k, where k = #S.
We assume further that the graph G(V, S) is connected; this is the case if
and only if Γ acts transitively on V.

Remark 6.1.6 If H is a subgroup of Γ, we consider the natural action of
Γ on Γ/H. The corresponding Schreier G(Γ/H, S) is connected. Every con-
nected Schreier graph G(V, S) is of the form G(Γ/H, S) for a subgroup H of
G. Indeed, if we choose a base point v0 ∈V, the Γ-space V can be identified
with Γ/H, where H is the stabilizer of v0 in Γ.

With the previous notation, let πV be the quasi-regular representation of
Γ on ℓ2(V ), thus

πV (γ)ξ(x) = ξ(γ−1x),

6.2
Norm of convolution operators

Let G be a locally compact group with left Haar measure dx. Let (π, H)
be a unitary representation of G without non-zero invariant vectors and µ
an absolutely continuous probability measure on G. We give estimates of
the norm of the associated “convolution” operator π(µ) ∈L(H) in terms of
Kazhdan constants.
Recall from Section F.4 that π extends to a *-representation, again de-
noted by π, of the Banach *-algebra L1(G) of all integrable functions on G :
for µ in L1(G), the operator π(µ) ∈L(H) is defined by

π(µ)ξ =

Lemma 3.3 in [FisMa–05].

In case G has Property (T), the previous proposition yields, for fixed µ
as above, a uniform bound for the norms of the operators π(µ) for every
unitary representation π without non-zero invariant vectors.

Corollary 6.2.3 Let G be locally compact group with Property (T), and let
(Q, ε) be a Kazhdan pair for G, where Q is a compact Kazhdan set with
Q−1 = Q and 1 ∈Q. Let µ ∈C(G)1,+ with µ > 0 on Q.
Then there exists a constant δ < 1 such that, for every unitary represen-
tation π of G without non-zero invariant vectors, we have ∥π(µ)∥≤δ.

6.3
Ergodic theory and Property (T)

Property (T) for a group G has strong implications for its measure preserving
actions. Indeed, Property (T) can be characterised in terms of such actions.
Let G be a second countable locally compact group acting measurably on
a measure space (Ω, ν); see Section A.6. Throughout this section, we assume
that

• ν is a probability measure on Ω;

• ν is G-invariant.

A unitary representation πν of G is defined on L2(Ω, ν) by

πν(g)f(ω) = f(g−1ω),
f ∈L2(Ω, ν), g ∈G, ω ∈Ω

(Proposition A.6.1). Observe that L2(Ω, ν) contains the constant function
1Ωon Ω. The subspace orthogonal to 1Ωis

L2
0(Ω, ν) =
�

There is a close link, first observed by Koopman [Koopm–31], between
ergodic theory of group actions and unitary representations. In particular,
the following properties are equivalent (Exercise 6.5.4):

(i) the action of G on Ωis ergodic, which means that any measurable
subset A of Ωwhich is G-invariant is trivial in the sense that ν(A) = 0
or ν(Ω\ A) = 0;

280
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

(ii) the unitary representation π0
ν has no non-zero invariant vectors.

The following result is a rephrasing of Corollary 6.2.3 in the context of
ergodic group actions.

Corollary 6.3.1 Let G be a second countable locally compact group with
Property (T). Let (Q, ε) be a Kazhdan pair for G, where Q is a compact
Kazhdan set with Q−1 = Q and 1 ∈Q. Let µ ∈C(G)1,+ with µ(x) > 0 for
all x ∈Q.
Then there exists a constant δ < 1 such that, for every measure preserving
ergodic action of G on a probability space (Ω, ν), we have ∥π0
ν(µ)∥≤δ.

For later use, we recall that a measure preserving action of G on a prob-
ability space (Ω, ν) is said to be weakly mixing if the diagonal action of G on
the product measure space (Ω×Ω, ν ⊗ν) is ergodic. Since L2(Ω×Ω, ν ⊗ν) ∼=
L2(Ω, ν) ⊗L2(Ω, ν), the following properties are equivalent (see Proposi-
tion A.1.12):

(i) the action of G on Ωis weakly mixing;

(ii) the unitary representation (π0

sional subrepresentation.

In particular, this shows that every weakly mixing action is ergodic.

6.3. ERGODIC THEORY AND PROPERTY (T)
281

Schmidt-Connes-Weiss’ characterisation of Property (T)

Let G be a second countable locally compact group acting measurably on
the probability space (Ω, ν) and preserving ν. If this action is ergodic, there
is no non-trivial invariant measurable subset of Ω. Nevertheless, there might
exist non-trivial asymptotically invariant subsets in the following sense.
A sequence of measurable subsets (An)n of Ωis said to be asymptotically
invariant if, for every compact subset Q of G,

lim
n sup
g∈Q
ν(gAn△An) = 0,

where △denotes the symmetric difference. If either ν(An) →0 or ν(An) →1,
then (An)n is clearly asymptotically invariant. An asymptotically invariant
sequence (An)n is said to be non-trivial if

inf
n ν(An)(1 −ν(An)) > 0.

The action of G on Ωis said to be strongly ergodic if there exists no non-trivial
asymptotically invariant sequence in Ω.

Proposition 6.3.2 Let G be a second countable locally compact group acting
on the probability space (Ω, ν) and preserving ν. If there exists a non-trivial
asymptotically invariant sequence in Ω, then the unit representation 1G is
weakly contained in π0
ν.
Equivalently: if 1G is not weakly contained in π0
ν, then the action of G on
(Ω, ν) is strongly ergodic.

Proof
If (An)n is a non-trivial asymptotically invariant sequence in Ω, set

fn = χAn −ν(An)1Ω.

Then fn ∈L2
0(Ω, ν),

∥fn∥2 = ν(An)(1 −ν(An))
and
∥π0
ν(g)fn −fn∥2 = ν(gAn△An).

It follows that π0
ν almost has invariant vectors. ■

Remark 6.3.3 (i) The converse does not hold in the previous proposition: in
[Schmi–81, (2.7) Example], an example is given of a strongly ergodic measure

282
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

preserving action of the free group F3 on a probability space such that the
associated representation π0
ν almost has invariant vectors.
(ii) Let G be a second countable locally compact group acting on the
probability space (Ω, ν) and preserving ν. Following [Schmi–81], we say that
a sequence of measurable subsets (An)n of Ωis an I-sequence, if ν(An) > 0
for every n, limn ν(An) = 0, and

lim
n sup
g∈Q
ν(gAn△An)

ν(An)
= 0

for every compact subset Q of G. As in the previous proposition, it is easy
to show that the existence of an I-sequence implies that the associated π0
ν
almost has invariant vectors. In [Schmi–81], it is shown that the converse
is true for discrete groups: if π0
ν almost has invariant vectors, then an I-
sequence exists. This was generalized to arbitrary locally compact groups in
[FurSh–99, Section 5]; see also [KleMa–99, Appendix].

Using Theorem 2.12.9, we give a slightly stronger version of Connes-Weiss’
result from [ConWe–80], with a simplified proof.

Theorem 6.3.4 (Connes-Weiss) Let G be a second countable locally com-
pact group. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) G has Property (T);

(ii) every measure preserving ergodic action of G is strongly ergodic;

(iii) every measure preserving weakly mixing action of G is strongly ergodic.

Proof
That (i) implies (ii) follows from Proposition 6.3.2. It is clear that
(ii) implies (iii).
We assume that G does not have Property (T) and construct a weakly
mixing action of G on a measure space (Ω, ν) with a non-trivial asymptoti-
cally invariant sequence.
There exists a unitary representation π of G on a separable Hilbert space
H which almost has invariant vectors but no non-zero invariant vectors. By
Theorem 2.12.9 (see also Remark 2.12.11), we can assume that π has no non-
zero finite dimensional subrepresentation and that π is the complexification
of an orthogonal representation. Thus H = H′ ⊗C for a real Hilbert space
H′ such that π(g)H′ = H′ for all g ∈G.

283

Orbit equivalence and measure equivalence

Let (Ω, ν) be a Borel space with a non-atomic probability measure ν. We will
always assume that Ωis an uncountable standard Borel space, so that there
exists an isomorphism of Borel spaces f : [0, 1] →Ω(see Section F.5). We
will also assume that ν is the image under f of the Lebesgue measure on
[0, 1].
Given a measure preserving ergodic action of a countable group Γ on a
standard Borel space (Ω, ν), we consider the equivalence relation RΓ on Ω
associated to it and defined by

xRΓy
if
Γx = Γy.

We can ask which properties of the group Γ and its action on (Ω, ν) are
determined by the equivalence relation RΓ. This leads to the notion of orbit
equivalence.
Two measure preserving ergodic actions of two countable groups Γ1 and
Γ2 on standard Borel spaces (Ω1, ν1) and (Ω2, ν2) are orbit equivalent if there
exist measurable subsets Ω′
1 and Ω′
2 with measure 1 in Ω1 and Ω2 and a Borel
isomorphism f : Ω′
1 →Ω′
2 with f∗(ν1) = ν2 such that, for ν1-almost every
ω ∈Ω′
1, we have
f(Γ1ω) = Γ2f(ω).

An action of a group Γ on a probability space is essentially free if, for
ν-almost every ω ∈Ω, the stabilizer of ω is reduced to {e}.
It has been shown by Dye [Dye–59] that any two essentially free, mea-
sure preserving ergodic actions of Z on standard probability spaces are orbit
equivalent. This has been generalized as follows by [OrnWei–80].

286
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

Theorem 6.3.5 Let Γ1 and Γ2 be countable amenable groups. Any two es-
sentially free, measure preserving ergodic actions of Γ1 and Γ2 on standard
Borel spaces are orbit equivalent.

For a further generalization of this result, see [CoFeW–81]. In contrast
to this, we have the following theorem of Hjorth [Hjort–05].

Theorem 6.3.6 Let Γ be an infinite discrete group with Property (T). There
exist uncountably many essentially free, measure preserving ergodic actions
of Γ which are pairwise not orbit equivalent.

Remark 6.3.7 (i) The previous result was obtained for some higher rank
lattices in [GefGo–89] as a consequence of Zimmer’s superrigidity results for
cocycles; see also [Zimm–84a, Example 5.2.13].
(ii) While the proof of Theorem 6.3.6 in [Hjort–05] is an existence result,
[Popa–06c] gives an explicit construction of uncountably many non orbit
equivalent ergodic actions, for any group Γ containing an infinite normal
subgroup N such that (Γ, N) has Property (T).

As a consequence of Theorem 6.3.4, we obtain the following result from
[Schmi–81, Corollary 3.6].

Corollary 6.3.8 Let Γ be a countable group which is non-amenable and does
not have Property (T). Then Γ has two essentially free, measure preserving
ergodic actions on standard Borel spaces which are not orbit equivalent.

Proof
Consider the Bernoulli shift of Γ. More precisely, let

Ω1 =
�

γ∈Γ
Z/2Z

be the direct product of copies of Z/2Z indexed by Γ, equipped with the
product topology and the normalised Haar measure ν1. Consider the action
of Γ on (Ω1, ν1) given by shifting the coordinates. The corresponding uni-
tary representation on L2
0(Ω1, ν1) is equivalent to a multiple of the regular
representation of Γ (Exercise E.4.5). Since Γ is not amenable, there exists no
non-trivial asymptotically invariant sequence in Ω1 (Proposition 6.3.2), that
is, the Γ-action on Ω1 is strongly ergodic. Moreover, this action is essentially
free.

6.3. ERGODIC THEORY AND PROPERTY (T)
287

On the other hand, since Γ does not have Property (T), it has a measure
preserving weakly mixing action on a standard Borel space (Ω2, ν2) which is
not strongly ergodic, by Connes-Weiss Theorem 6.3.4. The diagonal action
of Γ on the direct product (Ω1 × Ω2, ν1 ⊗ν2) is ergodic. Indeed, we have the
following decomposition of L2(Ω1 ×Ω2, ν1 ⊗ν2) ∼= L2(Ω1, ν1)⊗L2(Ω2, ν2) into
Γ-invariant subspaces:

L2(Ω1 × Ω2, ν1 ⊗ν2) ∼=
�

�

�
C1Ω1 ⊗L2

�
⊕C1Ω1×Ω2.

The unitary representation on L2

tiple of the regular representation of Γ (Corollary E.2.6). Hence, since Γ is
infinite, L2
0(Ω1, ν1) ⊗L2(Ω2, ν2) contains no non-zero invariant function. The
space C1Ω1 ⊗L2
0(Ω2, ν2) = L2
0(Ω2, ν2) contains no non-zero invariant function,
by ergodicity of the action of Γ on Ω2. It follows that the only Γ-invariant
functions in L2(Ω1 × Ω2, ν1 ⊗ν2) are the constants.
Moreover, the action of Γ on Ω1 × Ω2 is obviously essentially free and not
strongly ergodic. Since strong ergodicity is an invariant for orbit equivalence
(Exercise 6.5.8), the claim follows. ■

From the previous corollary together with Theorems 6.3.5 and 6.3.6, we
obtain the following characterisation of countable amenable groups.

Corollary 6.3.9 A countable group Γ is amenable if and only if any two
essentially free, measure preserving ergodic actions of Γ on standard Borel
spaces are orbit equivalent.

We now turn to the notion of measure equivalent groups, introduced
by Gromov in [Gromo–93, 0.5.E] and studied by Furman in [Furma–99a],
[Furma–99b].

Definition 6.3.10 Two countable groups Γ and Λ are measure equivalent if
there exist commuting, essentially free, and measure preserving actions of Γ
and Λ on a standard Borel space Ω, equipped with a σ-finite measure ν, such
that the action of each of the groups Γ and Λ has a measurable fundamental
domain with finite measure. The space (Ω, ν) is called a coupling of Γ and
Λ.

Example 6.3.11 Let Γ and Λ be lattices in a second countable locally com-
pact group G. Then Γ and Λ are measure equivalent. Indeed, we can take as

288
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

coupling (G, ν), where ν is a left Haar measure of G and where Γ and Λ act
on G by translations from the left and from the right, respectively. Observe
that G is unimodular, since it contains a lattice (Proposition B.2.2). This
shows that these actions are measure preserving.

Remark 6.3.12 (i) Measure equivalence can be considered as a measure-
theoretic analogue of quasi-isometry (see Example 3.6.2). Indeed, the follow-
ing criterion for quasi-isometric groups holds: two finitely generated groups
Γ and Λ are quasi-isometric if and ony if there exist commuting, proper
continuous actions of Γ and Λ on a locally compact space X such that the
action of each of the groups Γ and Λ has a compact fundamental domain (see
[Gromo–93, 0.2.C′
2] and [Harpe–00, Chapter IV, Exercises 34 and 35]).
(ii) Measure equivalence is related to orbit equivalence as follows. The
countable groups Γ and Λ are measure equivalent if and only if they admit
essentially free, measure preserving actions on standard Borel spaces (Ω1, ν1)
and (Ω2, ν2) which are stably orbit equivalent, that is, there exist measurable
subsets of positive measure A of Ω1 and B of Ω2 and an isomorphism f :
A →B such that f∗(ν1/ν1(A)) = ν2/ν2(A) and f(Γω ∩A) = Λf(ω) ∩B for
almost every ω ∈A (see [Furma–99b, Lemma 3.2 and Theorem 3.3]).

Recall that Property (T) is not an invariant of quasi-isometry (see Theo-
rem 3.6.5). In contrast to this, we have the following result, which is Corollary
1.4 in [Furma–99a] and which is also a consequence of Theorems 4.1.7 and
4.1.9 in [Popa].

Theorem 6.3.13 Let Γ and Λ be measure equivalent countable groups. If Γ
has Property (T), then Λ has Property (T).

Remark 6.3.15 Zimmer introduced a notion of Property (T) for a measured
equivalence relation and, in particular, for an ergodic action of a countable
group Γ on a standard Borel space (Ω, ν) with a quasi-invariant probability
measure ν (see [Zimme–81] and [Moore–82]). He showed that, if ν is invariant
and the action is weakly mixing, then the action has Property (T) if and only
if Γ has Property (T). For a generalization to the context of groupoids, see
[Anant–05].

6.4
Uniqueness of invariant means

Let n be an integer, n ≥1. The so-called Banach-Ruziewicz problem asks
whether the normalised Lebesgue measure λ, defined on all Lebesgue mea-
surable subsets of the unit sphere Sn in Rn+1, is the unique normalised
SOn+1(R)-invariant finitely additive measure.
In the case n = 1, it was

292
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

shown by Banach that the answer is negative. Moreover, Tarski proved, us-
ing the Banach-Tarski paradox, that every SOn+1(R)-invariant finitely addi-
tive measure on the Lebesgue measurable subsets of Sn has to be absolutely
continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure λ. This shows that the
Banach–Ruziewicz problem can be formulated as follows (see Section G.1):
Is integration against λ the unique SOn+1(R)-invariant mean on L∞(Sn, λ)?
The following result, due to Rosenblatt [Rosen–81], Schmidt [Schmi–81] and
Losert-Rindler [LosRi–81], relates the Banach-Ruziewicz problem to Prop-
erty (T).

Proposition 6.4.1 Let Γ be a countable group acting in a measure preserv-
ing way on a probability space (Ω, ν). Assume that the associated unitary
representation π0
ν of Γ on L2
0(Ω, ν) does not weakly contains 1Γ. Then inte-
gration against ν is the unique Γ-invariant mean on L∞(Ω, ν).

Proof
Let m be a Γ-invariant mean on L∞(Ω, ν). We show that m = ν.
The arguments are similar to arguments used in the proofs of Theorems G.3.1
and G.3.2.
Using the density of

and

to m in the weak*-topology. By invariance of m, we have

argument (see Proof of Theorem G.3.1), we can assume that the convergence
in (∗) holds in the L1- norm.
Define fi = √gi ∈L2(Ω, ν). Then ∥fi∥2 = 1 and, as in the proof of G.3.2,

Remark 6.4.2 In the previous proposition, the converse is also true: if ν is
the unique invariant mean on L∞(Ω, ν), then π0
ν does not weakly contains 1Γ.
This implication holds for arbitrary second countable locally compact groups
(see [FurSh–99, Theorem 1.6]).

Corollary 6.4.3 Let Γ be a discrete group with Property (T) acting in a
measure preserving way on a probability space (Ω, ν). Assume that this action
is ergodic. Then ν is the unique invariant mean on L∞(Ω, ν).

Proof
Since the action of Γ is ergodic, the representation π0
ν has no non-
zero fixed vectors, and the claim follows from the previous proposition. ■

The following theorem, due to Margulis [Margu–80] and Sullivan [Sulli–81],
combined with the previous corollary, shows that Banach-Ruziewicz problem
for Sn has a positive answer when n ≥4. Observe that a dense subgroup
group Γ of SOn+1(R) acts ergodically on Sn. Indeed, if f ∈L2
0(Sn, λ) is a Γ-
invariant function, then f is SOn+1(R)-invariant, by density of Γ, and hence
f = 0.

Theorem 6.4.4 For n ≥5, the group SOn(R) contains a dense subgroup Γ
which has Property (T) as discrete group.

Proof
We follow the construction from [Margu–80]. Let p be a prime
integer with p ≡1 mod 4. Since the equation x2 = −1 has a solution in Qp,
the group SOn has Qp–rank equal to [n/2]. Hence, SOn(Qp) has Property

294
CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

(T) for n ≥5 (see Theorem 1.6.1; observe that SO4 is not simple). The
group Λ = SOn(Z[1/p]) embeds diagonally into the Kazhdan group

G = SOn(Qp) × SOn(R)

as a lattice in G, and therefore has Property (T). Denote by Γ the projection
of Λ to SOn(R). Then Γ is a dense subgroup of SOn(R) and has Property
(T).■

Remark 6.4.5 (i) Zimmer [Zimm–84c] showed that SOn(R) does not con-
tain an infinite countable group with Property (T) for n = 3, 4. A stronger
result was proved in [GuHiW–05]: For a field K, every countable subgroup
of GL2(K) has the Haagerup property (see Definition 2.7.5).
(ii) The previous theorem has the following extension. Let G be a simple
compact real Lie group which is not locally isomorphic to SO3(R). Then G
contains a dense subgroup Γ which has Property (T) as discrete group (see
[Margu–91, Chapter III, Proposition 5.7]).
(iii) Drinfeld [Drinf–84] proved, using deep estimates for Fourier coeffi-
cients of modular forms, that SOn(R), for n = 3, 4, contains a dense subgroup
Γ such that the Γ-representation π0
λ on L2
0(Sn−1, λ) does not weakly contain
1Γ. So, Proposition 6.4.1 still applies and shows that the Banach-Ruziewicz
problem has a positive answer for n = 2, 3.

In summary, we have the following result.

Corollary 6.4.6 For n ≥2, the Lebesgue measure is the unique rotation-
invariant, finitely additive normalised measure defined on all Lebesgue mea-
surable subsets of Sn.

Remark 6.4.7 It is crucial that we considered above the Lebesgue measur-
able subsets of Sn. The corresponding problem for the Borel subsets of Sn is
open.

CHAPTER 6. SOME APPLICATIONS OF PROPERTY (T)

Chapter 7

A short list of open questions

We have collected below a sample of open questions which are standard in the
subject. There are several other ones in the various papers of our reference
list.

Open examples of groups

(7.1) It is not known whether the automorphism group Aut(Fk) of a non
abelian free group Fk on k generators has Property (T) for k large enough.
The question is often asked for the related group Out(Fk) of outer automor-
phisms, which is the quotient of Aut(Fk) by the group of inner automorphisms
(which is isomorphic to Fk).
The relevance of the question is discussed in [LubPa–01]; see the end
of our historical introduction. Observe that Aut(F2) does not have Prop-
erty (T) since it has a quotient isomorphic to GL2(Z), neither does Aut(F3)
by [McCoo–89]. See also Corollary 1.3 in [GruLu].

(7.2) Let Σg denote a closed oriented surface of genus g ≥2. Denote by Modg
the corresponding mapping class group, namely the group of homotopy classes
of orientation–preserving homeomorphisms of Σg. It is not known whether
this group has Property (T) for g large enough.
There is in [Taher–00] a report of a GAP–assisted computation which
shows that Mod2 does not have Property (T).

(7.3) Similarly, we do not know whether Property (T) holds for the Burnside

299

300
CHAPTER 7. A SHORT LIST OF OPEN QUESTIONS

group
B(k, n) = Fk/ ⟨xn = 1 | x ∈Fk⟩,

or for some infinite quotient of it (it is natural to assume k ≥2 and n large
enough for B(k, n) to be infinite). See the last section in [Shal–ICM] for a
short discussion.

(7.4) Does there exist a subgroup of infinite index in SL3(Z) which has
Property (T)?
More generally, if G denotes one of the groups SL3(R), Sp4(R), and
Sp(2, 1), does there exist a discrete subgroup of G which is not a lattice and
which has Property (T)?

(7.5) What are the groups of homeomorphisms of the circle which have
Property (FH)? See Section 2.9 for the result of Navas and Reznikov on the
appropriate diffeomorphism group.
It is known that, for n ≥1, the group of homeomorphisms of the sphere
of dimension n, viewed as a discrete group, has Property (FH) [Cornu–06b].

Open examples of pairs of groups

(7.6) We feel that we do not know enough examples of pairs (Γ, ∆) which
have Property (T). As a sample of specific pairs for which we do not know
whether Property (T) holds or not, there are

• (Aut(Fk), Fk), where Fk is embedded via inner automorphisms;

• (Aut(Fk) ⋉Fk, Fk), where Fk is embedded as the second factor;

• (Modg ⋉π1(Σg), π1(Σg)).

(7.7) Let G be a locally compact group; in case it helps for what follows,
assume that G is compactly generated and second countable. Let H, K, L be
closed subgroups of G such that L ⊂K ⊂H ⊂G. As a first preliminary
observation, let us point out that, if the pair (H, K) has Property (T), then
so does (G, L). From now on, assume that there exists a finite K–invariant
measure on K/L and a finite G–invariant measure on G/H. Recall that, if
the pair (G, L) has Property (T), then the pair (G, K) also has it.

301

If the pair (G, L) has Property (T), does it follow that (H, L) has it? The
answer is known to be affirmative in the particular case where L is moreover
a normal subgroup of G.
See Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 in [BatRo–99], as well as [Jolis–05].

Properties of Kazhdan groups

(7.8) Is it true that a countable group which can be left ordered does not
have Property (T)?
For comparison with Question (7.5), recall that a countable group is left
orderable if and only if it is isomorphic to a subgroup of the group of orienta-
tion preserving homeomorphisms of the real line (see for example Theorem 6.8
in [Ghys–01]).
A related result was proved in [ChMaV–04]: a left ordered locally compact
group G with the property that segments {x ∈G
:
a ≤x ≤b} are
measurable with finite Haar measure cannot have Property (T).

(7.9) Does there exist an infinite group with Kazhdan Property (T) which
is not of uniform exponential growth?
See the discussion in [Harpe–02].

(7.10) Does there exist a countable infinite Kazhdan group Γ such that the
subset {π ∈�Γ : dim(π) < ∞} is dense in the unitary dual �Γ of Γ?
See [Bekk–99] and [LubSh–04].

Uniform Kazhdan property

(7.11) Say that a finitely generated group Γ has Property (T) uniformly if
there exists a number κ > 0 which is a Kazhdan constant for Γ and every
finite generating set Q of Γ (Definition 1.1.3).
Does the group SL3(Z) have Property (T) uniformly? (This appears for
example as Problem 10.3.1 in [Lubot–94].)
A related question is to know whether there exists κ′ > 0 which is a
Kazhdan constant for SL3(Z) and every generating set of at most 100 (say)
elements. For groups which have Property (T) but which do not have it
uniformly, see [GelZu–02] and [Osin–02]. For an infinite group which has
Property (T) uniformly, see [OsiSo].

302
CHAPTER 7. A SHORT LIST OF OPEN QUESTIONS

Kazhdan subsets of amenable groups

(7.12) Kazhdan property involves compact Kazhdan sets, but arbitrary
Kazhdan sets (Definition 1.1.3) could deserve more attention.
The question of knowing if a subset Q of Z is a Kazhdan set is possibly
related to the equidistribution in the circle of the sequence (e2iπnθ)n∈Q for θ
irrational, in the sense of Weyl [Weyl–16] (this was brought to our attention
by Y. Shalom).
More generally, what are the Kazhdan subsets of Zk, Rk, the Heisen-
berg group, other infinite amenable groups ? Observe that, for a countable
group which has Property (T), a subset is a Kazhdan set if and only if it is
generating.

Fundamental groups of manifolds

(7.13) Let Γ be the fundamental group of a compact manifold M. If Γ has
Property (T), what are the consequences for the topology and the geometry
of M?
For example, if M is a closed Riemannian manifold with pinched sec-
tional curvature, −4 < K < −1, can π1(M) be Kazhdan ? (Problem 5.3 in
[Spatz–95]).

(7.14) Let Γ be the fundamental group of a compact orientable 3–manifold.
Is there an elementary proof which would show that Γ has Property (T) if
and only if Γ is finite?
In [Fujiw–99] (see also Exercise 1.8.18), there is an argument reducing
the claim to the geometrization conjecture of Thurston.

(7.15) If the manifold is hyperbolic, is it true that Γ does not have Prop-
erty (τ)?
This is the so–called Lubotzky–Sarnak conjecture. See Conjecture 4.2 in
[Lubot–97], and [Lacke–06].

Part II

Background on unitary
representations

303

Appendix A

Unitary group representations

A linear representation, or simply a representation, of a group G in a vector
space V is a group homomorphism from G to the group of linear automor-
phisms of V. Chapter A of this appendix collects basic facts about unitary
representations of topological groups in Hilbert spaces. Hilbert spaces are as-
sumed to be complex, unless stated otherwise (as in Chapter 2, Section A.7,
and Section C.2, where Hilbert spaces are most often real).
Topological
groups are always assumed to be Hausdorff.

A.1
Unitary representations

The inner product of two vectors ξ, η in a Hilbert space H is denoted by ⟨ξ, η⟩,
and is linear in the first variable. We denote by L(H1, H2) the vector space
of all continuous linear operators from a Hilbert space H1 to a Hilbert space
H2; an operator T ∈L(H1, H2) has an adjoint T ∗∈L(H2, H1). We write
L(H) for L(H, H), and observe that it is naturally an involutive complex
algebra, with unit the identity operator I of H. An operator U : H →H is
unitary if
UU ∗= U ∗U = I

or, equivalently, if ⟨Uξ, Uη⟩= ⟨ξ, η⟩for all ξ, η ∈H and if U is onto. The
unitary group U(H) of H is the group of all unitary operators in L(H).

Definition A.1.1 A unitary representation of a topological group G in a
Hilbert space H is a group homomorphism π : G →U(H) which is strongly

305

306
APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

continuous in the sense that the mapping

G →H,
g �→π(g)ξ

is continuous for every vector ξ in H. We will often write (π, H) instead of
π : G →U(H). About continuity, see Exercise A.8.1.
Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G, and let K be a closed G-
invariant subspace of H. Denoting, for every g in G, by

πK(g) : K →K

the restriction of the operator π(g) to K, we obtain a unitary representation
πK of G on K. We say that πK is a subrepresentation of π.
Given a unitary representation (π, H) of G and vectors ξ, η in H, the
continuous function
G →C,
g �→⟨π(g)ξ, η⟩

is called a matrix coefficient of π.

An important feature of unitary representations is that they are com-
pletely reducible in the sense that every closed invariant subspace has a
closed invariant complement. More precisely:

Proposition A.1.2 Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G, and let K
be a G-invariant subspace. Then K⊥, the orthogonal complement of K in H,
is G-invariant.

Proof
We have

⟨π(g)ξ, η⟩= ⟨ξ, π(g)∗η⟩= ⟨ξ, π(g−1)η⟩= 0,

for every g in G, ξ in K⊥, and η in K. ■

Definition A.1.3 An intertwining operator between two unitary represen-
tations (π1, H1) and (π2, H2) of G is a continuous linear operator T from H1
to H2 such that Tπ1(g) = π2(g)T for all g ∈G. The representations π1 and
π2 are equivalent, and we will write π1 ≃π2, if there exists an intertwining
operator T ∈L(H1, H2) which is isometric and onto; the next proposition
shows in particular that we would define the same notion by requiring only
T to be invertible.

A.1. UNITARY REPRESENTATIONS
307

First, let us recall some terminology. An operator U from a Hilbert space
H1 to another Hilbert space H2 is a partial isometry if there exists a closed
subspace M of H1 such that the restriction of U to M is an isometry (that
is, ∥Uξ∥= ∥ξ∥for all ξ in M) and such that U = 0 on M⊥. The subspace
M = (KerU)⊥is the initial space and U(M) the final space of the partial
isometry U.
Given a continuous operator T : H1 →H2, set |T| = (T ∗T)1/2. Since

∥|T|ξ∥2 = ⟨T ∗Tξ, ξ⟩= ∥Tξ∥2,

the mapping |T|ξ �→Tξ extends to an isometry U from the closure of |T|(H1)
onto the closure of T(H1). Extend now U linearly to H1 by setting U = 0 on
|T|(H1)⊥= KerT. Then U is a partial isometry with initial space (KerT)⊥

and final space the closure of T(H1), and we have T = U|T|. This is the
so-called polar decomposition of T (for more details, see Problem 105 in
[Halmo–67]).

Proposition A.1.4 Let (π1, H1) and (π2, H2) be two unitary representations
of G. Let T ∈L(H1, H2) be an intertwining operator between π1 and π2; set
M1 = (KerT)⊥and let M2 denote the closure of the image of T.
Then M1 and M2 are closed invariant subspaces of H1 and H2 respec-
tively, and the subrepresentation of π1 defined by M1 is equivalent to the
subrepresentation of π2 defined by M2.

Proof
We first check that T ∗∈L(H2, H1) intertwines π2 and π1. Indeed,
for all g ∈G, we have

T ∗π2(g) = (π2(g−1)T)∗= (Tπ1(g−1))∗= π1(g)T ∗.

It follows that T ∗T ∈L(H1) intertwines π1 with itself. Since |T| = (T ∗T)1/2

is a limit in the strong operator topology of polynomials in T ∗T (see Prob-
lem 95 in [Halmo–67]), |T| also intertwines π1 with itself. Let T = U|T|
be the polar decomposition of T; then KerU = KerT and the restriction of
U to M1 = (KerT)⊥is an isometry onto M2. It remains to check that U
intertwines π1 with π2.
Let g in G. On the one hand,

π2(g)U|T|ξ = π2(g)Tξ = Tπ1(g)ξ = U|T|π1(g)ξ = Uπ1(g)|T|ξ,

for all ξ in H1. This shows that π2(g)U and Uπ1(g) coincide on the image
of |T|, and therefore on its closure M1. On the other hand, U = 0 on KerT
and KerT is π1(G)-invariant. Hence π2(g)U = Uπ1(g) on H1. ■

308
APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

Definition A.1.5 A unitary representation ρ of G is strongly contained or
contained in a representation π of G if ρ is equivalent to a subrepresentation
of π. This is denoted by ρ ≤π.

π(g) = πK(g) ⊕πK⊥(g),

for all g ∈G. Thus, π is the direct sum of the two representations πK and

Definition A.1.7 A unitary representation (π, H) of G is said to be irre-
ducible if the only G-invariant closed subspaces of H are the trivial ones,
that is, {0} and H.
The set of equivalence classes of irreducible representations of G is called
the unitary dual of G and is denoted by �G.
At this point, it is not clear that �G is a set. It will be seen that this is
indeed the case, in Corollary A.2.3 for abelian groups and in Remark C.4.13
in general; we will also equip �G with a natural topology, in Definition A.2.4
for abelian groups and in Section F.2 in general.

We will use several times the following elementary fact.

G and let (π, K) be an irreducible unitary representation of G. Assume that π

from Proposition A.1.4.■

One-dimensional unitary representations of G are obviously irreducible.
They correspond to the unitary characters of G.

Definition A.1.9 A unitary character of G is a continuous homomorphism
χ : G →S1, where S1 is the multiplicative group of all complex numbers of
modulus 1. We will identify a one-dimensional representation π of G with its
character g �→Trace(π(g)).
The constant character

G →S1,
g �→1

is called the unit representation or unit character of G and will be denoted
by 1G.

Observe that 1G is contained in a unitary representation (π, H) if and
only if the subspace

HG = {ξ ∈H : π(g)ξ = ξ, for all
g ∈G}

of G-invariant vectors in H is non-zero. Observe also that two one-dimensional
unitary representations are equivalent if and only if their associated unitary
characters coincide.
Let N be a closed normal subgroup of G. Let the quotient group G/N
be given the quotient topology, and let p : G →G/N denote the canonical
projection. Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G/N. Then (π ◦p, H)
is a unitary representation of G called the lift of π to G. We will often use

APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

the same notation for π and its lift to G, and in particular view �
G/N as a
subset of �G.

Let H be a Hilbert space. The conjugate Hilbert space H is the Hilbert
space with underlying additive group identical to that of H, with scalar
multiplication defined by

(λ, ξ) �→¯λξ,
λ ∈C, ξ ∈H

and with inner product [·, ·] defined by

[ξ, η] = ⟨η, ξ⟩,
ξ, η ∈H.

By Riesz theorem, the mapping

H →L(H, C),
η �→ϕη,

with ϕη(ξ) = ⟨ξ, η⟩, is a linear isomorphism of complex vector spaces.
Let G be a topological group and (π, H) a unitary representation of G.
For g ∈G, denote by ¯π(g) the operator on H which coincides with π(g) as
a set-theoretical transformation. It is straightforward to check that ¯π is a
unitary representation of G in H. Observe that ¯¯π = π.

Definition A.1.10 The representation ¯π is called the contragredient or con-
jugate representation of π.

We now discuss tensor products of unitary representations. Let H and K
be Hilbert spaces, and H⊗K their algebraic tensor product . The completion
of H ⊗K, with respect to the unique inner product for which

⟨ξ1 ⊗η1, ξ2 ⊗η2⟩= ⟨ξ1, ξ2⟩⟨η1, η2⟩,
ξ1, ξ2 ∈H, η1, η2 ∈K,

is called the
Hilbert tensor product of H and K and is again denoted by
H ⊗K.

Definition A.1.11 If π and ρ are unitary representations of a topological
group G on H and K, their tensor product is the unitary representation π ⊗ρ
of G defined on H ⊗K by

(π ⊗ρ)(g)(ξ ⊗η) = π(g)ξ ⊗ρ(g)η,

for all ξ ∈H, η ∈K, and g ∈G.

A.1. UNITARY REPRESENTATIONS
311

Observe that π ⊗ρ is the restriction to the diagonal subgroup ∆∼= G of
the outer tensor product π × ρ, a unitary representation of G × G defined
on the same Hilbert space by

(π × ρ)(g1, g2)(ξ ⊗η) = π(g1)ξ ⊗ρ(g2)η.

We discuss now another realization of π ⊗ρ. Let K be the conjugate
Hilbert space of K, and denote by HS(K, H) the space of Hilbert-Schmidt
operators from K to H. Equipped with the inner product

(T, S) �→Trace(S∗T),
T, S ∈HS(K, H),

HS(K, H) is a Hilbert space. Let Φ : H ⊗K →HS(K, H) be the linear
mapping defined, for all ξ ∈H and η ∈K, by

Φ(ξ ⊗η)(ζ) = ⟨η, ζ⟩ξ,
ζ ∈K,

where the inner product on the right is taken in K. Then Φ is easily seen to
be an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces.
The tensor product π ⊗ρ corresponds to the unitary representation of G
on HS(K, H) given by

T �→π(g)Tρ(g−1),
T ∈HS(K, H), g ∈G.

This realization of π ⊗ρ is often very useful.
For instance, it shows
immediately that if π is finite dimensional then π ⊗π contains the unit
representation: the identity operator I is obviously invariant.
This is a
particular case of the following proposition.

Proposition A.1.12 Let π and ρ be unitary representations of the topolo-
gical group G on H and K. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) π ⊗ρ contains the unit representation 1G (that is, π ⊗ρ has a non-zero
invariant vector);

(ii) there exists a finite dimensional representation of G which is a subrep-
resentation of both π and ρ.

Proof
That (ii) implies (i) was already mentioned. Assume that π ⊗ρ
contains 1G. Then there exists a non-zero T ∈HS(K, H) with

π(g)Tρ(g−1) = T

312
APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

for all g ∈G. Thus, T intertwines π and ρ. Since T ∗T ∈L(K) is a compact
positive operator and is non zero, it has an eigenvalue λ > 0. The corre-
sponding eigenspace Eλ of T ∗T is a finite dimensional closed subspace of K.
As T ∗T intertwines π with itself, Eλ is invariant. Since

∥Tξ∥2 = ⟨T ∗Tξ, ξ⟩= λ∥ξ∥2,
for all
ξ ∈Eλ,

the restriction of λ−1/2T to Eλ is a bijective isometry between the finite
dimensional invariant subspaces Eλ and T(Eλ) of K and H, intertwining ρ
and π. ■

Corollary A.1.13 Let π and ρ be unitary representations of the topologi-
cal group G, and assume that π is irreducible. The following properties are
equivalent:

(i) π ⊗ρ contains the unit representation 1G;

(ii) π is finite dimensional and π is contained in ρ.

A.2
Schur’s Lemma

For a unitary representation (π, H) of G, the set of all operators T ∈L(H)
which intertwine π with itself (that is, those such that Tπ(g) = π(g)T for all
g ∈G) is called the commutant of π(G) and is denoted by π(G)′.
It is obvious that π(G)′ is a subalgebra of L(H) which is closed for the
weak operator topology. Moreover, π(G)′ is selfadjoint, that is, if T ∈π(G)′

then T ∗∈π(G)′; see the proof of Proposition A.1.4.

Proposition A.2.1 Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G. Let K be a
closed subspace of H and let P ∈L(H) be the orthogonal projection onto K.
Then K is G-invariant if and only if P ∈π(G)′.

Proof
Let Q = I −P be the orthogonal projection on K⊥. Then, for ξ in
H and g in G, we have

(∗)

Assume that K is G-invariant. Then K⊥is also G-invariant (Proposition A.1.2).
Hence, π(g)Pξ ∈K and π(g)Qξ ∈K⊥. This shows that (∗) is the orthogonal

decomposition of π(g)ξ with respect to K. In particular, π(g)Pξ = Pπ(g)ξ.
Thus, π(g)P = Pπ(g).
Conversely, assume that π(g)P = Pπ(g) for all g in G. Then, clearly, K
is G-invariant. ■

For an operator T ∈L(H), recall that the spectrum σ(T) of T is the set

so that σ(T) is contained in R.

Theorem A.2.2 (Schur’s Lemma) A unitary representation (π, H) of G
is irreducible if and only if π(G)′ consists of the scalar multiples of the identity
operator I.

Proof
Assume that π(G)′ consists of the multiples of I. Let K be a closed
G-invariant subspace of H, with corresponding orthogonal projection P. By
the previous proposition, P ∈π(G)′. So, P = λI for some complex number
λ. As P 2 = P, we have λ = 0 or λ = 1, that is, K = {0} or K = H. Thus, π
is irreducible.
Conversely, assume that π is irreducible and let T ∈π(G)′. Set T1 =
(T + T ∗)/2 and T2 = (T −T ∗)/2i. Then T = T1 + iT2 and T1, T2 ∈π(G)′.
Since T1 and T2 are selfadjoint, we can assume that T is selfadjoint.

Fix some λ0 in the spectrum σ(T) of T. Let f be the characteristic func-
tion of {λ0}. Let f(T) ∈L(H) be defined by functional calculus.
Then
f(T)2 = f(T) = f(T)∗, by (ii) above. That is, f(T) is an orthogonal pro-
jection. Moreover, f(T) ∈π(G)′, since T commutes with every π(g). Hence,
f(T) = 0 or f(T) = I, by irreducibility of π (see Proposition A.2.1). As
f ̸= 0, it follows that f(T) = I. This shows that σ(T) = {λ0}, that is,
T = λ0I. ■

Corollary A.2.3 Let G be an abelian topological group.
Then any irre-
ducible unitary representation of G is one-dimensional. Thus, the unitary
dual �G can be identified with the set of unitary characters of G.

Proof
Let (π, H) be an irreducible unitary representation of G. Since G
is abelian, π(G) is contained in π(G)′. Thus, by Schur’s Lemma, for every
g ∈G, the unitary operator π(g) is of the form χ(g)I. It is clear that the
dimension of H is 1 and that g �→χ(g) is a unitary character of G. ■

For an arbitrary topological group G, the set of unitary characters of G
is a group for pointwise multiplication

χ1χ2(g) := χ1(g)χ2(g),
g ∈G,

the group unit being the unit character, and the inverse of χ being the con-
jugate character χ (which corresponds to the contragredient representation).
Hence, if G is abelian, the unitary dual �G is an abelian group.

Definition A.2.4 Let G be an abelian topological group. The unitary dual
of G is called the dual group of G. Equipped with the topology of uniform
convergence on compact subsets of G, the dual group �G is a topological
group.

Let G be again arbitrary; let [G, G] denote the closure of the subgroup
generated by all commutators [g, h] = ghg−1h−1 for g, h ∈G. Every unitary
character of G is the lift of a character of G/[G, G]. Thus, the set of unitary
characters of G can be identified with the dual group of G/[G, G].

Example A.2.5 (i) Every y ∈R defines a unitary character of R by

Hence, by density, χ(x) = χy(x) for all x ∈R. The uniqueness of y is clear,

See Proposition D.4.5 for a generalisation of this to any local field.

APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

A.3
The Haar measure of a locally compact
group

A topological group G is locally compact if and only if there exists a compact
neighbourhood of the group unit e. This class of groups includes all discrete
groups (that is, groups with the discrete topology), all compact groups, and
all (finite dimensional) Lie groups.
One of the features which distinguishes locally compact groups from other
topological groups is the existence of a Haar measure.
We first recall a
few facts about measure theory on locally compact spaces.
It should be
mentioned that our measures are usually not assumed to be σ-finite. For
what follows, some good references are [BeChR–84], [Bou–Int1], [Bou–Int2],
[Halmo–74], [HewRo–63], and [Rudin–3].
Let X be a locally compact space.
A Borel measure on X is a (not
necessarily finite) positive measure on the σ-algebra B(X) of Borel subsets
of X (this is the smallest σ-algebra containing all open subsets). A Borel
measure µ on X is said to be regular if

(i) µ(B) = inf{µ(V ) : V
is open and
B ⊂V }, for any Borel set B,

(ii) µ(U) = sup{µ(K) :
K
is compact and
K ⊂U}, for every open
subset U of X,

(iii) µ(K) < ∞for every compact subset K of X.

In general, a regular Borel measure does not satisfy (ii) for all Borel sets.
However, if every open subset of X is σ-compact (that is, a countable union
of compact subsets), then (iii) implies (i) and (ii) (see [Rudin–3, Chapter 2,
Exercise 17 and Theorem 2.18]).
Let Cc(X) denote the space of continuous functions on X with compact
support. A linear functional ϕ : Cc(X) →C is said to be positive if ϕ(f) ≥0
for all non-negative real valued functions f in Cc(X). Such a linear functional
is called a Radon measure on X. A regular measure µ defines a Radon measure
on X, still denoted by µ, by means of the formula

µ(f) =
�

X
f(x)dµ(x),
for all
f ∈Cc(X).

Conversely, any Radon measure on X is obtained in this way from a unique
regular Borel measure on X. This is the standard
Riesz Representation
Theorem (see [Rudin–3, Theorem 2.14]).

A.3. HAAR MEASURE
317

Let G be a locally compact group. There exists a non-zero regular Borel
measure µ on G which is left invariant, that is,

µ(gB) = µ(B),
for all
B ∈B(G), g ∈G,

or, equivalently,
�

G
f(g−1x)dµ(x) =
�

G
f(x)dµ(x),
for all
f ∈Cc(G), g ∈G.

Moreover µ is unique, up to a multiplicative constant, that is, if µ′ is another
regular Borel measure which is left invariant, then µ′ = cµ for some non-
negative number c. For all this, see [HewRo–63, Chapter 15]. The measure
µ is called a left Haar measure on G.

Remark A.3.1 A result of Weil says that the class of groups which admit a
left invariant measure essentially coincides with the class of locally compact
groups. See [Weil–65, pp. 140–146]; see also [Macke–57] and [Varad–68].

As the next proposition shows, the support of a Haar measure on G is G.
Recall that the support of a regular Borel measure µ on a locally compact
space X is the smallest closed subset F of X such that µ(X \ F) = 0.

Proposition A.3.2 Let µ be a left Haar measure on G. Then, µ(U) > 0
for every non-empty open subset U of G and

Proof

Then µ(gU) = 0 for all g ∈G, by left invariance of µ. For any compact
subset K of G, there exists g1, . . . , gn ∈G such that K ⊂∪n
i=1giU. Hence,

that µ = 0, a contradiction.

A mapping f from a topological group G to some metric space (X, d) is

Example A.3.5 (i) The Lebesgue measure is a Haar measure on Rn. The

any subset B of G is a Haar measure. As µ is also right invariant, discrete
groups are unimodular.

(vii) The existence of Haar measures on connected Lie groups is easy to

(x) By (ix), semisimple real Lie groups are unimodular.

The modular function of a connected Lie group G can be determined as
follows. Let g be the Lie algebra of G, identified with the tangent space of
G at e. For g in G, let Ad(g) : g →g be the differential at e of the group
automorphism G →G, x �→gxg−1. Then Ad(g) is an automorphism of the
Lie algebra g, and Ad : G →GL(g) is a representation of G, called the
adjoint representation of G. The proof of the following proposition is left as
Exercise A.8.7.

Proposition A.3.6 Let G be a connected Lie group. Then

∆G(g) = det(Ad(g−1)),
g ∈G.

Example A.3.7 Let G be a connected nilpotent Lie group. Then Ad(g) is
unipotent (that is Ad(g) −I is nilpotent) and, hence, det(Ad(g)) = 1 for
every g in G. Thus, connected nilpotent Lie groups are unimodular. More

322
APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

generally, any locally compact nilpotent group is unimodular. Indeed, such a
group G is of polynomial growth, that is, for every compact neighbourhood U
of G, there exist a constant C > 0 and an integer d such that µ(U n) ≤Cnd

for all n ∈N, where µ is a left Haar measure on G [Guiva–73]. On the
other hand, a locally compact group with polynomial growth is unimodular
(Exercise A.8.10).

A.4
The regular representation of a locally
compact group

Thoughout this section, G is a locally compact group, with fixed left Haar
measure, usually denoted by dg.
For a function f : G →C on G and for a ∈G, we define the left and
right translates af : G →C and fa : G →C of f by

af(x) = f(ax)
and
fa(x) = f(xa)
for all
x ∈G.

Let L2(G) = L2(G, dg) be the Hilbert space of (equivalence classes of)
square integrable functions f : G →C with respect to the Haar measure.
For g in G, the operator λG(g) : L2(G) →L2(G) defined by

λG(g)ξ(x) = (g−1ξ)(x) = ξ(g−1x),
ξ ∈L2(G), x ∈G

is unitary, by left invariance of the Haar measure.
Moreover, λG(gh) =
λG(g)λG(h) for g, h in G, and the mapping

G →L2(G), g �→λG(g)ξ

is continuous for each ξ in L2(G); see Exercise A.8.3. Thus, (λG, L2(G)) is a
unitary representation, called the left regular representation of G.
The right regular representation of G is the unitary representation ρG on
L2(G) defined by

ρG(g)ξ(x) = ∆G(g)1/2(ξg)(x) = ∆G(g)1/2ξ(xg),
ξ ∈L2(G), g, x ∈G,

the constant factor ∆G(g)1/2 being introduced in order to insure the unitarity
of ρG(g).

A.5. COMPACT GROUPS

Proposition A.4.1 The regular representations λG and ρG are equivalent.

Proof
Let T : L2(G) →L2(G) be defined by

Tξ(x) = ∆G(x)−1/2ξ(x−1),
ξ ∈L2(G).

By Lemma A.3.4 above, T is a unitary operator. It is clear that TλG(g) =
ρG(g)T, for all g in G. ■

Combining the representations λG and ρG, we obtain a unitary represen-
tation π of the product G × G on L2(G), defined by

π(g1, g2)ξ(x) = λG(g1)ρG(g2)ξ(x)

= ∆G(g2)1/2ξ(g−1
1 xg2),
ξ ∈L2(G), x ∈G,

and called the
left-right regular representation of G × G. The restriction
of π to the diagonal subgroup {(g, g) :
g ∈G} ∼= G is the conjugation
representation γG in L2(G) :

γG(g)ξ(x) = ∆G(g)1/2ξ(g−1xg),
ξ ∈L2(G), g, x ∈G.

A.5
Representations of compact groups

Let G be a compact group.
Then its Haar measure dg is finite, that is,

the unit representation 1G is contained in the regular representation λG. Each
of these properties characterises compact groups:

Proposition A.5.1 For a locally compact group G, the following properties
are equivalent:

(i) G is compact;

(ii) 1G is contained in λG;

(iii) the Haar measure on G is finite.

Proof

The central result in the “abstract” theory of unitary representations of
compact groups is Peter-Weyl’s Theorem (see [Robet–83, Chapter 5]). For
the definition of the unitary dual �G of G, see A.1.7.

Theorem A.5.2 (Peter-Weyl) Let G be a compact group.

(i) Every unitary representation of G is the direct sum of irreducible sub-
representations.

(ii) Every irreducible unitary representation of G is finite dimensional.

(iii) Every irreducible unitary representation of G is contained in the regular
representation λG of G. More precisely, λG is the direct sum �

π∈bG σπ
of subrepresentations σπ, where each σπ is equivalent to (dim π)π.

A.6
Unitary representations associated to group
actions

The regular representation is an example of more general representations
associated to group actions on measure spaces.
A measure space (Ω, B, µ) is a set Ωequipped with a σ-algebra B and a
positive measure µ : B →R.

A.6. REPRESENTATIONS ASSOCIATED TO GROUP ACTIONS
325

Let G be a topological group. A group action of G on the measure space
Ωis measurable if the mapping

G × Ω→Ω
(g, x) �→gx

is measurable, when G is equipped with the σ-algebra of its Borel subsets.
Given such an action, we define for each g ∈G a measure gµ on Ωby
gµ(A) = µ(g−1A), where A is a measurable subset of Ω.
The measure µ is invariant if gµ = µ for all g ∈G. We say that µ is
quasi-invariant if µ and gµ are equivalent measures for all g ∈G. Recall that
two measures µ1 and µ2 on Ωare equivalent if, for every measurable subset
A of Ω, we have µ1(A) = 0 if and only if µ2(A) = 0.
Consider a measurable group action of G on Ωsuch that µ is quasi-
invariant. Assume either that µ is σ-finite (that is, Ωis a countable union of
measurable subsets of finite measure) or that Ωis a locally compact space,
that G acts continuously on Ω, and that µ is a regular Borel measure. Then

there exists a non-negative measurable function dgµ

Proposition A.6.1 Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group, let (Ω, µ)
be a σ-finite measurable space such that L2(Ω, µ) is separable, and let G×Ω→
Ωbe a measurable action such that µ is quasi-invariant.
Then the homomorphism πµ defined above is a unitary representation of
G on L2(Ω, µ).

Proof
Since G is σ-compact, for all f1, f2 ∈L2(Ω, µ), the matrix coefficient

g �→⟨πµ(g)f1, f2⟩=
�

is a measurable function on G, by Fubini’s theorem. The strong continuity
of πµ follows from the next lemma. ■

Lemma A.6.2 Let G be a locally compact group, and let H be a separable
Hilbert space. Let π : G →U(H) be a homomorphism from G to the unitary
group of H. Assume that the function g �→⟨π(g)ξ, ξ⟩is measurable for all
ξ ∈H. Then π is strongly continuous.

Proof
Let ξ be a vector in H. It suffices to show that g �→π(g)ξ is
continuous at the unit element e.
Choose ε > 0 and set A = {g ∈G :
∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥< ε/2}. Then A is
measurable, since

A = {g ∈G : 2Re⟨π(g)ξ, ξ⟩> 2∥ξ∥2 −ε2/4}.

Moreover, A = A−1 and

A2 = AA−1 ⊂{g ∈G : ∥π(g)ξ −ξ∥< ε},

since ∥π(g1g2)ξ −ξ∥≤∥π(g1)ξ −ξ∥+ ∥π(g2)ξ −ξ∥for all g1, g2 ∈G.
The subset π(G)ξ = {π(g)ξ : g ∈G} is separable, since H is separable.
Hence, there exists a sequence (gn)n in G such that (π(gn)ξ)n is dense in
π(G)ξ. For any g ∈G, we have

∥π(g−1
n g)ξ −ξ∥= ∥π(g)ξ −π(gn)ξ∥< ε/2

for some n. Therefore, G = �

n gnA and the Haar measure of A is non-zero.
It follows that A2 = AA−1 is a neighbourhood of e (Exercise C.6.11) and this
proves the claim. ■

A.6. REPRESENTATIONS ASSOCIATED TO GROUP ACTIONS
327

Remark A.6.3 (i) The Radon-Nikodym theorem is not valid for a general
measure space Ω: for instance, consider Ω= R with the σ-algebra of its
Borel subsets and the counting measure µ. The Lebesgue measure λ on R
is obviously absolutely continuous with respect to µ (in the sense that every
µ-null set is a λ-null set), but there exists no measurable function f such
that λ = fµ. For an extensive discussion of the Radon-Nikodym theorem,
see [Halmo–74, Section 31].
(ii) The separability condition on H in the previous lemma is necessary:
let G = Rd denote the group R equipped with the discrete topology, and
let π be the regular representation of G on ℓ2(Rd). Then x �→⟨π(x)ξ, ξ⟩is a
measurable function on G for all ξ ∈ℓ2(Rd), but π is not strongly continuous;
for more details, see Exercise A.8.4 and [HewRo–63, (22.20),(c)].
(iii) Let (Ω, B, µ) be a measure space.
The Hilbert space L2(Ω, µ) is
separable when B is generated as σ-algebra by a countable family of subsets.
More generally, say that two subsets A, B ∈B are equivalent if µ(A△B) = 0,
where
A△B = (A \ B) ∪(B \ A)

denotes the symmetric difference.
The set S(µ) of equivalence classes of
elements in B of finite measure is a metric space for the distance d(A, B) =
µ(A△B). Then L2(Ω, µ) is separable if and only if S(µ) is separable; see
[Halmo–74, Section 42, Exercise 1].

Example A.6.4 (i) Interesting geometrical examples arise when Ωis an
oriented manifold and G is a topological group acting by diffeomorphisms
on Ω. Let v be a volume form on Ω. For g in G, let g∗v be the volume form
which is the pull-back of v by the diffeomorphism g. Then we have

g∗v(ω) = c(g−1, ω)v(ω)
for all
ω ∈Ω,

for some continuous function c : G × Ω→R∗
+. Thus, the regular Borel
measure µv on Ωassociated to v is quasi-invariant under G.

(ii) As an example for the situation described in (i), the group G =
SL2(R) acts by fractional linear transformations on the real projective line
R ∪{∞}. The Lebesgue µ measure on R ∪{∞} is quasi-invariant (and not
invariant) under the action of G. The associated unitary representation πµ is
the representation of SL2(R) on L2(R ∪{∞}) given by

A.7
Group actions associated to orthogonal
representations

Let G be a second countable locally compact group with a measure preserving
action on a probability space (Ω, µ). The space L2
R(Ω, µ) of all real-valued
square-integrable functions on Ω, modulo equality µ-almost everywhere, is
a real Hilbert space. As in Section A.6, we can define, for each g ∈G, an
orthogonal operator πµ(g) on L2
R(Ω, µ). Assuming that L2
R(Ω, µ) is separable,
the mapping g �→πµ(g) is an orthogonal representation of G in the sense of
the definition below. The aim of this section is to show that every orthogonal
representation of G occurs as subrepresentation of a representation of the
form (πµ, L2
R(Ω, µ)) for some probability space (Ω, µ).

A.7. GROUP ACTIONS ASSOCIATED TO REPRESENTATIONS
329

Definition A.7.1 An orthogonal representation of a topological group G in
a real Hilbert space H is a strongly continuous group homomorphism from
G to the orthogonal group O(H) of H.
The notions of orthogonal equivalence, subrepresentation, direct sum, and
tensor product of orthogonal representations are defined in the same way as
for unitary representations.

Remark A.7.2 To every orthogonal representation (π, H) of the topological
group G is canonically associated a unitary representation πC of G, called
the complexification of π; it acts on the complexification HC = H ⊗R C of
H by
πC(g)(ξ ⊗λ) = π(g)ξ ⊗λ,
g ∈G, ξ ∈H, λ ∈C.

Let us recall some standard notions from probability theory (see, for
instance, [Loeve–77]). Let (Ω, B, µ) be a probability space.

Definition A.7.3 A real-valued random variable X on Ωis a measurable
function from Ωto the real line R; two random variables are identified if
they are equal µ-almost everywhere.

µX on R which is the image of µ under X. If X is either integrable on Ωor
positive-valued, its expectation or mean value E[X] is defined by

Remark A.7.7 Every real Hilbert space is isometrically isomorphic to a
Gaussian Hilbert space. Indeed, as the previous example shows, Gaussian
Hilbert spaces of arbitrary dimensions exist.

Lemma A.7.8 Let K be a Gaussian Hilbert space in L2

Given an isomorphism Φ between a real Hilbert space H and an appro-
priate Hilbert space of Gaussian variables on a probability space (Ω, B, µ),
we will extend Φ to an isomorphism between S(H) and L2
R(Ω, µ), with the
following property: every orthogonal operator on H induces a measure pre-
serving transformation of (Ω, µ).
By a measure preserving transformation of (Ω, µ), we mean a measurable
bijective mapping θ : Ω→Ωsuch that θ∗(µ) = µ and such that θ−1 is

APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

measurable. Observe that any such transformation gives rise to an orthogonal
operator
θ∗: L2
R(Ω, µ) →L2
R(Ω, µ),
X �→X ◦θ−1,

with the property θ∗(Y Z) = θ∗(Y )θ∗(Z) for every pair (Y, Z) of characteristic
functions of measurable subsets. The next lemma shows that the converse is
true if Ωis a standard Borel space.
Let �B be the quotient space of B by the equivalence relation B1 ∼B2 if
µ(B1△B2) = 0. Then �B is in a natural way a Boolean σ-algebra under the
operations of complementation, countable unions and intersections, and the
measure µ induces a measure �µ on �B defined by �µ( �B) = µ(B) if �B is the
equivalence class of B ∈B. The pair ( �B, �µ) is called the measure algebra of
(Ω, B, µ). An automorphism of ( �B, �µ) is a bijection �B →�B which preserves
complements, countable unions, and the measure �µ. A measure preserving
transformation of Ωinduces in a natural way an automorphism of ( �B, �µ).
For the notion of a standard Borel space, see Definition F.5.2.

Lemma A.7.11 Let (Ω, B) be a standard Borel space equipped with a non-
atomic probability measure µ. Let V : L2(Ω) →L2(Ω) an orthogonal lin-
ear bijective mapping. Assume that V (Y Z) = V (Y )V (Z) and V −1(Y Z) =
V −1(Y )V −1(Z) for every pair (Y, Z) of characteristic functions of measurable
subsets.
Then there exists a measure preserving transformation θ of Ωsuch that
V = θ∗.

Proof
Let B ∈B. Then, by assumption, we have V (χB)2 = V (χ2
B) =
V (χB). Hence, there exists B′ ∈B such that V (χB) = χB′. This defines a
mapping
λ : �B →�B,
�B �→�B′,

where �B is the class of B in the measure algebra ( �B, �µ). Using the same
procedure for V −1 and the fact that V is orthogonal, it is straightforward to
check (see the proof of Theorem 2.4 in [Walte–82]) that λ is an automorphism
of ( �B, �µ).
Since Ωis a standard Borel space and µ is non-atomic, we can assume
without loss of generality that Ω= [0, 1] and µ is the Lebesgue measure (see
[Sriva–98, Theorem 3.4.2.3]). It is known that then λ is induced by a measure
preserving transformation θ of Ω(see [vNeum–32, Satz 1]). We clearly have
V = θ∗. ■

Remark A.7.14 If (π, H) is an orthogonal representation of G, then, for
every n ≥0, the subspace Sn(H) is a closed G-invariant subspace of H⊗n

and defines a subrepresentation of π⊗n, which is called the n-th symmetric
tensor power of π and which we denote by Sn(π).

Corollary A.7.15 Let G be a second countable locally compact group, and
let π be an orthogonal representation of G in a separable real Hilbert space
H. There exist a probability space (Ω, µ) and a measure preserving action of
G on Ωsuch that the associated orthogonal representation of G on L2
R(Ω, µ)
is equivalent to the direct sum �∞
n=0 Sn(π) of all symmetric tensor powers of
π.

Proof
Let Φ be an isometric isomorphism between H and a Gaussian
Hilbert space K ⊂L2
R(Ω, µ). As Example A.7.6 shows, we can assume that
Ωis a standard Borel space and that µ is non-atomic; we can also assume
that the σ-algebra generated by all X ∈K coincides with the σ-algebra of
all measurable subsets of Ω.
Let �Φ : S(H) →L2
R(Ω, µ) be the extension of Φ as in the previous
theorem. For g ∈G, the orthogonal operator π(g) on H induces a measure
preserving mapping θg : Ω→Ω. Since π is a representation of G, we have
for every g, h ∈G,

θg(θh(ω)) = θgh(ω)
for almost all
ω ∈Ω,
θg−1(θg(ω)) = ω
for almost all
ω ∈Ω.

It follows that there exists a measurable subset Ω0 of Ωwith measure 1,
which is θg-invariant for every g ∈G and such that the mapping

(g, ω) �→θg(ω)

APPENDIX A. UNITARY GROUP REPRESENTATIONS

Appendix B

Measures on homogeneous
spaces

Let G be a topological group, and let H be a closed subgroup of G. In
the present chapter, we recall how, in case G is locally compact, G/H has
also a measure which is quasi-invariant by G, and canonically defined up to
appropriate equivalence. It follows that any closed subgroup provides the
corresponding quasi-regular representation of G, which is a basic example of
unitary representation. More generally, quasi-invariant measures enter the
definition of induced representations: see Chapter E.
We discuss the existence of invariant measures on G/H in Section B.1.
The important case where H is a lattice is treated in Section B.2.

B.1
Invariant measures

For the quotient topology on the homogeneous space G/H, the canonical
projection p : G →G/H is continuous and open.

Lemma B.1.1 Let Q be a compact subset of G/H. Then there exists a com-
pact subset K of G with p(K) = Q.

Proof
Let U be a compact neighbourhood of e in G. As Q is compact and
p(xU) is a neighbourhood of xH for each x in G, there exists x1, . . . xn in G
such that Q ⊂�n
i=1 p(xiU). The set

K = p−1(Q) ∩

n�

xiU

is compact and p(K) = Q. ■

THψ > 0 on a neighbourhood of Q = supp ϕ, its support is compact, and
THf = ϕ. ■

Proposition B.1.5 Let µ be a quasi-invariant regular Borel measure on
G/H as above. Then the support of µ is G/H.

Proof
The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition A.3.2.
Indeed,
assume that µ(U) = 0 for some open non-empty subset U of G/H. Then
µ(gU) = 0 for all g ∈G, by quasi-invariance of µ. For any compact subset
K of G/H, there exists g1, . . . , gn ∈G such that K ⊂�n
i=1 giU. Hence,
µ(K) = 0 for every compact subset K of G/H. Since µ is regular, this
implies that µ = 0, a contradiction. ■

Under suitable conditions, there exist relatively invariant measures on
homogeneous spaces. A measure µ on G/H is said to be relatively invariant
if, for every g ∈G, there exists a constant χ(g) > 0 such that g−1µ = χ(g)µ,

that is, the Radon-Nikodym derivative dgµ

dµ is a constant, possily depending

on g. It is clear that χ is then a continuous homomorphism from G to R∗
+,
called the character of µ. Relatively invariant measures are also called semi-
invariant measures (see [Raghu–72, page 18]).

Proposition B.1.6 (i) Assume that there exists a relatively invariant regu-

Corollary B.1.8 Let H be a unimodular subgroup of the locally compact
group G.

(i) Then G/H has a relatively invariant regular measure µ, with character
(∆G)−1.

(ii) If µ is finite (this happens, for instance, if G/H is compact), then µ is
invariant, and G is unimodular.

Proof
(i) This is a particular case of Proposition B.1.6.
(ii) Assume that µ(G/H) < ∞. For g ∈G, we have

µ(G/H) = µ(gG/H) = g−1µ(G/H) = (∆G(g))µ(G/H).

and therefore ∆G(g) = 1. ■

Definition B.1.9 Let H be a closed subgroup of the locally compact group
G. Let ρ be a rho-function for (G, H) and let µ be the corresponding quasi-
invariant measure on G/H. The unitary representation λG/H of G defined on
L2(G/H) = L2(G/H, µ) by

λG/H(g)ξ(x) =
�ρ(g−1x)
