�1/2
ξ(g−1x),
g, x ∈G, ξ ∈L2(G/H)

is called the quasi-regular representation of G associated to H.

B.1. INVARIANT MEASURES
351

Remark B.1.10 (i) The quasi-regular representation λG/H coincides with
the representation πµ defined in Section A.6.
(ii) If H is normal in G, then λG/H coincides with the lift to G of the left
regular representation of the quotient group.

Example B.1.11 (i) Let G = SL2(R) and P the subgroup of upper trian-
gular matrices. Then G is unimodular, but P is not (Example A.3.5). Thus,
we recover the fact, mentioned at the end of Section A.6, that G/P has no
invariant measure.
Moreover, G has no character different from 1G, since [G, G] = G. Hence,
there is no relatively invariant measure on G/P.

(ii) Let K be a compact subgroup of the locally compact group G. Then
G/K has an invariant Borel measure.
Indeed, ∆G(K), being a compact
subgroup of R∗
+, is trivial. For the same reason, K is unimodular.
The fact that G/K has an invariant measure can also be seen by the
following argument. The projection p : G →G/K is proper (that is, p−1(Q)
is compact for every compact subset Q of G/K).
For any regular Borel
measure µ on G, the image p∗(µ), defined by p∗(µ)(A) = µ(p−1(A)) for Borel
subsets A in G/K, is a regular Borel measure on G/K. It is clear that p∗(µ)
is invariant if µ is a left Haar measure on G.

B.2
Lattices in locally compact groups

Let G be a locally compact group.

Definition B.2.1 A lattice in G is a discrete subgroup Γ of G such that
G/Γ carries a finite invariant regular Borel measure. Observe that such a
measure is then unique up to a constant factor, by Corollary B.1.7.

As discrete groups are unimodular, the following proposition is an imme-
diate consequence of Corollary B.1.8.

Proposition B.2.2 Let Γ be a discrete subgroup of G.
(i) If Γ is cocompact (that is, if G/Γ is compact), then Γ is a lattice.
(ii) If Γ is a lattice, then G is unimodular.

A more direct argument for (ii) is as follows. Since Γ is unimodular, it is
contained in the normal subgroup Ker ∆G of G. Hence, the Haar measure of
the locally compact group G/Ker ∆G is finite. This implies that G/Ker ∆G
is compact (see Proposition A.5.1). Hence, G/Ker ∆G is topologically iso-
morphic to a compact subgroup of R∗
+. It follows that G = Ker ∆G.

B.2. LATTICES
353

The following proposition is often used to show that a discrete subgroup
is a lattice.

Proposition B.2.4 Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group and let Γ
be a discrete subgroup of G.

(i) There exists a Borel fundamental domain for Γ.

(ii) If Γ is a lattice in G, then every Borel fundamental domain for Γ has
finite Haar measure.

G.

fact (see, e.g., [Bore–69b, 1.11 Lemme]) is due to H. Minkowski. The homo-
geneous space G/Γ – which can be identified in a natural way with the set
of unimodular lattices in Rn – is not compact.

Appendix C

Functions of positive type and
GNS construction

Two of the most important notions for unitary group representations are
functions of positive type and induced representations, defined respectively
in the present chapter and in Chapter E.
We first discuss two kinds of kernels on a topological space X : those of
positive type and those conditionally of negative type; the crucial difference
is the presence or not of “conditionally”, whereas the difference between
“positive” and “negative” is only a matter of sign convention.
For each
of these two types of kernels, there is a so-called GNS construction (for
Gelfand, Naimark, and Segal) which shows how kernels are simply related
to appropriate mappings of X to Hilbert spaces. Moreover, a theorem of
Schoenberg establishes a relation between the two types of kernels.
A function ϕ on a topological group G is of positive type if the kernel
(g, h) �→ϕ(g−1h) is of positive type. Functions of positive type provide an
efficient tool to prove some basic general results, such as Gelfand-Raikov’s
Theorem according to which a locally compact group has sufficiently many
irreducible unitary representations to separate its points.

C.1
Kernels of positive type

Let X be a topological space.

Definition C.1.1 A kernel of positive type on X is a continuous function
Φ : X × X →C such that, for any n in N, any elements x1, . . . , xn in X and

Theorem C.1.4 (GNS Construction) Let Φ be a kernel of positive type
on the topological space X. Then there exists a Hilbert space H and a contin-
uous mapping f : X →H with the following properties:

C.1. KERNELS OF POSITIVE TYPE

(i) Φ(x, y) = ⟨f(x), f(y)⟩for all x, y in X;

(ii) the linear span of {f(x) : x ∈X} is dense in H.

Moreover, the pair (H, f) is unique, up to canonical isomorphism, that is,
if (K, g) is another pair satisfying (i) and (ii), then there exists a unique
Hilbert space isomorphism T : H →K such that g = T ◦f.

Remark C.1.5 (i) With the above notation, it follows from the continuity
of Φ that Φ is locally bounded, that is, for every x ∈X, there exists a neigh-
bourhood Ux of x and a constant c > 0 such that Φ(y, y) ≤c for all y in Ux.
This shows that the associated Hilbert space H can be realized as a space of
continuous functions on X. Indeed, the above inequality (∗) shows that, if
(ϕn)n is a Cauchy sequence in V, then (ϕn)n converges locally uniformly on
X.

We state for later use some permanence properties of kernels of positive
type.

Proposition C.1.6 Let Φ and Ψ be kernels of positive type on X. Then the
following are kernels of positive type:

(i) Φ : (x, y) �→Φ(x, y),

(ii) tΦ : (x, y) �→tΦ(x, y), for all t ≥0,

(iii) Φ + Ψ : (x, y) �→Φ(x, y) + Ψ(x, y),

(iv) ΦΨ : (x, y) �→Φ(x, y)Ψ(x, y).

(v) Let (Φt)t be a family of kernels of positive type on X converging point-
wise on X × X to a continuous kernel Φ : X × X →C. Then Φ is a
kernel of positive type.

Proof
Assertions (i), (ii), (iii) and (v) are obvious from the definition.
As to (iv), let f : X →H and g : X →K be the mappings given by
Theorem C.1.4. Let H ⊗K be the Hilbert space tensor product, and let

h : X →H ⊗K,
x �→f(x) ⊗g(x).

Then
(ΦΨ)(x, y) = ⟨h(x), h(y)⟩,

for all x, y ∈X. ■

In other words, the set of kernels of positive type is a convex cone in the
vector space of all continuous kernels on X which is closed under complex
conjugation, under pointwise product, and under the topology of pointwise
convergence.

The following lemma is used in the proof of Theorem 2.11.3.

Lemma C.1.8 Let Φ be a kernel of positive type on a set X such that
|Φ(x, y)| < 1 for all x, y ∈X. Then the kernel

We state some elementary facts about kernels conditionally of negative
type; we leave the proof as Exercise C.6.9.

Proposition C.2.4 Let X be a topological space.

(i) The set of all kernels conditionally of negative type on X is a convex
cone, that is, if Ψ1 and Ψ2 are kernels conditionally of negative type,
then so is sΨ1 + tΨ2 for all real numbers s, t ≥0.

(ii) Let (Ψt)t be a family of kernels conditionally of negative type on X
converging pointwise on X ×X to a continuous kernel Ψ : X ×X →R.
Then Ψ is a kernel conditionally of negative type.

(iii) Let Φ be a real valued kernel of positive type on X. Then

(x, y) �→Φ(x, x) −Φ(x, y)

is a kernel conditionally of negative type.

Φ(x, y) = Ψ(x, x0) + Ψ(y, x0) −Ψ(x, y).

A more important connection between kernels conditionally of negative

Theorem C.3.2 (Schoenberg) Let X be a topological space, and let Ψ :
X×X →R be a continuous kernel on X such that Ψ(x, x) = 0 and Ψ(y, x) =
Ψ(x, y) for all x, y ∈X. The following properties are equivalent:

Examples of functions of positive type arise from diagonal matrix coeffi-

The functions of the type ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩are said to be the functions of positive
type associated to π.

So,
{f ∗�g : f, g ∈L2(G)} = {f ∗ˇg : f, g ∈L2(G)}

is the set of all matrix coefficients of the regular representation of G.

Recall that a continuous function f on a topological space X is said to
vanish at infinity if, for all ε > 0, the set {x ∈X : |f(x)| ≥ε} is compact.
The space of all such functions is denoted by C0(X). Observe that C0(X) is
closed in C(X) in the topology of uniform convergence on X.

Proposition C.4.6 Every matrix coefficient of the regular representation of
a locally compact group G vanishes at infinity.

Proof
Let f ∗ˇg be a coefficient of the regular representation of G, where
f, g ∈L2(G). Since Cc(G) is dense in L2(G), there exists fn, gn ∈Cc(G) with
limn fn = f and limn gn = g in L2(G). Then limn fn ∗ˇgn = f ∗ˇg, uniformly
on G. Hence, to show that f ∗ˇg belongs to C0(G), we can, by the observation
above, assume that f, g ∈Cc(G). If K = supp f and L = supp g, the support
of f ∗ˇg is contained in KL−1. Thus, f ∗ˇg ∈Cc(G). ■

The following corollary generalises the implication “(ii) =⇒(i)” in Propo-
sition A.5.1.

Corollary C.4.7 Let G be a non-compact locally compact group. Then λG
has no finite dimensional subrepresentation.

Proof
Let (π, H) be a finite dimensional unitary representation of G. Fix
an orthonormal basis (vi)i of (π, H). By the previous proposition, it suffices to
show that some matrix coefficient ⟨π(·)vi, vj⟩of π does not vanish at infinity.
For this, observe that (⟨π(g)vi, vj⟩)i,j, which is the matrix of π(g) with respect
to the basis (vi)i, is a unitary matrix. Hence, for any i, we have
�

j
|⟨π(g)vi, vj⟩|2 = 1,
for all
g ∈G,

from which the claim follows. ■

The above corollary shows in particular that the regular representation
of a non-compact locally compact abelian group has no irreducible subrepre-
sentation. It follows that the regular representation of such a group cannot
be decomposed as a direct sum of irreducible subrepresentations. However,

376
APPENDIX C. FUNCTIONS OF POSITIVE TYPE

it can be decomposed as a direct integral of irreducible representations, that
is, of unitary characters (see F.5.4).
Using the GNS-construction of Proposition C.1.4, we will show that ev-
ery function of positive type on G is the diagonal matrix coefficient of an
essentially unique cyclic unitary representation of G.

Definition C.4.8 A unitary representation (π, H) of G is cyclic if there
exists a vector ξ in H such that the linear span of π(G)ξ = {π(g)ξ : g ∈G}
is dense in H. In this case, ξ is said to be a cyclic vector for π.

Observe that a unitary representation (π, H) of G is irreducible if and
only if every non-zero vector ξ in H is cyclic for π.
As the next proposition shows, an arbitrary unitary representation can
always be decomposed as direct sum of cyclic representations.

Proposition C.4.9 Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of the topological
group G. Then H can be decomposed as a direct sum H = �

i Hi of mutually
orthogonal, closed, and invariant subspaces Hi, such that the restriction of π
to Hi is cyclic for every i.

Proof
Let X be the set of all families (Hi)i of mutually orthogonal, closed,
and invariant subspaces Hi of H, such that the restriction of π to Hi is cyclic.
We equip X with the ordering given by set inclusion. By Zorn’s lemma,
X contains a maximal family (Hi)i. We claim that H = �

i Hi. Indeed,
otherwise, there exists a non-zero vector ξ which is orthogonal to all Hi’s.
Let K be the closure of the linear span of π(G)ξ. Then K is orthogonal to all
Hi’s. Hence, the family {(Hi)i, K} is in X and strictly contains (Hi)i. This
is a contradiction. ■

Theorem C.4.10 (GNS Construction) Let ϕ be a function of positive
type on the topological group G. Then there exists a triple (πϕ, Hϕ, ξϕ) con-
sisting of a cyclic unitary representation πϕ in a Hilbert space Hϕ and a
cyclic vector ξϕ in Hϕ such that

ϕ(g) = ⟨πϕ(g)ξϕ, ξϕ⟩,
g ∈G.

Moreover, this triple is unique in the following sense: if (π, H, ξ) is another
such triple, then there exists a Hilbert space isomorphism T : Hϕ →H
intertwining πϕ and π, and such that Tξϕ = ξ.

C.4. FUNCTIONS ON GROUPS
377

Proof
Let Φ be the kernel of positive type on G associated to ϕ. By
Theorem C.1.4, there exists a Hilbert space Hϕ and a continuous mapping
f : G →Hϕ such that

⟨f(x), f(y)⟩= Φ(x, y) = ϕ(y−1x),
for all
x, y ∈G.

For every g ∈G, we have

⟨f(gx), f(gy)⟩= Φ(gx, gy) = Φ(x, y).

Hence, by the uniqueness result in Theorem C.1.4, there exists a unitary
operator πϕ(g) on Hϕ such that πϕ(g)f(x) = f(gx) for all x in G. By density
of the linear span of {f(x) : x ∈G}, we have

πϕ(g1g2) = πϕ(g1)πϕ(g2)

for all g1, g2 in G. Moreover, since f is continuous, g �→πϕ(g)ξ is continuous
for every ξ in Hϕ. Thus, πϕ is a unitary representation of G. Set ξϕ = f(e).
Then ξϕ is a cyclic vector for Hϕ and

ϕ(g) = Φ(g, e) = ⟨f(g), f(e)⟩= ⟨πϕ(g)ξϕ, ξϕ⟩

for all g ∈G.
If (π, H, ξ) is a triple with the same properties as (πϕ, Hϕ, ξϕ), then

Φ(g1, g2) = ⟨π(g1)ξ, π(g2)ξ⟩,
for all
g1, g2 ∈G.

By the uniqueness result in Theorem C.1.4 again, there exists an isomorphism
T : Hϕ →H such that Tπϕ(g)ξϕ = π(g)ξ for all g ∈G. In particular, Tξϕ = ξ
and T intertwines the representations πϕ and π, since ξϕ is cyclic. ■

Definition C.4.11 The triple (πϕ, Hϕ, ξϕ) as above is called the GNS-triple
associated to the function of positive type ϕ on G.

Example C.4.12 Let G be a discrete group, and let ϕ = δe be the Dirac
function at e. Then ϕ is a function of positive type on G (Exercise C.6.7)
and the GNS-representation associated to ϕ is equivalent to the regular rep-
resentation of G (Exercise C.6.8).

378
APPENDIX C. FUNCTIONS OF POSITIVE TYPE

Remark C.4.13 The family of equivalence classes of all cyclic unitary rep-
resentations of G is a set. Indeed, let (π, H) be such a representation, with
cyclic vector ξ. Then, by Theorem C.4.10, π is unitary equivalent to πϕ,
where ϕ = ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩. Thus, the family of equivalence classes of cyclic repre-
sentations of G can be parametrized by a subset of the set of all functions of
positive type on G.
In particular, the unitary dual �G of G is indeed a set.

Remark C.4.14 Here is another proof of Inequality (iii) in Proposition C.4.2.
Let ϕ be a function of positive type on a topological group G, with ϕ(e) = 1.
By Theorem C.4.10, there exists a unitary representation (π, H) of G and a
unit vector ξ ∈H such that ϕ(x) = ⟨π(x)ξ, ξ⟩for all x ∈G. Then

|ϕ(x) −ϕ(y)|2 = |⟨π(x)ξ −π(y)ξ, ξ⟩|2

≤∥π(x)ξ −π(y)ξ∥2 = 2(1 −Reϕ(y−1x))

for all x, y ∈G.

Corollary C.4.15 Let (π, H) be a unitary representation of G and ξ ∈H.
Let ϕ be the function of positive type defined by ϕ(g) = ⟨π(g)ξ, ξ⟩. Then πϕ
is contained in π.

Proof
The uniqueness assertion of Theorem C.4.10 implies that πϕ is
equivalent to the subrepresentation of π defined on the closure of the linear
span of π(G)ξ. ■

Corollary C.4.16 Let ϕ and ϕ′ be continuous functions of positive type on
G, and let ψ = ϕϕ′. Then πψ is contained in πϕ ⊗πϕ′.

Proof
Since

ψ(g) = ⟨(π1 ⊗π2)(g)(ξϕ ⊗ξϕ′), (ξϕ ⊗ξϕ′)⟩,

the claim follows from the previous corollary. ■

Observe that, in general, πψ and πϕ ⊗πϕ′ are not unitarily equivalent
(Exercise C.6.8).

C.5. THE CONE OF FUNCTIONS OF POSITIVE TYPE
379

Functions conditionally of negative type

Let G be a topological group.

Definition C.4.17 A continuous function ψ : G →R is
conditionally of
negative type if the kernel Ψ on G, defined by Ψ(g, h) = ψ(h−1g) for g, h in
G, is conditionally of negative type.

Remark C.4.18 Let G be a topological group acting continuously on the
topological space X. Let Ψ be a kernel conditionally of negative type on X
which is G-invariant, that is, Ψ(gx, gy) = Ψ(x, y) for all g ∈G and x, y ∈X.
For fixed x0 ∈X, the function ψ : G →R defined by ψ(g) = Ψ(gx0, x0) is a
function conditionally of negative type on G. For examples, see Sections 2.10
and 2.11.

Specializing to functions conditionally of negative type on a group, The-
orem C.3.2 takes the following form.

Corollary C.4.19 (Schoenberg) Let G be a topological group, and let ψ :
G →R be a continuous function with ψ(e) = 0 and ψ(g−1) = ψ(g) for all
g ∈G. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) ψ is conditionally of negative type;

(ii) the function exp(−tψ) is of positive type, for every t ≥0.

C.5
The cone of functions of positive type

Let P(G) denote the set of all functions of positive type on the topological
group G. It follows from Proposition C.1.6 that P(G) is a convex cone, closed
under complex conjugation and pointwise product.
We will also consider the convex set P1(G) of all ϕ in P(G) normalised
by the Condition ϕ(e) = 1. We continue to use the notation πϕ, Hϕ of The-

Let ext(P1(G)) be the set of extreme points of the convex set P1(G). Thus,
ϕ ∈P1(G) lies in ext(P1(G)) if and only if, whenever ϕ = tϕ1 + (1 −t)ϕ2 for

in ext(P1(G)) are called pure functions of positive type.

Recall that, by Proposition C.4.2, every ϕ in P(G) is bounded and that
∥ϕ∥∞= ϕ(e).
Assume from now on that G is locally compact, so that we can consider
P(G) as a subset of L∞(G) = L∞(G, µ), for a Haar measure µ on G. Due to
the fact that G is not necessarily σ-finite, the definition of L∞(G) has to be
phrased as follows:
A subset A of G is locally Borel if A ∩B is a Borel set for every Borel set
B with µ(B) < ∞. Such a set A is locally null if µ(A∩B) = 0 for every Borel
set B with µ(B) < ∞. A function f : G �→C is locally measurable if f −1(E)

382
APPENDIX C. FUNCTIONS OF POSITIVE TYPE

is locally Borel for all Borel subsets E of C. A property is said to hold locally
almost everywhere on G, if it is satisfied outside a locally null set. Then
L∞(G) is defined to be the space of all locally measurable functions which
are bounded locally almost everywhere, two such functions being identified
if they agree locally almost everywhere.
With this definition, L∞(G) can be identified with the (topological) dual
space of L1(G) by means of the formula

⟨ϕ, f⟩=
�

G
f(x)ϕ(x)dx,
ϕ ∈L∞(G), f ∈L1(G)

(see [Bou–Int1, Chapter 5, Section 5, No 8]). Equipped with convolution and
with the involution

f ∗(x) = ∆G(x−1)f(x−1),
f ∈L1(G),

L1(G) is a Banach ∗-algebra.
The following lemma is obvious when G is discrete, and is proved by an
approximation procedure in the general case. The proof can be found in
[Folla–95, (3.35)] or [Dixmi–69, 13.4.4 Proposition]; see also Exercise C.6.1.

Lemma C.5.3 For a function ϕ ∈L∞(G), the following properties are
equivalent:
(i) ϕ agrees locally almost everywhere on G with a continuous function
of positive type;
(ii) ⟨ϕ, f ∗∗f⟩≥0 for all f ∈L1(G).

By Banach-Alaoglu’s Theorem, the unit ball in L∞(G) is compact in the
weak* topology. Let P≤1(G) be the convex set consisting of all ϕ in P(G)
with ∥ϕ∥∞= ϕ(e) ≤1.

Lemma C.5.4 The set P≤1(G) is compact in the weak* topology on L∞(G).

Proof
As P≤1(G) is contained in the unit ball of L∞(G), it suffices to
show that P≤1(G) is weak* closed. This is the case, by Lemma C.5.3. ■

It follows from the previous lemma and from Krein-Milman Theorem
[Rudin–73, 3.21] that the convex hull of ext(P≤1(G)) is weak* dense in
P≤1(G). If G is discrete, P1(G) is compact in the weak* topology (indeed,
the equality ϕ(e) = ⟨ϕ, δe⟩shows that P1(G) is weak* closed in the unit ball
of ℓ∞(G)). This is not true in general for non-discrete groups (see Exercise
C.6.10). Nevertheless, the following result holds for P1(G).

C.5. THE CONE OF FUNCTIONS OF POSITIVE TYPE
383

Theorem C.5.5 Let G be a locally compact group. With the notation as
above, we have:
(i) ext(P≤1(G)) = ext(P1(G)) ∪{0};
(ii) the convex hull of ext(P1(G)) is weak* dense in P1(G).

Proof
(i) • First step: We claim that 0 ∈ext(P≤1(G)). Indeed, let ϕ1, ϕ2 ∈
P≤1(G) and 0 ≤t ≤1 be such that tϕ1 + (1 −t)ϕ2 = 0. Then ϕ1(e) = 0 or
ϕ2(e) = 0 and hence ϕ1 = 0 or ϕ2 = 0.

• Second step: We show that ext(P1(G)) ⊂ext(P≤1(G)). For this, let ϕ ∈
ext(P1(G)), ϕ1, ϕ2 ∈P≤1(G), and 0 ≤t ≤1 be such that tϕ1+(1−t)ϕ2 = ϕ.
Then tϕ1(e) + (1 −t)ϕ2(e) = 1. Hence ϕ1(e) = ϕ2(e) = 1, that is, ϕ1, ϕ2 ∈
P1(G). This implies that ϕ ∈ext(P≤1(G)).

• Third step: We claim that ext(P≤1(G)) ⊂ext(P1(G)) ∪{0}. Indeed, let

ϕ ∈P≤1(G) \ (P1(G) ∪{0}) , and set t = ϕ(e). Then 0 < t < 1 and 1

it follows that ϕ /∈ext(P≤1(G)). This shows that ext(P≤1(G)) is contained
in ext(P1(G)) ∪{0} and finishes the proof of (i).

(ii) Let ϕ ∈P1(G). Since the convex hull of

ext(P≤1(G)) = ext(P1(G)) ∪{0}

is weak* dense in P≤1(G), there exists a net (ψi)i in P≤1(G) converging to ϕ
in the weak* topology, where the ψi’s are functions of the form

Another useful topology on P1(G) and on P≤1(G) is the topology of
uniform convergence on compact subsets of G. It is a remarkable fact, due
to Raikov, that this topology coincides with the weak* topology on P1(G).

Remark C.5.7 Raikov’s Theorem is not true when P1(G) is replaced by
P≤1(G). Indeed, for the circle G = S1, the sequence of characters χn : t �→eint

belongs to P1(G), tends to 0 in the weak* topology (by Riemann-Lebesgue
lemma, see Section D.1), but χn(1) = 1 for all n.

The following proposition is a consequence of C.5.6 and C.5.5.

Proposition C.5.8 Let G be a locally compact group. The convex hull of
ext(P1(G)) is dense in P1(G) for the topology of uniform convergence on
compact subsets of G.

Corollary C.5.9 (Gelfand-Raikov’s Theorem) Let G be a locally com-
pact group. Then, for x and y in G with x ̸= y, there exists an irreducible
unitary representation π of G with π(x) ̸= π(y).

C.6. EXERCISES
387

Proof
Assume, by contradiction, that π(x) = π(y) for all irreducible uni-
tary representations π of G. Then, by Theorem C.5.2, we have ϕ(x) = ϕ(y)
for all ϕ ∈ext(P1(G)). Therefore, ϕ(x) = ϕ(y) for all ϕ ∈P(G), by the pre-
vious proposition. It follows that f1 ∗f2(x) = f1 ∗f2(y) for all f1, f2 ∈Cc(G),
since f1 ∗f2 is a matrix coefficient of the regular representation of G (see
Example C.4.5).
On the other hand, observe that the set {f1 ∗f2
:
f1, f2 ∈Cc(G)} is
dense in Cc(G) for the topology of uniform convergence. Indeed, take f in
Cc(G). For each compact neighbourhood U of e, set fU =
1
|U|χU, where χU
is the characteristic function of U and |U| the Haar measure of U. Then

lim
U→e fU ∗f = f

uniformly on G, by the uniform continuity of f. In particular, there exist
f1, f2 ∈Cc(G) such that f1 ∗f2(x) ̸= f1 ∗f2(y). This is a contradiction. ■

A topological group need not have any unitary representation besides mul-
tiples of the identity representation [Banas–83]. A topological group which
have a faithful unitary representation need not have any irreducible unitary
representation distinct from the identity representation. The following ex-
ample was drawn to our attention by V. Pestov.

Example C.5.10 Consider the Hilbert space L2([0, 1]). The abelian von
Neumann algebra A = L∞([0, 1]) is an algebra of operators on L2([0, 1]),
a function ϕ ∈A being viewed as a multiplication operator ξ �→ϕξ on
L2([0, 1]). Let U denote the unitary group of A, together with the strong
operator topology which makes it a Polish abelian group; elements of U are
measurable functions from [0, 1] to the unit circle S1, up to equality almost
everywhere. The tautological representation of U on L2([0, 1]) is faithful.
Let π be an irreducible unitary representation of U. As U is abelian, π
is one-dimensional, and (u, z) �→π(u)z defines a continuous action of U on
the circle. Now the group U is extremely amenable [Glasn–98], which means
that any continuous action of U on a compact space has a fixed point. In
particular, there exists z ∈S1 such that π(u)z = z for all u ∈U. It follows
that π is the unit representation of U.

Appendix D

Unitary representations of
locally compact abelian groups

Classical harmonic analysis rests on a few general and powerful theorems
concerning the Fourier transform. These are Plancherel’s Theorem on L2-
functions and Pontrjagin’s Duality, recalled without proofs in Section D.1
(overall references are [Bou–Spec] and [Folla–95]), and Bochner’s Theorem
on Fourier transforms of positive measures, stated and proved here in Sec-
tion D.2.
It was Hermann Weyl who realized around 1925 that classical harmonic
analysis is naturally expressed in terms of unitary group representations (clas-
sically, the groups are Rn, Zn, and Tn = Rn/Zn). Then Stone described in
1930 all unitary representations of the additive group R; the generalisation
to arbitrary locally compact abelian groups is due independently to Naimark,
Ambrose, and Godement, and is the so-called SNAG Theorem of Section D.3.
The SNAG Theorem, concerning abelian groups, is necessary in Chapter 1
for our analysis of the unitary representations of groups like SLn(K), where
K is a local field. We recall in Section D.4 a few facts about local fields.
In Sections D.1 to D.3, we denote by G a locally compact abelian group,
with fixed Haar measure dx. The dual group �G of G (see Section A.2) is
contained in the set P1(G) of normalised functions of positive type (see
Section C.5).
More precisely, with appropriate identifications (see Corol-
lary A.2.3, Remark C.4.13, and Theorem C.5.2), we have �G = ext(P1(G)).
The topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets of G makes �G a
topological group, and it is shown in Section D.1 that �G is locally compact.

D.1
The Fourier transform

Let us first recall part of Gelfand’s theory of commutative Banach algebras.
Let A be a (not necessarily unital) Banach algebra. The spectrum σ(x) of an
element x in A is the set of all λ ∈C such that x −λe is not invertible in �A,
the Banach algebra obtained from A by adjunction of a unit e. The spectral
radius r(x) of x is sup{|λ| : λ ∈σ(x)}. By the spectral radius formula , we
have
r(x) = lim
n→∞∥xn∥1/n.

From now on, A will denote a commutative Banach algebra. A character
of A is a non-zero algebra homomorphism from A to C. The set ∆(A) of
all characters of A is a locally compact subspace in the unit ball of A∗, the
topological dual space of A (where A∗is equipped with the weak* topology).
Every element x ∈A defines through

�x : ∆(A) →C,
Φ �→Φ(x)

a continuous function �x on ∆(A), which vanishes at infinity.
The mapping x �→�x, called the Gelfand transform on A, is an algebra
homomorphism from A to C0(∆(A)). For every x ∈A, the uniform norm of
�x coincides with the spectral radius of x, that is,

∥�x∥∞= lim
n→∞∥xn∥1/n.

Let G be a locally compact abelian group. Consider the Banach algebra
L1(G). Every χ ∈�G defines a character Φχ of L1(G) by

The mapping χ �→Φχ is known to be a homeomorphism from �G onto
∆(L1(G)). In particular, the dual group �G is a locally compact abelian group.
Let M(G) denote the Banach ∗-algebra (under convolution and total
variation) of all finite complex regular Borel measures on G, endowed with
the involution dµ∗(x) = dµ(x−1). By Riesz Representation Theorem, M(G)
can be identified with the Banach space dual of C0(G). Observe that L1(G)
can be viewed as subset of M(G).

x /∈B}.

ξ and η in H. Then ϕ is a linear combination of diagonal matrix coefficients

D.4
Local fields

Let K be a field. An absolute value on K is a real-valued function x �→|x|
such that, for all x and y in K :

(i) |x| ≥0 , and |x| = 0 if and only if x = 0;

(ii) |xy| = |x||y|;

(iii) |x + y| ≤|x| + |y|.

An absolute value defines a topology on K given by the metric d(x, y) =
|x −y|.

Definition D.4.1 A field K is a local field if K can be equipped with an
absolute value for which K is locally compact and not discrete.

For a given absolute value, K is locally compact if and only if the ball
U = {x ∈K : |x| ≤1} is compact, and K is not discrete if and only if the
absolute value is non trivial (that is, |x| ̸= 1 for some x ∈K, x ̸= 0).

Example D.4.2 (i) K = R and K = C with the usual absolute value are
local fields.

(ii) Fix a prime p ∈N. For x ∈Q \ {0}, write x = pma/b with a, b ∈
Z \ {0}, m ∈Z, and a, b prime to p. Define |x|p = p−m; set |0|p = 0. Then

x �→|x|p

is a non trivial absolute value on Q, called the p-adic absolute value. The
completion of Q for the corresponding distance

dp(x, y) = |x −y|p,
x, y ∈Q

is the field Qp of p-adic numbers .
The p-adic absolute value on Q extends to an absolute value on Qp. The
subset
Zp = {x ∈Qp : |x|p ≤1} = {x ∈Qp : |x|p < p}

is an open and compact subring of Qp, called the ring of p-adic integers. This
shows that Qp is locally compact, and is a local field.
Observe that the p-adic absolute value is non-archimedean : it satisfies
the following ultrametric strengthening of the triangle inequality:

|x + y|p ≤max{|x|p, |y|p},
x, y ∈Qp.

D.4. LOCAL FIELDS
401

Remark D.4.3 Every non-discrete locally compact topological field is a lo-
cal field. Indeed, let µ be a Haar measure on the additive group (K, +).
It follows from the uniqueness of µ (up to a multiplicative constant) that,
for every x ∈K, x ̸= 0, there exists a real number c(x) > 0 such that
µ(xB) = c(x)µ(B) for every Borel subset B of K (see Exercise A.8.8); we set
c(0) = 0. The function x �→c(x) (respectively, x �→c(x)1/2) is an absolute
value on K if K is not isomorphic to C (respectively, if K is isomorphic to C).
The topology induced by this function coincides with the original topology
of K.
Moreover, a local field field is isomorphic to one of the following fields:

(i) R or C with the usual absolute value,

(ii) a finite extension of the field of p-adic numbers Qp with an extension
of the p-adic absolute value,

(iii) the field K = k((X)) of Laurent series over a finite field k with absolute
value | �∞
i=m aiXi| = e−m with am ∈k \ {0}.

For all this, see [Weil–73] or [Bou–AlCo].

If K is a local field, let us show that the dual group of the additive group
K can be identitified with K, in a non canonical way. For this, we need the
following general elementary fact.

Lemma D.4.4 Let G be a topological group, and let H be a subgroup. As-
sume that H is locally compact, for the induced topology from G. Then H is
closed in G.

Proof
Since H is locally compact, there exists a neighbourhood U0 of e
in G such that U0 ∩H is compact. As G is Hausdorff, U0 ∩H is closed in G.
Fix a neighbourhood V0 of e in G with V −1
0
= V0 and V 2
0 ⊂U0.
Let x be in the closure H of H in G. Then x ∈V0H; so we can choose
v0 ∈V0 such that v0x ∈H. We claim that v0 ∈H and hence that x ∈H.
Indeed, for any neighbourhood W of e in G with W −1 = W and W ⊂V0,
we have x−1 ∈HW, since x−1 ∈H. This implies that

v0 = (v0x)x−1 ∈H(HW) = HW.

So there exists w ∈W such that v0w ∈H. Since V0W ⊂V 2
0 ⊂U0, we have
v0w ∈U0 ∩H. Hence v0 ∈(U0 ∩H)W. As W is arbitrary, v0 is in the closure
of U0 ∩H in G. Since U0 ∩H is closed, v0 ∈H. ■

Proposition D.4.5 Let K be a local field. Let χ be a fixed unitary character
on the additive group of K, distinct from the unit character. For each y ∈K,
let χy ∈�K be defined by χy(x) = χ(yx) for all x in K. The mapping

K →�K,
y �→χy

is a topological group isomorphism.

Proof
The mapping Φ : y �→χy is clearly a continuous injective group
homomorphism. Let H be the image of Φ. Then H is a dense subgroup of
�K. Indeed, otherwise, the quotient group �K/H would be a non trivial locally
compact abelian group. Hence, by Pontrjagin’s Duality Theorem D.1.3, there
would exist x ∈K, x ̸= 0, with χy(x) = 1 for all y in K. This is impossible,
since χ ̸= 1K.
We claim that the isomorphism Φ−1 : H →K is continuous. It suffices
to show that Φ−1 is continuous at the unit character 1K. Let ε > 0. Since χ
is non trivial, χ(x0) ̸= 1 for some x0 ∈K. Set δ = |χ(x0) −1| > 0 and

Q = {x ∈K : |x| ≤|x0|/ε},

where | · | is an absolute value on K. Then Q is a compact subset of K, and

UQ,δ = {χy : |χ(yx) −1| < δ, for all
x ∈Q}

is a neighbourhood of 1K in H. Let y ∈K with χy ∈UQ,δ. Then, since

|χ(y(y−1x0)) −1| = |χ(x0) −1| = δ,

we have y−1x0 /∈Q, that is, |y| < ε. Thus, Φ−1 is continuous and is therefore
a topological isomorphism between H and K. Hence, H is locally compact
and, by the previous lemma, H is closed in �K. Since H is dense, H = �K. ■

Appendix E

Induced representations

Let G be a locally compact group. To any closed subgroup H and any unitary
representation (σ, K) of H is associated the induced representation IndG
Hσ of
G. This is one of the most important notion of the theory.
In case G is a finite group, the construction goes back to Frobenius;
the space H of IndG
Hσ consists of mappings ξ : G →K such that ξ(xh) =
σ(h−1)ξ(x) for all x in G and h in H, and IndG
Hσ(g) is given by left translation
by g−1 on H for every g in G (see [Curti–99, Chapter II,§4]). In case G
is locally compact and σ unitary, Section E.1 shows how to modify this
construction in order to obtain a unitary representation of G.
The second section of this chapter shows two useful facts concerning in-
duced representations. The first one is the important result of induction by
stages: if σ is itself of the form IndH
Kτ for a unitary representation τ of a
closed subgroup K of H, then IndG
H(IndH
Kτ) and IndG
Kτ are equivalent. The
second one deals with tensor products.
In the third section, we give a simple necessary and sufficient condition
for IndG
Hσ to have non-zero invariant vectors.
For more details on this chapter, see [Folla–95] or [Gaal–73].

E.1
Definition of induced representations

Let G be a locally compact group, H a closed subgroup of G, and (σ, K) a
unitary representation of H.
We first define a (usually non-complete) space of functions from G to
K on which G acts in a natural way. Let p : G →G/H be the canonical

405

406
APPENDIX E. INDUCED REPRESENTATIONS

projection, and let A be the vector space of all mappings ξ : G →K with
the following properties:

(i) ξ is continuous,

(ii) p(supp ξ) is compact,

(iii) ξ(xh) = σ(h−1)ξ(x) for all x ∈G and all h ∈H.

The induced representation of σ has two ingredients: the natural left action
of G on A (and on its completion, to be defined) and the Radon-Nikodym
factor which appears in Formula (∗∗) below.
The following proposition shows how to construct “many” elements from
A. For this, we need to consider vector valued integrals. Let X be a locally
compact space with a regular Borel measure µ, and let ξ : X →K be a
continuous function with compact support from X to some Hilbert space K.

E.3
Induced representations with invariant
vectors

The following important theorem is a characterisation of induced representa-
tions which have non-zero invariant vectors. The result is used in the proof
of Theorem 1.7.1.

Theorem E.3.1 Let G be a σ-compact locally compact group and H a closed
subgroup of G. Let (σ, K) be a unitary representation of H. The following
properties are equivalent:

(i) the unit representation 1G of G is contained in IndG
Hσ, that is, the
Hilbert space H of IndG
Hσ contains a non-zero invariant vector;

(ii) the space G/H has a finite invariant regular Borel measure and 1H is
contained in σ.

Proof
We first show that (ii) implies (i), which is the easier implication.
By assumption, 1H is contained in σ. Hence, by Proposition E.2.2, the quasi-
regular representation IndG
H1H is contained in IndG
Hσ. Since G/H has finite
measure, the constant function 1 on G/H belongs to L2(G/H), the Hilbert
space of IndG
H1H, and is invariant.
To show that (i) implies (ii), we realize the Hilbert space H of π = IndG
Hσ
as a space of measurable mappings from G to K (see Remark E.1.2).
Let ξ be a non-zero invariant vector in H. Thus, ξ : G →K is a measurable
non-zero mapping such that, for every h ∈H,

ξ(xh) = σ(h−1)ξ(x)

for almost every x ∈G, and such that
�

G/H
∥ξ(x)∥2dµ(xH) < ∞.

Since ξ is invariant, we have, for every g ∈G and for almost every x ∈G,

(∗)
�ρ(g−1x)

ρ(x)

�1/2
ξ(g−1x) = ξ(x),

where ρ is a rho-function defining the quasi-invariant measure µ on G/H,
which we assume to be normalised by ρ(e) = 1.

APPENDIX E. INDUCED REPRESENTATIONS

Appendix F

Weak containment and Fell
topology

The notion of subrepresentation, which is convenient for comparing represen-
tations of compact groups, is far too rigid for other groups. The appropriate
notion for locally compact groups is that of weak containment, introduced by
Godement [Godem–48] and then much developped by Fell, both for locally
compact groups and for C∗-algebras (see [Fell–60], [Fell–62], and [Dixmi–69]).
Weak containment is defined in Section F.1. In particular, consider two
unitary representations π, ρ of a locally compact group G, with π irreducible;
if π is a subrepresentation of ρ, then π is weakly contained in ρ; the converse
holds when G is compact, but not in general.
In Section F.2, we analyze the notion of weak containment in the context
of Fell’s topology. In particular, the usual operations (direct sums, tensor
products, restrictions, inductions) are shown to be continuous for this topol-
ogy in Section F.3. Section F.4 expresses weak containment in terms of group
C∗-algebras. In Section F.5, we discuss briefly direct integral decompositions
of unitary representations.

F.1
Weak containment of unitary represen-
tations

If (π, H) is a representation of a topological group, recall that the diagonal
matrix coefficients ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩for ξ in H are called the functions of positive
type associated to π; see Proposition C.4.3.

419

420
APPENDIX F. WEAK CONTAINMENT AND FELL TOPOLOGY

Definition F.1.1 Let (π, H) and (ρ, K) be unitary representations of the
topological group G. We say that π is weakly contained in ρ if every function
of positive type associated to π can be approximated, uniformly on compact
subsets of G, by finite sums of functions of positive type associated to ρ. This
means: for every ξ in H, every compact subset Q of G and every ε > 0, there
exist η1, . . . , ηn in K such that, for all x ∈Q,

|⟨π(x)ξ, ξ⟩−

n
�

i=1
⟨ρ(x)ηi, ηi⟩| < ε.

We write for this π ≺ρ.
If π ≺ρ and ρ ≺π, we say that π and ρ are weakly equivalent and denote
this by π ∼ρ.

Remark F.1.2 (i) For π ≺ρ, it clearly suffices that the condition stated
in the above definition holds for any normalised function of positive type
ϕ = ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩associated to π (that is, with ϕ(e) = ∥ξ∥2 = 1).
On the other hand, if this condition holds for a normalised function of
positive type ϕ = ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩, then ϕ can be approximated, uniformly on
compact subsets of G, by convex combinations of normalised functions of
positive type associated to ρ. Indeed, let �n
i=1 ψi be close to ϕ, uniformly on
a compact set Q. We can assume that e ∈Q. Hence, �n
i=1 ψi(e) is close to
ϕ(e) = 1. This implies that the convex sum

n
�

i=1

ψi(e)
ψ1(e) + · · · + ψn(e)

�ψi

�

is close to ϕ, uniformly on Q.
(ii) The relation π ≺ρ depends only on the equivalence classes of the
representations π and ρ.
(iii) If π is contained in ρ, then π ≺ρ. Indeed, in this case, every function
of positive type associated to π is a function of positive type associated to ρ.
(iv) For unitary representations π, ρ, and σ of G, weak containments
π ≺ρ and ρ ≺σ imply π ≺σ.
(v) For unitary representations π and ρ and cardinal numbers m, n > 0,
we have π ≺ρ if and only if mπ ≺nρ. Thus, the weak containment relation
does not take multiplicities in account.

F.1. WEAK CONTAINMENT OF UNITARY REPRESENTATIONS 421

(vi) If π is finite dimensional and if ρ is irreducible, then ρ ≺π implies
that ρ is contained in π (Corollary F.2.9 below). For this reason, the notion
of weak containment is not relevant for finite dimensional representations.
(vii) For unitary representations (π)i∈I and ρ of G, we have �

i∈I πi ≺ρ
if and only if πi ≺ρ for all i ∈I. See also Proposition F.2.7 below.
(viii) The notion of weak containment is best understood in terms of
representations of the C∗-algebra of G, for which it appears in a natural way.
For this, see Section F.4.
(ix) Definition 7.3.5 of [Zimm–84a] offers a different definition of weak
containment. Let (π, H) and (ρ, K) be unitary representations of the topo-
logical group G. To a finite orthonormal set ξ1, . . . , ξn of H is associated a
continuous function

Φ(ξ1,...,ξn) : G →Mn(C),
g �→(⟨π(g)ξi, ξj⟩)1≤i,j≤n

which is called a n-by-n submatrix of π. Define then π to be weakly contained
in ρ in the sense of Zimmer, and write π ≺Z ρ, if any submatrix of π is a
limit of submatrices of ρ, uniformly on compact subsets of G.
If π and ρ are finite dimensional, then π ≺Z ρ if and only if π is contained
in ρ; in particular, ρ ⊕ρ is not weakly contained in ρ in the sense of Zimmer,
whereas ρ ⊕ρ ≺ρ. In general, it can be shown that π ≺ρ if and only if
π ≺Z ∞ρ; in case π is irreducible, then π ≺ρ if and only if π ≺Z ρ.

The following lemma, due to Fell [Fell–63], will be used several times
(proofs of Propositions F.1.10, F.3.2 and of Theorem F.3.5). Its proof is
surprisingly less elementary than one might first expect.

Lemma F.1.3 Let (π, H) and (ρ, K) be unitary representations of the topo-
logical group G. Let V be a subset of H such that {π(x)ξ : x ∈G, ξ ∈V} is
total in H. The following are equivalent:
(i) π ≺ρ;
(ii) every function of positive type of the form ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩with ξ ∈V can be
approximated, uniformly on compact subsets of G, by finite sums of functions
of positive type associated to ρ.

Proof
We only have to show that (ii) implies (i).
Let X be the set of all vectors ξ in H such that the associated function of
positive type ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩can be approximated, uniformly on compact subsets
of G, by finite sums of functions of positive type associated to ρ. We have to
show that X = H. This will be done in several steps.

In the important case where an irreducible representation π is weakly
contained in ρ, we can approximate the functions of positive type associated
to π by functions of positive type associated to ρ, and not just by sums of
such functions.

Proposition F.1.4 Let (π, H) and (ρ, K) be unitary representations of the
locally compact group G such that π ≺ρ. Assume that π is irreducible. Let
ξ be a unit vector H. Then ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩can be approximated, uniformly on
compact subsets of G, by normalised functions of positive type associated to
ρ.

Proof
Let F be the set of normalised functions of positive type on G which
are associated to ρ. Let C be the closure of the convex hull of F in the weak*
topology of L∞(G). Then C is a compact convex set and, by assumption,
ϕ = ⟨π(·)ξ, ξ⟩is in C.
On the other hand, since π is irreducible, ϕ ∈ext(P≤1(G)) by Theo-
rem C.5.5 and, hence, ϕ ∈ext(C). It is then a standard fact that ϕ is in the
weak* closure of F in C (see, e.g., [Conwa–87, Theorem 7.8]). By Raikov’s
Theorem C.5.6, it follows that ϕ is in the closure of F with respect to the
topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets of G. ■

a unit vector ξ in H such that

As the following proposition shows, the relation of weak containment
essentially coincides with the relation of containment in the case of compact
groups.

Proposition F.1.8 Let G be a compact group, and let π and ρ be unitary
representations of G. Then π ≺ρ if and only if every irreducible subrepre-
sentation of π is contained in ρ.

Proof
Assume that π ≺ρ. Since π can be decomposed as direct sum of
irreducible representations, we can assume that π is irreducible. Let ϕ be a
normalised function of positive type associated to π. Then, as π ≺ρ, there
exists a sequence (ψn)n of functions of positive type associated to ρ which
converges to ϕ uniformly on G. For n large enough, we have

Remark F.1.9 Let Γ be a discrete group and H a subgroup of Γ. The
restriction λΓ|H of the regular representation of Γ to H is a multiple of the
regular representation λH of H. Indeed, let T be a set of representatives
for the right coset space H\Γ. Then ℓ2(Γ) has a direct sum decomposition
ℓ2(Γ) = �

of λΓ|H to each subspace ℓ2(Ht) is equivalent to λH, this proves the claim.

bitrary locally compact group (see also Remark F.1.11 below).

Remark F.1.11 Conversely, λH is weakly contained in λG|H (Exercise F.6.1)

Example F.1.12 As shown in Remark 1.1.2.vi, the unit representation 1R
of R is weakly contained in the regular representation λR. Let us see that
every character χ ∈�R is weakly contained in λR. Indeed, given a compact

for all

for all x ∈Q. For a more general fact, see Theorem G.3.2.

F.2
Fell topology on sets of unitary represen-
tations

Let G be a topological group. One would like to define a topology on the fam-
ily of equivalences classes of unitary representations of G. There is a problem
since this family is not a set. For this reason, we have to restrict ourselves
to sets of such classes. One standard way is to consider only equivalences
classes of unitary representations in Hilbert spaces with dimension bounded
by some cardinal number; another is to consider equivalence classes of irre-
ducible representations, namely to consider the unitary dual �G; a third way
is to consider cyclic representations (see Remark C.4.13).
Let R be a fixed set of equivalence classes of unitary representations of
G.

Definition F.2.1 For a unitary representation (π, H) in R, functions of pos-
itive type ϕ1, . . . , ϕn associated to π, a compact subset Q of G, and ε > 0, let
W(π, ϕ1, . . . , ϕn, Q, ε) be the set of all unitary representations ρ in R with
the following property:

for each ϕi, there exists a function ψ which is a sum of functions of
positive type associated to ρ and such that

|ϕi(x) −ψ(x)| < ε,
for all
x ∈Q.

428

The sets W(π, ϕ1, . . . , ϕn, Q, ε) form a basis for a topology on R, called Fell’s
topology.

Fell’s topology can be described in terms of convergence of nets as follows.

Proposition F.2.2 Let π ∈R. A net (πi)i in R converges to π if and only
if π ≺�

j πj for every subnet (πj)j of (πi)i.

Proof
This is clear since the sets W(π, ϕ1, . . . , ϕn, Q, ε) form a basis for
the family of neighbourhoods of π. ■

Remark F.2.3 If the net (πi)i converges to π, then (πi)i converges also to
every unitary representation ρ which is weakly contained in π.

Proposition F.1.4 implies that, in the important case R = �G, the ψ’s
occuring in Definition F.2.1 can be taken as functions of positive type associ-
ated to ρ, and not just sums of such functions. More generally, the following
proposition is an immediate consequence of Proposition F.1.4.

Proposition F.2.4 Let R be a set of unitary representations of G, and let
π ∈R be an irreducible representation. A basis for the family of neighbour-
hoods of π in the Fell topology on R is given by the sets �

consisting of all ρ ∈R such that, for each ϕi, there exists a function of pos-

for all

Example F.2.5 (i) Let G be an abelian topological group. The topology of

Corollary F.2.9 Let G be a topological group, and let π and ρ be unitary
representations of G. Assume that π is finite dimensional and that ρ is irre-
ducible. If ρ ≺π, then ρ is contained in π.
In particular, every finite dimensional irreducible unitary representation
of G is a closed point in �G.

Proof
Let ϕ be a normalised function of positive type associated to ρ.
Since ρ ≺π, it follows from the previous lemma that ϕ is a finite sum of
functions of positive type associated to π. As ρ is irreducible, this implies
that ρ is contained in π (Proposition C.5.1). ■

F.3
Continuity of operations

We are going to show that the usual operations on unitary representations are
continuous with respect to Fell’s topology. In order to show this, it suffices
to show that any one of these operations preserves the weak containment
relation.
The continuity of the operation of taking direct sums of representation is
obvious from the definitions.

F.3. CONTINUITY OF OPERATIONS
433

Proposition F.3.1 If (πi)i and (ρi)i are families of unitary representations
of the topological group G such that πi ≺ρi for every i, then �

i πi ≺�

i ρi.

The continuity of the tensor product operation is less obvious, but it
follows from Lemma F.1.3.

Proposition F.3.2 Let π1, π2, ρ1, ρ2 be unitary representations of the topo-
logical group G such that π1 ≺ρ1 and π2 ≺ρ2. Then π1 ⊗π2 ≺ρ1 ⊗ρ2.

Proof
Let H1 and H2 be the Hilbert spaces of π1 and π2. It is clear that
every function of positive type of the form

x �→⟨((π1 ⊗π2)(g))(ξ1 ⊗ξ2), ξ1 ⊗ξ2⟩= ⟨π1(g)ξ1, ξ1⟩⟨π2(g)ξ2, ξ2⟩

can be approximated by sums of functions of positive type associated to
ρ1 ⊗ρ2. Since the set {ξ1 ⊗ξ2 : ξ1 ∈H1, ξ2 ∈H2} is total in H1 ⊗H2, the
claim follows from Lemma F.1.3. ■

Corollary F.3.3 For a locally compact group G, the following properties are
equivalent:

(i) 1G ≺λG;

(ii) π ≺λG, for every unitary representation π of G.

Proof
If 1G ≺λG, then π = 1G ⊗π ≺λG ⊗π, by the above proposition.
As λG ⊗π is a multiple of λG (see Corollary E.2.6), the claim follows. ■

As we will see in Theorem G.3.2, each of the Properties (i) and (ii) in the
previous corollary characterises amenable locally compact groups.
The continuity of the operation of restriction to closed subgroups is clear
from the definition of weak containment.

Proposition F.3.4 Let H be a closed subgroup of the topological group G,
and let π and ρ be unitary representations of G such that π ≺ρ. Then
π|H ≺ρ|H.

We turn to the continuity of induction. This fact plays a crucial role in
the proof of Theorem 1.7.1.

F.4
The C∗-algebras of a locally compact group

The study of the unitary representations of a locally compact group can be
cast into the general framework of C∗-algebras. An overall reference for what
follows is [Dixmi–69].
A Banach ∗-algebra A is called a C∗-algebra if the norm on A satisfies

(∗)
∥x∗x∥= ∥x∥2,
for all
x ∈A.

Example F.4.1 (i) For any locally compact space X, the algebra C0(X),
with the obvious operations and the uniform norm, is a C∗-algebra.

(ii) If A is a commutative C∗-algebra, then the Gelfand transform (see
D.1) is an isometric *-isomorphism between A and C0(∆(A)). So, any com-
mutative C∗-algebra occurs as in (i).

(iii) Let H be a Hilbert space. It is easy to verify that ∥T ∗T∥= ∥T∥2 for
all T ∈L(H). Hence, every norm closed *-subalgebra of L(H) is a C∗-algebra.

(iv) By a theorem of Gelfand and Naimark, any C∗-algebra occurs as
in (iii).

Let G be a locally compact group, fixed throughout this section. To every
unitary representation (π, H) of G is associated a ∗-representation of the
Banach ∗-algebra L1(G) in H, that is, a continuous ∗-algebra homomorphism
L1(G) →L(H), again denoted by π and defined by

π(f) =
�

G
f(x)π(x)dx ∈L(H),

namely by

⟨π(f)ξ, η⟩=
�

G
f(x)⟨π(x)ξ, η⟩dx ∈C,
ξ, η ∈H,

for f ∈L1(G). This ∗-representation of L1(G) is non-degenerate, which means
that, for every ξ ∈H \ {0}, there exists f ∈L1(G) such that π(f)ξ ̸= 0.
Conversely, any non-degenerate ∗-representation of L1(G) is of this form.
This is straightforward if the algebra L1(G) has a unit, namely if the group
G is discrete. In the general case, this follows from a standard argument
using “ approximate units” in L1(G); see Proposition 13.4.2 in [Dixmi–69].
To any unitary representation (π, H) of G, we can associate the sub-C∗-
algebra of L(H) generated by π(L1(G)). Definitions F.4.3 and F.4.6 below
refer to the two most important cases.

F.4. THE C∗-ALGEBRAS OF A LOCALLY COMPACT GROUP
437

Example F.4.2 For the left regular representation λG of G, the ∗-representation
λG : L1(G) →L(L2(G)) is given by convolution:

λG(f)ξ = f ∗ξ,
for
f ∈L1(G)
and
ξ ∈L2(G).

Define the universal representation πuniv of G to be the direct sum of
all cyclic unitary representations of G. We have a ∗-representation πuniv :
L1(G) →L(Huniv) and we define the maximal norm of f ∈L1(G) by

∥f∥max = ∥πuniv(f)∥.

Observe that ∥λG(f)∥≤∥f∥max ≤∥f∥1.

Definition F.4.3 The completion of L1(G) with respect to the norm f �→
∥f∥max is a C∗-algebra called the maximal C∗-algebra of G, and is denoted
by C∗(G).

Let π be a unitary representation of G. As π is a direct sum of cyclic rep-
resentations (Proposition C.4.9), ∥π(f)∥≤∥f∥max for all f in L1(G). Hence,
f �→π(f) extends to a ∗-representation of C∗(G), also denoted by π. In this
way, we obtain a one-to-one correspondence between unitary representations
of G and non-degenerate ∗-representations of the C∗algebra C∗(G).
The notion of weak containment introduced in the previous section has the
following neat interpretation in terms of C∗(G). For the proof, see [Dixmi–69,
Section 18].

Theorem F.4.4 Let π and ρ be unitary representations of G. Denote by
C∗Kerπ and C∗Kerρ the kernels of the corresponding representations of C∗(G).
The following properties are equivalent:

(i) π ≺ρ;

(ii) C∗Kerρ ⊂C∗Kerπ;

(iii) ∥π(f)∥≤∥ρ(f)∥for all f in L1(G).

Remark F.4.5 Let Prim(C∗(G)) be the primitive ideal space of C∗(G), that
is, the set of the kernels C∗Kerπ of all irreducible representations π of G.
The Jacobson topology on Prim(C∗(G)) is a natural topology defined as fol-
lows: the closure of a subset S of Prim(C∗(G)) is the set of all C∗Kerπ in
Prim(C∗(G)) such that
�

ρ∈S
C∗Kerρ ⊂C∗Kerπ.

438
APPENDIX F. WEAK CONTAINMENT AND FELL TOPOLOGY

Define the Jacobson topology on �G to be the inverse image of the mapping

Φ : �G →Prim(C∗(G)),
π �→C∗Kerπ

(that is, the closed subsets in �G are the sets Φ−1(S) where S is closed in
Prim(C∗(G)). It can be shown that Fell’s topology on �G as defined in the
previous section coincides with Jacobson’s topology (see [Dixmi–69, Section
18]).

There is another C∗-algebra one can associate to G.

Definition F.4.6 The norm closure of {λG(f) : f ∈L1(G)} in L(H) is a
C∗-algebra called the reduced C∗-algebra of G, and is denoted by C∗
red(G).

The C∗-algebra C∗
red(G) can also be described as the completion of L1(G)
with respect to the norm f �→∥λG(f)∥.

Example F.4.7 Let G be a locally compact abelian group. The Fourier
transform F : L2(G) →L2( �G) is a unitary equivalence between λG and the
unitary representation π of G on L2( �G) defined by

(π(x)ξ)(�x) = �x(x)ξ(�x),
ξ ∈L2( �G), �x ∈�G, x ∈G

(see Remark D.1.4). The representation of L1(G) in L2( �G) associated to π
is given, for f in L1(G), by π(f) = TFf, where TFf is the multiplication
operator by the bounded function Ff. By the Stone-Weierstraß Theorem,
{Ff :
f ∈L1(G)} is a dense subalgebra of C0( �G). Hence, λG(f) �→TFf
extends to an isomorphism between C∗
red(G) and C0( �G).

The regular representation defines a surjective ∗-homomorphism

λG : C∗(G) →C∗
red(G),

so that C∗
red(G) is a quotient of C∗(G). Observe that, by the previous theorem,
λG is an isomorphism if and only if ∥f∥max = ∥λG(f)∥for all f in L1(G),
that is, if and only if every unitary representation of G is weakly contained
in λG. We will characterise the groups for which this holds in Chapter G.

F.5. DIRECT INTEGRALS OF UNITARY REPRESENTATIONS
439

F.5
Direct integrals of unitary representations

As previously mentioned (see remark after Corollary C.4.7), direct sums are
not sufficient in order to decompose a given unitary representation into irre-
ducible ones. One needs the notion of a direct integral of unitary represen-
tations.
Let (Z, µ) be a measure space, where µ is a σ-finite positive measure on
Z. A field of Hilbert spaces over Z is a family (H(z))z∈Z, where H(z) is a
Hilbert space for each z ∈Z. Elements of the vector space �

z∈Z H(z) are
called vector fields over Z.
To define a measurable field of Hilbert spaces over Z, we have to specify
the measurable vector fields. This depends on the choice of a fundamental
family of measurable vector fields; by definition, this is a sequence (xn)n∈N
of vector fields over Z with the following properties:

(i) for any m, n ∈N, the function z �→⟨xm(z), xn(z)⟩is measurable;

(ii) for every z ∈Z, the linear span of {xn(z) : n ∈N} is dense in H(z).

Fix a fundamental family of measurable vector fields. A vector field x ∈
�

z∈Z H(z) is said to be a measurable vector field if all the functions

z �→⟨x(z), xn(z)⟩,
n ∈N

are measurable. As is easily shown (Exercise F.6.6), the set M of measurable
vector fields is a linear subspace of �

z∈Z H(z). Moreover, if x, y ∈M, then
the function

Let now G be a second countable locally compact group. Let (Z, µ) be

Definition F.5.2 A Borel space is a space together with a σ-algebra of
subsets. Such a space Z is a standard Borel space, if Z is isomorphic, as a
Borel space, to a Borel subspace of a complete separable metric space.
A standard Borel space is either finite, or Borel isomorphic to Z, or Borel
isomorphic to the interval [0, 1] (see [Sriva–98, Corollaty 3.3.16]).

The following theorem shows that every unitary representation of G can
be decomposed as a direct integral of irreducible unitary representations over
a standard Borel space; for the proof, see Theorem 8.5.2 and Section 18.7 in
[Dixmi–69].

Theorem F.5.3 Let G be a second countable locally compact group and let
π be a unitary representation of G on a separable Hilbert space H. Then
there exist a standard Borel space Z, a bounded positive measure µ on Z, a
measurable field of Hilbert spaces z �→H(z) over Z, and a measurable field of
irreducible unitary representations z �→π(z) of G on the H(z)’s such that π
is unitarily equivalent to the direct integral
�⊕
Z π(z)dµ(z).

Appendix G

Amenability

Lebesgue proposed his view on integration in a short note (1901) and a fa-
mous book (1904). It was then a natural question to know whether Lebesgue’s
measure could be extended as a finitely additive measure defined on all sub-
sets of Rn which is invariant under orthogonal tranformations. Hausdorff
answered negatively for n ≥3 in 1914 and Banach positively for n ≤2 in
1923. Von Neumann showed in 1929 that the deep reason for this difference
lies in the group of isometries of Rn (viewed as a discrete group) which is
amenable for n ≤2 and which is not so for n ≥3.
In Section G.1, amenability is defined for a topological group in two
equivalent ways, by the existence of an invariant mean on an appropriate
space and as a fixed point property. Examples are given Section G.2. For a
locally compact group G, it is shown in Section G.3 that G is amenable if
and only if 1G ≺λG; a consequence is that amenability is inherited by closed
subgroups of locally compact groups.
It is remarkable that amenability can be given a very large number of
equivalent definitions, at least for locally compact groups. In Section G.4,
we give Kesten’s characterisation in terms of appropriate operators, which
is crucial for the study of random walks on groups.
In Section G.5, we
give Følner’s characterisation in terms of asymptotically invariant sets from
[Foeln–55]. For other equivalences, see the standard expositions which in-
clude [Green–69], [Eymar–75], Chapter 4 of [Zimm–84a], and [Pater–88]; see
also[CeGrH–99] and [AnDRe–00].
The notion of amenability has spread over many domains of mathematics:
Banach algebras, operator algebras (nuclearity, exactness, injectivity), metric
spaces, group actions, foliations, groupoids, . . . In the limited context of this

445

446
APPENDIX G. AMENABILITY

book, its importance is due to its relation with Property (T). In particular to
the fact (Theorem 1.1.6) that a locally compact group is both an amenable
group and a group with Property (T) if and only if it is compact.

G.1
Invariant means

Let X be a set. A ring R of subsets of X is a non-empty class of subsets
of X which is closed under the formation of union and differences of sets: if
A, B ∈R, then A ∪B ∈R and A \ B ∈R.

Definition G.1.1 A mean m on a ring R of subsets of X is a finitely additive
probability measure on R, that is, m is a function from R to R with the
following properties:

(i) m(A) ≥0 for all A ∈R;

(ii) m(X) = 1;

(iii) m(A1 ∪· · ·∪An) = m(A1)+· · ·+m(An) if A1, . . . , An ∈R are pairwise
disjoint.

If a group G acts on X leaving R invariant, then m is said to be a G-invariant
mean if

(iv) m(gA) = m(A) for all g ∈G and A ∈R.

For a ring R of subsets of X, denote by E the vector space of complex-
valued functions on X generated by the characteristic functions χA of subsets
A in R. There is a natural bijective correspondence between means m on R
and linear functionals M on E such that

m(A) = M(χA)
for all
A ∈R.

In case R is G-invariant for some group G acting on X, the mean m is G
invariant if and only if M(gϕ) = M(ϕ) for all g ∈G and ϕ ∈E, where gϕ is
the function on X defined by aϕ(x) = ϕ(ax) for x ∈X.
Assume now that there is given a σ-algebra B of subsets of X and a
measure µ on (X, B). There is again a bijective correspondence between ap-
propriate linear functionals on L∞(X, µ) and appropriate means on B, as we
now explain.

G.1. INVARIANT MEANS
447

Definition G.1.2 Let (X, B, µ) be a measure space and let E be a closed
subspace of L∞(X, B, µ) which contains the constant functions and is closed
under complex conjugation. A mean on E is a linear functional M : E →C
with the following properties:

(i) M(1X) = 1;

(ii) M(ϕ) ≥0 for all ϕ ∈E with ϕ ≥0.

Let G be a group acting on E. We say that M is G-invariant if, moreover,

(iii) M(gϕ) = M(ϕ) for all g ∈G and ϕ ∈E.

Remark G.1.3 (i) A mean M on E is automatically continuous. Indeed,

−∥ϕ∥∞1X ≤ϕ ≤∥ϕ∥∞1X.

Hence, |M(ϕ)| ≤∥ϕ∥∞by (i) and (ii).
(ii) A mean M on L∞(X, B, µ) defines a mean m on the σ-algebra B by
m(A) = M(χA) for all A ∈B. Observe that m is absolutely continuous with
respect to µ, in the sense that m(A) = 0 for all A ∈B with µ(A) = 0.
Conversely, if m is a mean on B which is absolutely continuous with
respect to µ, then there exists a unique mean M on L∞(X, B, µ) such that
m(A) = M(χA) for all A ∈B. Indeed, define

M(ϕ) =

m
�

αim(Ai)

if ϕ = �m
i=1 αiχAi is a measurable simple function on X. By finite additivity
of m, this definition does not depend on the given representation of ϕ as
linear combination of characteristic functions of measurable subsets. Let ϕ
be a measurable bounded function on X. There exists a sequence (ϕn)n of
measurable simple functions on X converging uniformly on X to ϕ. It is
easily verified that (M(ϕn))n is a Cauchy sequence in C and that its limit
does not depend on the particular choice of (ϕn)n. Define then

M(ϕ) = lim

Since m is absolutely continuous with respect to µ, the number M(ϕ) de-
pends only on the equivalence class [ϕ] of ϕ in L∞(X, µ), and we can define
M([ϕ]) = M(ϕ). One checks that M is a mean on L∞(X, µ). For more details,
see [HewSt–69, (20.35) Theorem].

From now on, we will identify a mean M on a space E in the sense of
Definition G.1.2 and the corresponding mean m on B (or on R), and we will
use the same notation m in both cases.
Let G be a topological group. Let ℓ∞(G) be the Banach space of bounded
functions on G. The group G acts on ℓ∞(G) by left translations:

ϕ �→gϕ,
ϕ ∈ℓ∞(G), g ∈G.

Recall that gϕ is defined by gϕ(x) = ϕ(gx).
Let UCB(G) be the closed subspace of ℓ∞(G), consisting of all left uni-
formly continuous functions on G. Observe that a function ϕ in ℓ∞(G) is in
UCB(G) if and only if the mapping x �→xϕ from G to ℓ∞(G) is continuous.
Observe also that UCB(G) is invariant under left translation by elements
from G.

Definition G.1.4 We consider the group G acting on itself by left trans-
lations. The topological group G is said to be amenable if there exists an
invariant mean on UCB(G)

Example G.1.5 Let G be a compact group. Then UCB(G) = C(G), and
an invariant mean m on C(G) is an invariant regular Borel measure on G
with m(1G) = 1. Hence, the normalised Haar measure is the unique invariant
mean on C(G). In particular, compact groups are amenable.

For a non-compact group G, invariant means on UCB(G) – when they
exist – are in general not unique.
Let X be convex subset of a locally convex topological vector space. A
continuous action G × X →X of G on X is said to be an affine action if

X →X,
x �→gx

is an affine mapping for all g in G. Recall that a mapping α : X →X is
affine if

α(tx + (1 −t)y) = tα(x) + (1 −t)α(y),
for all
x, y ∈X, 0 ≤t ≤1.

Remark G.1.6 The topological dual space UCB(G)∗of UCB(G), endowed
with the weak* topology, is a locally convex vector space. The set M of all
means on UCB(G) is a weak* closed and, hence, compact convex subset of
the unit ball of UCB(G)∗. Observe that M is non empty: for instance, the

G.1. INVARIANT MEANS
449

point evaluation f �→f(e) is a mean on UCB(G). There is a continuous
affine action m �→gm of G on M given by gm(ϕ) = m(g−1ϕ) for all g ∈G
and ϕ ∈UCB(G). The group G is amenable if and only if this action has a
fixed point in M.
The remarkable fact is that, if this is the case, any affine action of G on
a compact convex has a fixed point.

Theorem G.1.7 For a topological group G, the following properties are equiv-
alent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) (Fixed point property) any continuous affine action of G on a non-
empty compact convex subset X of a locally convex topological vector
space has a fixed point.

Proof
By the previous remark, (ii) implies (i).
Assume that G is amenable, and that a continuous affine action of G on a
non-empty compact convex set X in a locally convex vector space V is given.
Fix an element x0 in X. Let t : G →X, g �→gx0 be the corresponding
orbital mapping. For every f ∈C(X), the function f ◦t is in UCB(G).
Indeed, as X is compact, f ◦t is bounded. Since, moreover, the action of G
is continuous, for every ε > 0, there exists a neighbourhood U of e such that,
for all u ∈U,
sup
x∈X
|f(ux) −f(x)| ≤ε.

Let m be a mean on UCB(G). Then a probability measure µm on X is
defined by
µm(f) = m(f ◦t),
for all
f ∈C(X).

Let bm ∈X be the barycentre of µm. Recall that bm is defined as the X-valued
integral bm =
�

X xdµm(x) and that bm is the unique element in X with the
property that ϕ(bm) = µm(ϕ) for every ϕ ∈V ∗(see [Rudin–73, Theorem
3.27]). In particular, the mapping Φ : M →X, m �→bm is continuous, when
the set M of all means on UCB(G) is endowed with the weak* topology.
Observe that bm = gx0 if m = δg is the evaluation at g ∈G.
We claim that bgm = gbm for every g ∈G. Indeed, this is clearly true if m
is a convex combination of point evaluations. By Hahn-Banach’s theorem,

the set of all convex combinations of point evaluations is dense in M (Exer-
cise G.6.1). Since G acts by continuous affine mappings on X and since Φ is
continuous, the claim follows.
If now m is an invariant mean on UCB(G), then bm ∈X is a fixed point
for the action of G. ■

G.2
Examples of amenable groups

Besides compact groups, the first examples of amenable groups are the abelian
groups.

Theorem G.2.1 (Markov-Kakutani) Every abelian topological group G
is amenable.

Proof
Given a continuous affine action of G on a non-empty compact
convex subset X in a locally convex vector space V, define for every integer
n ≥0 and every g in G, a continuous affine transformation An(g) : X →X
by

subset of X for every γ in G.
We claim that �

Let γ = γ1γ2 . . . γn ∈G. Then, since G is abelian, γ(X) is contained in γi(X)
for all i = 1, . . . , n. Thus, γ1(X)∩· · ·∩γn(X) contains γ(X), and this proves
the claim.

all g in G. ■

G.2. EXAMPLES OF AMENABLE GROUPS
451

We discuss now the behaviour of amenability under exact sequences and
directed unions.

Proposition G.2.2 Let G be a topological group.
(i) Let N be a closed normal subgroup of G. If G is amenable, then G/N
is amenable.
(ii) Let N be a closed normal subgroup of G. If N and G/N are amenable,
then G is amenable.
(iii) Let (Gi)i∈I be a directed family of closed subgroups of G such that
�

i∈I Gi is dense in G. If Gi is amenable for every i ∈I, then G is amenable.

Proof
(i) follows from the fact that UCB(G/N) can be viewed as subspace
of UCB(G).
(ii) Assume that N and G/N are amenable, and that a continuous affine
action of G on a non-empty compact convex set X is given. Let XN be the
closed subspace of all fixed points of N in X. It is clear that XN is a compact
convex subset of X, that XN is invariant under G, and that the action of
G on XN factorizes to an action of G/N. Since N is amenable, XN is non
empty, by Theorem G.1.7. Hence, G/N has a fixed point x0 in XN, by the
amenability of G/N. Clearly, x0 is a fixed point for G. The claim follows now
from Theorem G.1.7.
(iii) Let G′ denote the union �

i∈I Gi, with the inductive limit topology.
The inclusion G′ →G is uniformly continuous with dense image, so that
the restriction mapping UCB(G) →UCB(G′) is an isomorphism. We can
therefore assume without loss of generality that G = �

i∈I Gi.
For every i ∈I, let Mi be the set of all Gi-invariant means on UCB(G).
We claim that �

i∈I Mi is non-empty. This will prove (iii), since any m ∈
�

i∈I Mi is a G-invariant mean on UCB(G).
Observe that every Mi is a closed subset of the compact set M of all
means on UCB(G) with respect to the weak* topology. Hence, it suffices to
show that the family (Mi)i∈I has the finite intersection property.
Let F is a finite subset of I. Let i ∈I be such that Gj ⊂Gi for all j ∈F.
Choose a Gi-invariant mean mi on UCB(Gi). Define a mean �
mi on UCB(G)
by
�
mi(f) = mi(f|Gi),
for all
f ∈UCB(G).

Then �mi is Gi-invariant and therefore �mi ∈Mj for all j ∈F. Thus, �

j∈F Mj ̸=
∅, as claimed. ■

452
APPENDIX G. AMENABILITY

Corollary G.2.3 Every compact extension of a soluble topological group is
amenable. Every locally finite group is amenable.

We now give examples of non amenable groups.

Example G.2.4 (i) The group G = SL2(R) is non amenable. Indeed, there
is no invariant measure on the projective real line R ∪{∞} ∼= G/P (see
Example A.6.4). In particular, the action of G on the compact convex set of
all probability measures on R ∪{∞} has no fixed points.

(ii) Let F2 be the non-abelian free group on two generators a and b, with
the discrete topology. Then F2 is non amenable.
Indeed, assume, by contradiction, that there exists an invariant mean m
on UCB(F2) = ℓ∞(F2). For each subset B of F2, write m(B) for the value
of m on the characteristic function of B. Every element in F2 can be written
as a reduced word in a, a−1, b, b−1. Let A be the subset of all words in F2
beginning with a non-zero power of a. Then F2 = aA∪A. As m(aA) = m(A)
and m(F2) = 1, if follows that m(A) ≥1/2. On the other hand, A, bA and
b2A are mutually disjoint subsets of F2. Hence

1 = m(F2) ≥m(A) + m(bA) + m(b2A) = 3m(A) ≥3

2.

This is a contradiction.

(iii) The fundamental group Γg of a closed orientable Riemann surface of
genus g ≥2 has a presentation

Γg =

�

and bj �→e for all i = 3, . . . , g and j = 1, . . . , g. It follows from (ii) and from
Proposition G.2.2 that Γg is not amenable.

G.3
Weak containment and amenability

Let G be a locally compact group. We will always assume that G is equipped
with a fixed left Haar measure; the spaces Lp(G) are taken with respect to
this measure.

G.3. WEAK CONTAINMENT AND AMENABILITY
453

For such groups, it is more convenient to characterise amenability of G
by the existence of invariant means on L∞(G).
For f in L1(G) and ϕ in L∞(G), the convolution product f ∗ϕ belongs
to UCB(G). Let L1(G)1,+ denote the convex set of all f ∈L1(G) with f ≥0
and ∥f∥1 = 1. Observe that L1(G)1,+ is closed under convolution.
The set M of all means on L∞(G) is a weak* closed (and hence compact)
convex subset of the unit ball of L∞(G)∗. We can view L1(G)1,+ as a subset
of M, since every f in L1(G)1,+ defines an element in M, via integration
against f. Hahn-Banach’s theorem shows that L1(G)1,+ is weak* dense in M
(Exercise G.6.2).
The following theorem is due to Reiter [Reiter–65] and Hulanicki [Hulan–66].
The proof we give for the equivalence of (iii) and (iv) is shorter than the proofs
that we have found in the literature.

Theorem G.3.1 Let G be a locally compact group. The following properties
are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) there exists a topological invariant mean on L∞(G), that is, a mean m
on L∞(G) such that m(f ∗ϕ) = m(ϕ) for all f in L1(G)1,+ and ϕ in
L∞(G);

(iii) (Reiter’s Property (P1)) for every compact subset Q of G and every
ε > 0, there exists f in L1(G)1,+ such that

The following result gives a characterisation of amenability of a locally
compact group G in terms of a weak containment property of λG. Its proof
is based on the equivalence between amenability and Reiter’s Property (P1)
from the previous lemma.

Theorem G.3.2 (Hulanicki-Reiter) Let G be a locally compact group.
The following properties are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) 1G ≺λG;

(iii) π ≺λG for every unitary representation π of G.

Proof
The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) was already proved in Proposi-
tion F.3.3. In view of the previous theorem, it suffices to show that (ii) is
equivalent to Reiter’s Property (P1).
Assume that (ii) holds. Then, given a compact subset Q of G and ε > 0,
there exists fQ,ε ∈L2(G) with ∥fQ,ε∥2 = 1 such that

sup
x∈Q
∥λG(x)fQ,ε −fQ,ε∥2 < ε

(see Corollary F.1.5). Set gQ,ε = |fQ,ε|2. Then gQ,ε ∈L1(G)1,+ and, by the
Cauchy Schwarz inequality,

∥x−1gQ,ε −gQ,ε∥1 ≤∥λG(x)fQ,ε + fQ,ε∥2∥λG(x)fQ,ε −fQ,ε∥2
≤2∥λG(x)fQ,ε −fQ,ε∥2 < 2ε

for all x ∈Q. Hence, G has Reiter’s Property (P1).
Conversely, assume that G has Reiter’s Property (P1). For a compact
subset Q of G and ε > 0, let f ∈L1(G)1,+ be such that

sup

∥x−1f −f∥1 < ε;

for all x ∈Q. This shows that 1G ≺λG. ■

Remark G.3.3 The amenability of G is equivalent to the fact that 1G is
weakly contained in λG, when both representations are viewed as unitary
representations of Gd, the group G viewed as discrete group. Indeed, the
proof of the previous theorem shows that Reiter’s Property (P ∗
1 ) from The-
orem G.3.1 is equivalent to the fact that 1G is weakly contained in λG when
both are viewed as representations of Gd.

Corollary G.3.4 Closed subgroups of amenable locally compact groups are
amenable.

Proof
Since the restriction of λG to H is weakly contained in λH (Propo-
sition F.1.10), the claim follows from the previous theorem. ■

Since the free group on two generators F2 is not amenable (see Exam-
ple G.2.4), this corollary has the following consequence.

Corollary G.3.5 Let G be a locally compact group containing F2 as a closed
subgroup. Then G is not amenable.

Example G.3.6 (i) The previous corollary can be applied to give another
proof of the non-amenability of SL2(R); see Example G.2.4. Indeed, SL2(R)
contains F2 as a lattice (Example B.2.5)

(ii) More generally, a non-compact semisimple real Lie group is non
amenable.
Indeed, such a group has a quotient which contains a closed
subgroup isomorphic to PSL2(R).

(iii) Let K be a local field (see D.4). Then SL2(K) is not amenable.
Indeed, SL2(K) contains a discrete group which is isomorphic to F2 (see
Exercises G.6.8 and G.6.9).

Remark G.3.7 The result in the previous corollary is, in general, not true
for non locally compact groups.
Indeed, let G = U(H) be the unitary group of an infinite dimensional
separable Hilbert space. Equipped with the weak operator topology, U(H)
is a topological group (Exercise G.6.3). It is shown in [Harpe–73] that U(H)
is amenable.

458
APPENDIX G. AMENABILITY

On the other hand, realizing U(H) as U(ℓ2(F2)), we see that U(H) con-
tains the subgroup
Γ = {λF2(γ) : γ ∈F2}

which is isomorphic to F2. The claim follows, as Γ is closed in U(ℓ2(F2)); see
Exercise G.6.4.

As we now show, the amenability of a closed subgroup H of a locally
compact group G implies the amenability of G if G/H is an amenable homo-
geneous space in Eymard’s sense, that is, if the quasi-regular representation
λG/H weakly contains 1G. This is the case, for example, if H is a lattice in
G, or, more generally, if G/H has a finite invariant regular Borel measure.
See [Eymar–72], where this notion is extensively studied.

Corollary G.3.8 Let H be a closed subgroup of a locally compact group G
such that G/H is amenable. The following are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) H is amenable .

Proof
In view of Corollary G.3.4, we only have to show that if H is
amenable, then so is G. By Hulanicki-Reiter Theorem G.3.2, we have 1H ≺
λH. Hence,
λG/H = IndG
H1H ≺IndG
HλH = λG,

by continuity of induction (Theorem F.3.5). Since G/H is amenable, we have
1G ≺λG/H and therefore 1G ≺λG. ■

The previous corollary, in combination with Remark G.3.6.ii, shows that
a lattice in a non-compact semisimple real Lie group is never amenable.
The following characterisation of amenability in terms of C∗-algebras fol-
lows immediately from Theorem F.4.4 and Hulanicki-Reiter’s Theorem.

Corollary G.3.9 For a locally compact group G, the following properties
are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) λG extends to an isomorphism between the maximal C∗-algebra C∗(G)
and the reduced C∗-algebra C∗
r(G) of G.

G.4
Kesten’s characterisation of amenability

Let G be a locally compact group, and let µ be a complex-valued finite regular
Borel measure on G. For a unitary representation (π, H) of G, let π(µ) be
the operator in L(H) defined by

⟨π(µ)ξ, η⟩=
�

G
⟨π(x)ξ, η⟩dµ(x),
ξ, η ∈H.

Then ∥π(µ)∥≤∥µ∥. Observe that π(µ∗∗µ) = π(µ)∗π(µ) is a positive self-
adjoint operator and that ∥π(µ∗∗µ)∥= ∥π(µ)∥2. (For a measure µ on G,
the measure µ∗is defined by dµ∗(x) = dµ(x−1).) In case µ is absolutely
continuous with respect to a Haar measure dx on G, that is, dµ(x) = f(x)dx
for some f ∈L1(G), we write π(f) instead of π(µ).
Asssume now that µ is a probablity measure on G, (that is, µ is posi-
tive with total mass µ(G) = 1). Then π(µ) is a linear contraction on H.
By a linear contraction on a normed vector space V, we mean a bounded
operator T : V →V with ∥T∥≤1. We show how weak containment of 1G
in π is related to the spectrum of the operator π(µ); compare [HaRoV–93,
Proposition 2]. We first need the following elementary lemma.

Lemma G.4.1 Let T be a linear contraction on a Hilbert space H. Then
1 belongs to the spectrum σ(T) if and only if 1 is an approximate eigen-
value of T (that is, there exists a sequence of unit vectors ξn in H such that
limn ∥Tξn −ξn∥= 0).

Proof
It is clear that if 1 is an approximate eigenvalue of T then 1 ∈σ(T).
Conversely, assume that 1 ∈σ(T). Two cases can occur. If the image of T −I
is dense in H, then 1 is an approximate eigenvalue of T. If the image of T −I
is not dense in H, then Ker(T ∗−I) ̸= 0, that is, T ∗has a non zero fixed
vector ξ. As T is a contraction, ξ is also a fixed vector of T. Indeed,

⟨Tξ, ξ⟩= ⟨ξ, T ∗ξ⟩= ∥ξ∥2,

and it follows from the equality case of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that
Tξ = ξ. Thus, 1 is an eigenvalue of T. ■

Remark G.4.3 (i) In the previous proposition, the assumption that µ is
absolutely continuous with respect to the Haar measure on G is necessary;
see Exercise G.6.6.
(ii) Let µ be as in the previous proposition. Denote by X the support of
the probability measure µ and by Y that of µ∗∗µ; denote by ⟨X⟩and ⟨Y ⟩
the subgroups of G generated by X and Y.
Then ⟨Y ⟩is a subgroup of ⟨X⟩. If e ∈X, then Y contains X and ⟨Y ⟩=
⟨X⟩. Otherwise, the inclusion ⟨Y ⟩⊂⟨X⟩can be strict: this is the case when
G = Z and µ = δ1, since µ∗∗µ = δ0.
(iii) With the notation of (ii), ⟨Y ⟩is not dense in G if and only if X is
contained in the left coset of a proper closed subgroup of G.
(iv) As the proof shows, the fact that (i) implies (ii) in the previous
proposition is true if µ is any probability measure on G.

The following characterisation of the amenability of a locally compact
group was given by Kesten (see [Kest–59a]) in the case of a countable group
G and a symmetric probability measure on G. The general case is due to
[DerGu–73], where the result is proved without the absolute continuity as-
sumption on the probability measure µ; see also [BeCh–74a].

Theorem G.4.4 (Kesten) Let G be a locally compact group, and let µ be a
probability measure on G. Assume that µ is absolutely continuous with respect
to the Haar measure on G and that its support generates a dense subgroup
in G. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) 1 belongs to the spectrum of the operator λG(µ);

(iii) the spectral radius r(λG(µ)) of λG(µ) is 1.

Proof
The equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from the previous proposition
together with Hulanicki-Reiter’s Theorem G.3.2.
Since r(λG(µ)) ≤∥λG(µ)∥≤1, it is obvious that (ii) implies (iii).

Remark G.4.7 The previous corollary admits the following probabilistic
interpretation.
Let Γ be a finitely generated group. Let S be a finite generating set of
G with S−1 = S. The Cayley graph G(Γ, S) of Γ with respect to S is the
graph where the vertices are the elements of Γ, and where x and y in Γ are
connected by an edge if x−1y ∈S. Consider the random walk on G(Γ, S) in
which every step consists of left multiplication by s ∈S with probability

G.5
Følner’s property

A useful refinement of Reiter’s Property (P1) of Theorem G.3.1 is Følner’s
Property. Recall that, for a measurable subset A of a locally compact group,
|A| denotes the measure of A with respect to a left invariant measure.

Theorem G.5.1 Let G be a locally compact group. The following properties
are equivalent:

(i) G is amenable;

(ii) (Følner’s Property) for every compact subset Q of G and every
ε > 0, there exists a Borel subset U of G with 0 < |U| < ∞such that

|xU△U|

|U|
≤ε,
for all
x ∈Q,

where △denotes the symmetric difference.

G.5. FOLNER’S PROPERTY
467

We will show that Følner’s Property is equivalent Reiter’s Property (P1).

for all x, y ∈G, so that

∥x−1fU −fU∥1 = |xU△U|

is proving the converse. We will need the following elementary lemma.

Remark G.5.3 Assume that G is compactly generated. It follows, by the
arguments given in the proof of Proposition F.1.7, that the amenability of G
is equivalent to Følner’s Property for some compact generating set Q of G,
that is, to the existence of a sequence of Borel subsets Un with 0 < |Un| < ∞
such that

lim
n sup
x∈Q
|xUn△Un|

|Un|
= 0.

Such a sequence (Un)n is called a Følner sequence.

Example G.5.4 Let Γ = Z with the set of generators S = {±1}. The
sequence of intervalls {−n, −(n −1), . . . , 0, . . . , n −1, n} is easily seen to be
a Følner sequence for Z.

We give an application of Følner’s Property to groups of subexponential
growth. Let Γ be a finitely generated group, and let S be a finite generating
subset of Γ with S−1 = S. The word metric on Γ with respect to S is the
metric dS on Γ associated to the Cayley graph G(Γ, S); see Remark G.4.7.
For each r > 0, let Br be the ball of radius r centered at e, that is, Br is the
set of γ ∈Γ which can be expressed as words of length ≤r in elements from
S. The limit
γS = lim
r→∞(#Br)1/r,

which is known to exist, is the growth of Γ (with respect to S). It is also
known that independently of S, we have

either
γS > 1
or
γS = 1.

470
APPENDIX G. AMENABILITY

In the first case, Γ is said to be of exponential growth and it is said to be of
subexponential growth in the second one. For instance, nilpotent groups are
of polynomial growth (that is, there exist a constant C and an integer n such
that #Br ≤Crn, for all r > 0) and hence of subexponential growth. For
more details, see [Harpe–00, Chapter VI].
The following result appeared for the first time in [AdVSr–57].

Corollary G.5.5 If Γ is a finitely generated group of subexponential growth,
then Γ is amenable. In particular, finitely generated groups of polynomial
growth are amenable.

Proof
Let S be a finite generating subset of Γ with S−1 = S. We claim
that a subsequence of the sequence of balls (Bn)n≥1 is a Følner sequence.
Indeed, assume, by contradiction, that this is not the case. Then there exists
ε > 0 and s ∈S such that #(sBn△Bn) > ε#Bn for infinitely many n. We
have
#(sBn \ Bn) > ε

2#Bn
or
#(Bn \ sBn) > ε

2#Bn.

Since sBn \ Bn and s−1(Bn \ sBn) are contained in Bn+1 \ Bn, it follows that
#Bn+1 ≥
�
1 + ε

2

�
#Bn. Hence, there exists a constant C > 0 such that

#Bn ≥C
�
1 + ε

2

�n

for infinitely many n’s. This contradicts the fact that Γ is of subexponential
growth. ■

Let Γ be a finitely generated group, and let S be a finite generating
subset of Γ with S−1 = S. The existence of a Følner sequence Fn in Γ has
the following interpretation in terms of the Cayley graph G(Γ, S).
Let G be a graph and a set F of vertices of G. The boundary ∂F of F is
the set of all vertices of G which are connected to some vertex in F but do
not belong to F.

Corollary G.5.6 Let Γ be a finitely generated group, and let S be a finite
generating subset of Γ with S−1 = S. The following properties are equivalent:

(i) Γ is amenable;
